On Model Theory of Second-Order Objects Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 101020762). J. Puljujärvi was partially supported by the Academy of Finland, grant 322795. D. E. Quadrellaro was partially supported by the Academy of Finland, grant 336283.
Tapani Hyttinen, Joni Puljujärvi, Davide Emilio Quadrellaro

Abstract
. Motivated by team semantics and existential second-order logic, we develop a model-theoretic framework for studying second-order objects such as sets and relations. We introduce a notion of abstract elementary team categories that generalizes the standard notion of abstract elementary class, and show that it is an example of an accessible category. We apply our framework to show that the logic 𝖥𝖮𝖳 \mathsf{FOT} introduced by Kontinen and Yang [ 17 ] satisfies a version of Lindström’s Theorem. Finally, we consider the problem of transferring categoricity between different cardinalities for complete theories in existential second-order logic (or independence logic) and prove both a downwards and an upwards categoricity transfer result.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
03C45, 03C48, 03C85

1.
Introduction

Model theory is the study of general properties of mathematical structures. In traditional model theory, the center of attention—we could say the smallest unit of interest—are elements, or tuples of elements. One is often interested in whether certain types of elements or tuples exist, or how many different types there might be, or how elements get mapped when one moves from one structure to another via some mapping that preserves certain properties of structures.

On the other hand, often in mathematics one is interested in sets of elements, or sets of tuples (i.e. relations). While elements are first-order objects, sets and relations are second-order objects. Not much model theory has been done in a setting where the basic building block is, instead of an element, a set. The reason may be that second-order logic—the natural counterpart of first-order logic in this setting—is so strong that it lacks many properties that make elementary model theory interesting. For example, the second-order theory of many interesting structures—such as the ordered field of real numbers, the field of complex numbers, or the semiring of natural numbers—are categorical, i.e. they have a unique model up to isomorphism. However, it may not be completely hopeless to study relations using simply first-order logic, or some other logic that lies between first and second order.

Our interest in the model theory of relations comes from the field of
team semantics
. Team semantics is an extension of the usual Tarski semantics of first-order logic that allows formulas to be evaluated over sets of assignments, called
teams
, rather than single assignments. Such a framework was originally introduced by
[
11
]
in order to provide a compositional semantics to Hintikka and Sandu’s IF-logic. In particular,
[
33
]
and
[
31
]
used team semantics to extend first-order logic by means of the so-called
dependence atom

\dep(x_{1}\dots x_{n},y),

which we read as saying “the value of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
completely determines the value of
y
”. The resulting logic, namely
dependence logic
, dramatically increases the expressive power of first-order logic and turns out to be equivalent both to IF-logic and to the existential fragment of second-order logic
\mathsf{ESO}
(cp.
Lemma

2.5
).

Additionally, team semantics proved soon to be a very powerful and flexible framework, and it was noticed by Väänänen and others that it allows to consider several atoms characterizing various notions of dependence, e.g. the
independence atom

x\perp y

[
10
]
or the
inclusion atom

x\subseteq y

[
9
]
. Importantly, each of the logics that one obtains by extending the syntax of first-order logic by these new atomic formulas correspond to different fragments of existential second order logic
\mathsf{ESO}
(with usual semantics) via a translation that goes both ways. More recently, however,
[
17
]
have also introduced a logic over team semantics that corresponds to first-order logic in this way, i.e. it captures exactly the elementary properties of teams. From the perspective of the present work, the results from
[
17
]
represent a significant breakthrough. In our view, Kontinen and Yang’s work shows that the the most essential aspect of team semantics lies in this shift of attention from first-order to second-order objects, rather than the increase in expressive power.

In fact, since teams are essentially relations, it is very natural to adopt team semantics as a framework to study the model theory of sets and relations. Interestingly enough, there has not been much study of the model theory of logics in team semantics, with the exception of the seminal work of
[
31
]
and the study of team ultraproducts by
[
21
]
. In
[
24
]
, the study of model theory for team semantics was initated, in particular by proving a full version of the compactness theorem for independence logic and several of its fragments.

In this article we continue the work from
[
24
]
and develop a model-theoretic framework for team semantics. We further believe this framework provides the natural environment to study the elementary and higher-order properties of sets and relations. In particular, we apply the model-theoretic toolkit developed in this article to study the problem of transferring categoricity for complete theories in existential second-order logic. This provides an important example of how team semantics can be used to deliver results on the model theory of existential second order logic.

We shall now summarize the structure and the major contributions of this work. In
Section

2
we review some basic notions from team semantics and existential second-order logic. In
Section

3
we introduce the notion of team maps between structures and prove some of their basic properties. In
Section

4
, we discus problems arising from trying to fit a class of structures together with elementary team embeddings into the framework of abstract elementary classes (AECs). We resolve these problems by defining a generalization of AECs that makes more sense in our setting. The objects of these so-called abstract elementary team classes (AETCs) are reminiscent of general models (or Henkin models) of second-order logic. The main result of the section is
Theorem

4.18
, which states that AETCs, when treated as categories with team maps as morphisms, are examples of accessible categories
[
1
]
, which are known to generalize AECs. In
Section

5
we use the general framework of abstract elementary classes to show that one can build a suitable version of the monster model for independence logic and
\mathsf{ESO}
.

We then consider two applications of this model-theoretic toolkit. First, in
Section

6
, we use the machinery introduced in the previous sections to show that the elementary team logic
\mathsf{FOT}
introduced by Kontinen and Yang in
[
17
]
satisfies a version of Lindström’s Theorem. Finally, we conclude the article in
Section

7
by focusing on the problem of classifying models of complete existential second-order theories. We prove, in particular, two versions of categoricity transfer: one from uncountable to countable cardinals (
Theorem

7.7
) and one from countable to uncountable cardinals (
Theorem

7.11
). These two results also show how the spectrum function of an existential second-order theory depends in many cases on the stability properties of its first-order reduct, and it also displays that the present work has many potential connections to the standard elementary model theory.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notational Conventions

We denote sets and relations with uppercase Roman letters such as
A
,
B
and
C
and elements by lowercase Roman letters such as
a
,
b
and
c
. We usually denote collections of sets
A
by calligraphic letters such as
\mathcal{A}
. By Fraktur letters such as
\mathfrak{A}
,
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}
we denote first-order structures of a given signature.

If
n
is a natural number, an
n
-tuple is technically a function with domain
n
. We usually denote tuples by
\vec{a}
,
\vec{b}
etc. and often write
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
, where
a_{i}=\vec{a}(i)
for
i<n
. We call
n
the length of
\vec{a}
and may denote it by
|\vec{a}|
. The empty set is the unique
0
-tuple. An
n
-ary relation is a set of
n
-tuples. If
A
is an
n
-ary relation, we denote by
\ar(A)
the arity of
A
, i.e. the number
n
. The empty set is considered a relation of every arity.
\{\emptyset\}
is the unique nonempty
0
-ary relation. If
f
is a function from
A^{n}
to
A
for some
n<\omega
, then we say that it has arity
n
and denote this number by
\ar(f)
.

If
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
is an
n
-tuple and
\vec{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{m-1})
is an
m
-tuple, the concatenation of
\vec{a}
and
\vec{b}
is the
n+m
-tuple
(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1},b_{0},\dots,b_{m-1})
, which we usually denote just by
\vec{a}\vec{b}
. If there is a risk of confusion, we may also denote the concatenation by
\vec{a}^{\frown}\vec{b}
. Often for our convenience, we also identify a pair
(\vec{a},\vec{b})
with the tuple
\vec{a}\vec{b}
. Note that
\emptyset\vec{a}=\vec{a}\emptyset=\vec{a}
for any tuple
\vec{a}
. If
A
is an
n
-ary and
B
an
m
-ary relation, then we identify the Cartesian product
A\times B
with the
n+m
-ary relation

\{\vec{a}\vec{b}\mid\text{$\vec{a}\in A$ and $\vec{b}\in B$}\}.

Note that by this convention,
\{\emptyset\}\times A=A\times\{\emptyset\}=A
. Also,
\emptyset\times A=A\times\emptyset=\emptyset
.

If
A
is an
n+k
-ary and
B
a
k+m
-ary relation, then the natural
k
-join
A\bowtie_{k}B
of
A
and
B
is the
n+k+m
-ary relation

\{\vec{a}\vec{b}\vec{c}\mid\text{$|\vec{a}|=n$, $|\vec{b}|=k$, $|\vec{c}|=m$ and $\vec{a}\vec{b}\in A$ and $\vec{b}\vec{c}\in B$}\}.

Note that
A\times B=A\bowtie_{0}B
. If
A
and
B
are
n
-ary relations, then
A\cap B=A\bowtie_{n}B
.

Let
A
be an
n
-ary relation, let
i_{0},\dots,i_{m-1}<n
and denote
\vec{\imath}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{m-1})
. We then denote by
\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(A)
the
m
-ary relation

\{(a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{m-1}})\mid\text{there is $(b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1})\in A$ with $b_{i_{j}}=a_{i_{j}}$ for $j<m$}\}.

This is a kind of projection, with the possibility of permuting and repeating coordinates. If
\pi
is a permutation of
n
, then

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi(0),\dots,\pi(n-1))}(A)

\displaystyle=\{(a_{\pi(0)},\dots,a_{\pi(n-1)})\mid(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in A\}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\mid(a_{\pi^{-1}(0)},\dots,a_{\pi^{-1}(n-1)})\in A\}.

If
\alpha
is a possibly infinite ordinal and
a
is a function with domain
\alpha
, we call
a
an
\alpha
-sequence and write
a=(a_{i})_{i<\alpha}
, where
a(i)=a_{i}
, similar to tuples, even though when
\alpha
is infinite, we usually do not call
a
an
\alpha
-tuple, nor do we call a set of
\alpha
-sequences an
\alpha
-ary relation.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is a structure, we do not distinguish between
\mathfrak{A}
and the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
when there is no risk of confusion, i.e. we write e.g.
a\in\mathfrak{A}
when
a
is an element of the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
and we write
|\mathfrak{A}|
for the cardinality of the domain. We also denote the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
by
\dom(\mathfrak{A})
when necessary for clarity.

If
\tau
is a signature and
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are elementarily equivalent
\tau
-structures, i.e. satisfy the same
\tau
-sentences of first-order logic, then we write
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
. If
L
is another logic and
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
satisfy the same
\tau
-sentences of
L
, we write
\mathfrak{A}\equiv_{L}\mathfrak{B}
.

We denote the powerset of a set
I
by
\mathcal{P}(I)
. By
\mathcal{P}^{+}(I)
we mean
\mathcal{P}(I)\setminus\{\emptyset\}
. By
\mathcal{R}(I)
we mean the collection of all relations on
I
, i.e. the set
\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathcal{P}(I^{n})
.

2.2.
Existential Second-Order Logic

We assume the reader is familiar with the syntax and semantics of second-order logic (
\mathsf{SO}
).
We refer the reader to the last chapter of
[
6
]
and to
[
4
]
and
[
32
]
for an overview of basic definitions and results.

If
\phi\in\mathsf{SO}
does not contain any second-order quantifiers, we say that
\phi
is first-order, even if it has free second-order variables. If no first-order variable occurs free in
\phi
and
\phi
is first-order, we say that
\phi
is a first-order sentence; with this terminology, a first-order sentence (of
\mathsf{SO}
) may contain free second-order variables. If
\phi
does not contain free (first- or second-order) variables, we say that
\phi
is a (second-order) sentence.

Fact
.

Every formula of
\mathsf{SO}
is equivalent to a formula of the form

Q_{0}X_{0}\dots Q_{m-1}X_{m-1}\phi,

where
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
for
i<m
, each
X_{i}
is either a relation or function variable and
\phi
is first-order.

We denote by
\mathsf{ESO}
the existential fragment of
\mathsf{SO}
, i.e. the set of formulas of
\mathsf{SO}
that are equivalent to a formula of the form

\exists X_{0}\dots\exists X_{m-1}\phi

for first-order
\phi
.

The following properties of
\mathsf{ESO}
are well known.

Lemma 2.1
.

(i)

Let
X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1}
be second-order variables. Then there is a relation variable
X
such that for any first-order
\tau
-sentence
\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})
, there is a first-order
\tau
-sentence
\phi^{*}(X)
such that

\exists X_{0}\dots\exists X_{n-1}\phi\equiv\exists X\phi^{*}.

(ii)

\mathsf{ESO}
is closed under conjunction, disjunction and first-order quantifiers.

It is easy to show that
\mathsf{ESO}
satisfies the compactness theorem and the following version of the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem: if
T
is an existential second-order theory with infinite models, then for any infinite
\kappa\geq|\tau|
,
T
has a model of cardinality
\kappa
. To see this, let
T
be an
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory with infinite models, and let
\{\exists X_{i}\alpha_{i}(X_{i})\mid i<|\tau|+\aleph_{0}\}
, for
\alpha_{i}
first-order, axiomatize
T
. Fix an infinite cardinal
\kappa\geq|\tau|
, and let
\mathfrak{A}
be an infinite model of
T
. Then there are relations
R_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{\ar(X_{i})}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\alpha_{i}(R_{i})
for all
i<|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
. Let
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
be an expansion of
\mathfrak{A}
by fresh predicate symbols
S_{i}
with
\ar(S_{i})=\ar(R_{i})
such that
S_{i}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}}=R_{i}
. Then
\Th_{\mathsf{FO}}(\hat{\mathfrak{A}})
has, by the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem of first-order logic, a model
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of cardinality
\kappa
. Then, letting
\mathfrak{B}=\hat{\mathfrak{B}}{\upharpoonright}\tau
, we have
\mathfrak{B}\models\exists X_{i}\alpha_{i}(X_{i})
for every
i<|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
, as witnessed by
S_{i}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}
. We stress that this does not contradict Lindström’s theorem, as
\mathsf{ESO}
is not closed under negation, which is a requirement imposed on an abstract logic in the statement of the theorem.

We wish to define the concept of completeness for theories in
\mathsf{ESO}
. Usually, one would define a complete theory to be one that semantically (or syntactically if there is a proof system available) entails, for every sentence
\phi
, either
\phi
or
\neg\phi
. Since
\mathsf{ESO}
is a positive logic, this option is not viable. We can, however, give a definition that does not use negation and is equivalent for logics that are closed under negation.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
T
be an existential second-order theory, i.e. a set of sentences of
\mathsf{ESO}
. We say that
T
is
complete
if all of its models are
\mathsf{ESO}
-equivalent, i.e. for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\models T
and sentences
\phi
of
\mathsf{ESO}
, we have

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi.

It may well be that the set of
\mathsf{ESO}
-sentences true in a structure
\mathfrak{A}
, which we denote by
\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
, is not complete in the above sense. For instance, if
\mathfrak{A}
is a
\{P\}
-structure, where
P
is a unary predicate, such that
|P^{\mathfrak{A}}|=\aleph_{1}
and
|\mathfrak{A}\setminus P^{\mathfrak{A}}|=\aleph_{0}
, then
\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
does not contain the sentence expressing that there is a bijection between
P^{\mathfrak{A}}
and its complement, but by the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem
\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
has a countable model, and such a model will satisfy said sentence. It turns out that a complete first-order theory has a unique
\mathsf{ESO}
-completion, consisting of every
\mathsf{ESO}
-sentence that is consistent with the first-order theory. We will discuss this in
Section

7.1
. We point out the following corollary on complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theories, which immediately follows by the previous Löwenheim–Skolem theorem.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
T
be a complete
\tau
-theory in
\mathsf{ESO}
, with infinite models. Then
T
has infinite models in all cardinalities
\geq|\tau|
.

We conclude this section by making the following observation, akin to the Łos–Vaught test of first-order logic.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
T
be an existential second-order theory. If
T
does not have models of size
<|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
and is categorical in every cardinality in which it has a model, then
T
is complete.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\models T
and let
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
. Since
|\mathfrak{A}|,|\mathfrak{B}|\geq|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
, by Löwenheim–Skolem, there is
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
with
|\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}|=|\mathfrak{B}|
and
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\models T\cup\{\phi\}
. As
T
is
|\mathfrak{B}|
-categorical,
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{B}
, and hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
.
∎

2.3.
Team Semantics

Next we recall the concept of a team and the team-semantic interpretations of first-order connectives and quantifiers. We refer the reader to
[
31
,
9
,
10
]
for an introduction to team semantics and proofs of the main facts recalled here.

Let
\tau
be a vocabulary,
\mathfrak{A}
a
\tau
-structure and
D\subseteq\{v_{i}\mid i<\omega\}
a set of (first-order) variables. An
assingment
of
\mathfrak{A}
with domain
D
is a function
D\to\mathfrak{A}
. A team of
\mathfrak{A}
with domain
D
is a set of assignments of
\mathfrak{A}
with domain
D
, i.e. a subset of
\mathfrak{A}^{D}
. We usually take
D
to be finite, but sometimes teams with infinite domains may be of interest. When given a team
X
, we denote its domain by
\dom(X)
, similarly to domains of functions.

If
s
is an assignment of
\mathfrak{A}
with
x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\in\dom(s)
and
t(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
is a
\tau
-term, we write
s(t)
as a shorthand for
t^{\mathfrak{A}}(s(x_{0}),\dots,s(x_{n-1}))
. If
\vec{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
is a tuple of variables, we write
s(\vec{x})
for
(s(x_{0}),\dots,s(x_{n-1}))
. If
X
is a team and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\in\dom(X)
, then we denote by
X[\vec{x}]
the relation

\{s(\vec{x})\mid s\in X\}.

Conversely, if
R
is an
n
-ary relation, we denote by
\team(R)
the team

\{\{(v_{i},a_{i})\mid i<n\}\mid(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in R\}

with domain
\{v_{i}\mid i<n\}
.

If
s
is an assignment of
\mathfrak{A}
,
a\in\mathfrak{A}
and
x
is a variable, we denote by
s(a/x)
the assignment
s^{\prime}
with domain
\dom(s)\cup\{x\}
such that

s^{\prime}(y)=\begin{cases}a&\text{if $y=x$},\\
s(y)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
X
is a team of
\mathfrak{A}
, then by
X(a/x)
we denote the team
Y
of
\mathfrak{A}
with domain
D\coloneqq\dom(X)\cup\{x\}
such that

Y=\{s(a/x)\mid s\in X\}.

If
F
is a function
X\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathfrak{A})
, we denote by
X(F/x)
the team

\{s(a/x)\mid\text{$s\in X$ and $a\in F(s)$}\}.

We call
F
a supplement function and
X(F/x)
the
supplementation
of
X
by
F
. If
F(s)=\mathfrak{A}
for all
s\in X
, then we denote the supplemented team by
X(\mathfrak{A}/x)
and call it the
duplication
of
X
. If
D\subseteq\dom(X)
, we denote by
X{\upharpoonright}D
the team

\{s{\upharpoonright}D\mid s\in X\}.

Given a vocabulary
\tau
, we consider the syntax of
\tau
-formulas of first-order logic
\mathsf{FO}
to be given by the grammar

\phi\Coloneqq t=t^{\prime}\mid\neg t=t^{\prime}\mid R(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})\mid\neg R(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})\mid\phi\land\phi\mid\phi\vee\phi\mid\exists x\phi\mid\forall x\phi,

where
t
,
t^{\prime}
and
t_{i}
are
\tau
-terms and
R\in\tau
is an
n
-ary relation symbol. Note that we readily assume a negation normal form for our first-order formulas. The team semantics of the operators above is the following.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
X
a team of
\mathfrak{A}
with
x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\in\dom(X)
. For a first-order formula
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
,
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
is defined as follows.

(i)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}t=t^{\prime}
if
s(t)=s(t^{\prime})
for all
s\in X
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\neg t=t^{\prime}
if
s(t)\neq s(t^{\prime})
for all
s\in X
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}R(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})
if
(s(t_{0}),\dots,s(t_{n-1})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
for all
s\in X
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\neg R(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})
if
(s(t_{0}),\dots,s(t_{n-1})\notin R^{\mathfrak{A}}
for all
s\in X
.

(v)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi\land\chi
if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi
and
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\chi
.

(vi)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi\vee\chi
if there are
Y,Z\subseteq X
such that
Y\cup Z=X
,
\mathfrak{A}\models_{Y}\psi
and
\mathfrak{A}\models_{Z}\chi
.

(vii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\exists x\psi
if there is a function
F\colon X\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X(F/x)}\psi
.

(viii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\forall x\psi
if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X(\mathfrak{A}/x)}\psi
.

We stress that the team semantic definition of the classical connectives and quantifiers do not provide us with anything new when it comes to just first-order logic, in the sense that formulas of first-order logic are
flat
.

Fact

(Flatness)
.

For any
\mathfrak{A}
,
X
and
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\in\mathsf{FO}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\text{$\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(s(x_{0}),\dots,s(x_{n-1}))$ for all $s\in X$}.

Hence for formulas of
\mathsf{FO}
, team semantics is equivalent to the usual Tarski semantics.

2.4.
Dependence Logic

When working with team semantics, we are generally interested in expanding the syntax of first-order logic by means of new atomic formulas encoding different relations of (in)dependence between variables. We recall here the semantics of the
dependence atom

\dep(\vec{x};\vec{y})
, the
independence atom

\vec{x}\perp_{\vec{z}}\vec{y}
, the
inclusion atom

\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}
and the
exclusion atom

\vec{x}\mid\vec{y}
.

(ix)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\dep(\vec{x};\vec{y})
if for all
s,s^{\prime}\in X
, if
s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{x})
then
s(\vec{y})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})
, i.e. there is a function
f\colon\mathfrak{A}^{|\vec{x}|}\to\mathfrak{A}^{|\vec{y}|}
such that
f(s(\vec{x}))=s(\vec{y})
for all
s\in X
.

(x)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}
, where
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
are tuples of the same length, if for all
s\in X
there is some
s^{\prime}\in X
such that
s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})
, i.e.
X[\vec{x}]\subseteq X[\vec{y}]
.

(xi)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\vec{x}\mid\vec{y}
, where
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
are tuples of the same length, if for all
s,s^{\prime}\in X
we have
s(\vec{x})\neq s^{\prime}(\vec{y})
, i.e.
X[\vec{x}]\cap X[\vec{y}]=\emptyset
.

(xii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\vec{x}\perp_{\vec{z}}\vec{y}
if for all
s,s^{\prime}\in X
such that
s(\vec{z})=s^{\prime}(\vec{z})
, there is
s^{\prime\prime}\in X
such that
s^{\prime\prime}(\vec{x}\vec{z})=s(\vec{x}\vec{z})
and
s^{\prime\prime}(\vec{y})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})
, i.e.
X[\vec{x}\vec{z}\vec{y}]=X[\vec{x}\vec{z}]\bowtie_{|\vec{z}|}X[\vec{z}\vec{y}]
.

As a special case of the dependence atom, we have the
constancy atom

\dep(\emptyset;\vec{x})
that is true in a team
X
if for all
s,s^{\prime}\in X
,
s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{x})
, i.e.
|X[\vec{x}]|\leq 1
. We denote the constancy atom simply by
\dep(\vec{x})
.

Note that adding any of the aforementioned atoms to the syntax of first-order logic results in a non-flat logic. If
C\subseteq\{\dep(\dots),\perp_{c},\subseteq,\mid\}
, we denote by
\mathsf{FO}(C)
the logic resulting from adding the atoms in
C
to the syntax of
\mathsf{FO}
. Below we list some well-known properties of these logics.

Fact
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
X
and
Y
teams of
\mathfrak{A}
with the same domain.

(i)

Locality
: For any formula
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\dep(\dots),\perp_{c},\subseteq,|)
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
if and only if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X{\upharpoonright}\Fv(\phi)}\phi
.

(ii)

The empty team property
: For any formula
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\dep(\dots),\perp_{c},\subseteq,|)
we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{\emptyset}\phi
.

(iii)

Downwards closure
: For any formula
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\dep(\dots),|)
, if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
and
Y\subseteq X
, then
\mathfrak{A}\models_{Y}\phi
.

(iv)

Union-closure
: For any formula
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\subseteq)
, if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
and
\mathfrak{A}\models_{Y}\phi
, then
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X\cup Y}\phi
.

By locality, whenever
\phi
is a sentence of
\mathsf{FO}(\dep(\dots),\perp_{c},\subseteq,|)
, we have

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X{\upharpoonright}\emptyset}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{\{\emptyset\}}\phi

for any
\mathfrak{A}
and
X
. We write
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
for
\mathfrak{A}\models_{\{\emptyset\}}\phi
whenever
\phi
is a sentence.

It is well known that in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
one can define all the other aforementioned atoms, i.e. the logics
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and
\mathsf{FO}(\dep(\dots),\perp_{c},\subseteq,\mid)
are equiexpressive. In fact, any property of teams definable in
\mathsf{ESO}
(modulo the empty team property) can be defined in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and, conversely, any formula of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
can be defined in
\mathsf{ESO}
, in the following sense.

Lemma 2.5

(Galliani, Grädel–Väänänen
[
10
,
9
]
)
.

Fix a finite set
D=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
of variables.

(i)

For every
\tau
-formula of
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\chi(R)
of
\mathsf{ESO}
with no free first-order variables and only one free
n
-ary second-order variable
R
, such that for every
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
with
D\subseteq\dom(X)
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\chi(X[x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}]).

We call
\chi(R)
a
translation
of
\phi
to
\mathsf{ESO}
.

(ii)

For every
\tau
-formula
\chi(R)
of
\mathsf{ESO}
with no free first-order variables and only one free
n
-ary second-order variable
R
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
such that for every
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
with
D\subseteq\dom(X)
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\chi^{+}(X[x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}]),

where
\chi^{+}(R)=\exists x_{0}\dots\exists x_{n-1}R(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\to\chi(R)
. We call
\phi
a
translation
of
\chi(R)
to
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
.

Finally, we define what it means for a theory in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
to be complete.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
T
be an
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
-theory.

(i)

We say that
T
is
first-order complete
if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\models T
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
.

(ii)

We say that
T
is
complete
if its
\mathsf{ESO}
-translation is, i.e. if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\models T
and sentences
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi.

2.5.
The Logic
\mathsf{FOT}

First-order Team Logic,
\mathsf{FOT}
, was introduced by
[
17
]
to capture exactly the team properties definable in first-order logic (modulo the empty team property) the same way, i.e.
\mathsf{FOT}
corresponds to
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
exactly the same way in which
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
corresponds to
\mathsf{ESO}
. We can view
\mathsf{FOT}
here as a small extension of first order logic whose expressive power lies between
\mathsf{FO}
and
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
. In particular, since
\mathsf{FOT}
captures all elementary team properties, it is powerful enough to express the most common dependence atoms, for instance those defined in the previous section.

Given a vocabulary
\tau
, the syntax of
\tau
-formulas of
\mathsf{FOT}
is given by the grammar

\phi\Coloneqq\lambda\mid\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}\mid\dep(\vec{x})\mid\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\mid\phi\land\phi\mid\phi\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\phi\mid\exists^{1}x\phi\mid\forall^{1}x\phi,

where
\lambda
is a first-order atomic formula and the
weak classical negation

\mathord{\dot{\sim}}
, the
weak disjunction

\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}
, and the
weak quantifiers

\exists^{1}
and
\forall^{1}
are interpreted as follows:

(iii)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi
if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
or
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
if
X=\emptyset
or
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\phi
.

(v)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\exists^{1}x\psi
if there is an element
a\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X(a/x)}\psi
.

(vi)

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\forall^{1}x\psi
if for every element
a\in\mathfrak{A}
we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X(a/x)}\psi
.

In
[
17
]
, the syntax of
\mathsf{FOT}
excludes the constancy atom
\dep(\vec{x})
. However, our definition results in an equiexpressive logic, as the constancy atom can be defined via the equivalence

\dep(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\equiv\exists^{1}y_{0}\dots\exists^{1}y_{n-1}\bigwedge_{i<n}x_{i}=y_{i}.

Note that as quantifiers seem to be required here, at the level of quantifier-free formulas there may be a difference in expressive power between the two versions of
\mathsf{FOT}
.

We denote by
\phi\rightarrowtriangle\psi
the formula
\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi
and call it the
weak classical implication
and by
\phi\leftrightarrowtriangle\psi
the formula
(\phi\rightarrowtriangle\psi)\land(\psi\rightarrowtriangle\phi)
and call it the
weak classical equivalence
. Clearly, for a nonempty team
X
,
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\rightarrowtriangle\psi
if and only if

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\implies\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi.

We also denote
\top\coloneqq\dep(\emptyset)
and
\bot\coloneqq\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\top
. Note that the empty constancy atom
\top
is true in any team, and hence its weak classical negation
\bot
is true only in the empty team.

The following is the
\mathsf{FOT}
-counterpart of
Lemma

2.5
.

Lemma 2.7

(Kontinen–Yang
[
17
]
)
.

Fix a finite set
D=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
of variables.

(i)

For every
\tau
-formula
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
, there is a first-order
\tau
-sentence
\chi(R)
with a single free
n
-ary second order variable
R
such that for every
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
with
D\subseteq\dom(X)
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\chi(X[x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}]).

We call
\chi(R)
a
translation
of
\phi
to
\mathsf{FO}
.

(ii)

For every first-order
\tau
-sentence
\chi(R)
with a single free
n
-ary second order variable
R
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
such that for every
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
with
D\subseteq\dom(X)
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\chi^{+}(X[x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}]),

where
\chi^{+}(R)=\exists x_{0}\dots\exists x_{n-1}R(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\to\chi(R)
. We call
\phi
a translation of
\chi(R)
to
\mathsf{FOT}
.

We can conclude that the relationships of the logics mentioned so far when it comes to expressive power is the following:

\mathsf{FO}\lneq\mathsf{FOT}\lneq\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c}).

Next we prove some basic but useful facts about
\mathsf{FOT}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
X
be a team of
\mathfrak{A}
and
Y
a team of
\mathfrak{B}
, both with domain
\{v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}\}
. Then

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{Y}\phi

for all formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
if and only if

(\mathfrak{A},X[v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}])\equiv(\mathfrak{B},Y[v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}]).

Proof.

Denote
\vec{x}=(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
. If
X=Y=\emptyset
, then clearly the claim holds. If
X=\emptyset
and
Y\neq\emptyset
(or vice versa), then we have that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\bot
but
\mathfrak{B}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{Y}\bot
. On the other hand, clearly
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])=(\mathfrak{A},\emptyset)\not\equiv(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])
, proving our claim. So we may assume that
X\neq\emptyset\neq Y
.

“
\Longrightarrow
”: Suppose
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])\not\equiv(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])
. Then there is a first-order
\tau\cup\{R\}
-sentence
\chi
such that
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])\models\chi
and
(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\chi
. Since
X\neq\emptyset\neq Y
, we have
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])\models\chi^{+}
and
(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\chi^{+}
. Considering
R
a second-order variable and letting
\phi(\vec{x})
be a translation of
\chi(R)
to
\mathsf{FOT}
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
but
\mathfrak{B}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{Y}\phi
.

“
\Longleftarrow
”: If
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])\equiv(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])
and
\phi(\vec{x})
is a formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
, let
\chi(R)
be the translation of
\phi
to
\mathsf{FO}
. Consider
R
as a relation symbol so that
(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])
and
(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])
are
\tau\cup\{R\}
-structures. Then as
X
and
Y
are nonempty, we have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{A},X[\vec{x}])\models\chi

\displaystyle\iff(\mathfrak{B},Y[\vec{x}])\models\chi,

whence by
Lemma

2.7
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{Y}\phi.\qed

Lemma 2.9
.

For any
n
-ary relation symbol
R
and an
n
-tuple
\vec{x}
of variables, there is an
\{R\}
-formula
\theta(R,\vec{x})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
such that for any
\{R\}
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and a
nonempty
team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\theta\iff X[\vec{x}]=R^{\mathfrak{A}}.

Proof.

Clearly
\theta=R(\vec{x})\land\forall^{1}y_{0}\dots\forall^{1}y_{n-1}(R(\vec{y})\rightarrowtriangle\vec{y}\subseteq\vec{x})
suffices.
∎

2.6.
Ultraproducts

Recall that given an index set
I
, an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
I
and nonempty sets
A_{i}
,
i\in I
, the ultraproduct
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}/\mathcal{U}
of the sets
A_{i}
is the quotient set
\{f/\mathcal{U}\mid f\in\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\}
of
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
by the equivalence relation

f\equiv_{\mathcal{U}}g\iff\{i\in I\mid f(i)=g(i)\}\in\mathcal{U},

where
f/\mathcal{U}
denotes the equivalence class of
f
. If even one of the sets
A_{i}
is empty, then the Cartesian product will also be empty and hence so will the ultraproduct. However, when one defines the ultraproduct
\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}
of
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i\in I
, the interpretation of a predicate symbol
R\in\tau
will be the set

\{(f_{0}/\mathcal{U},\dots,f_{n-1}/\mathcal{U})\mid\{i\in I\mid(f_{0}(i),\dots,f_{n-1}(i))\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}\}\in\mathcal{U}\}.

This set, which we naturally think as the ultraproduct of the sets
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
, is empty only when
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
is empty for
\mathcal{U}
-many indices
i
. Given a context of an ultraproduct structure
\mathfrak{A}=\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}
, we generalize the notion of an ultraproduct of sets to correspond to the interpretation of relation symbols, i.e. if
A_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{n}
for all
i\in I
, then we denote by
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}/\mathcal{U}
the set

\{(f_{0}/\mathcal{U},\dots,f_{n-1}/\mathcal{U})\in\mathfrak{A}^{n}\mid\{i\in I\mid(f_{0}(i),\dots,f_{n-1}(i))\in A_{i}\}\in\mathcal{U}\}.

This leads to the definition of the ultraproduct of teams.

Definition 2.10

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be the ultraproduct of the structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i\in I
, and let
D
be a set of first-order variables.

(i)

Given assignments
s_{i}\colon D\to\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i\in I
, we denote by
(s_{i})_{i\in I}
the assignment
s\colon D\to\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
such that
s(x)=(s_{i}(x))_{i\in I}
for all
x\in D
.

(ii)

Given an assignment
s\colon D\to\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, we denote by
s/\mathcal{U}
the assignment
t\colon D\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
t(x)=s(x)/\mathcal{U}
for all
x\in D
.

(iii)

Given a team
X_{i}
of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with domain
D
for each
i\in I
, we define their
team ultraproduct

\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}
as the set of all assignments
s\colon D\to\mathfrak{A}
such that there are
s_{i}\colon D\to\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i\in I
, with
s=(s_{i})_{i\in I}/\mathcal{U}
and
\{i\in I\mid s_{i}\in X_{i}\}\in\mathcal{U}
.

Note that with these definitions,
\left(\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}\right)[\vec{x}]=\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}[\vec{x}]/\mathcal{U}
for any variable tuple
\vec{x}
.

Recall that the classical fundamental theorem of ultraproducts, also known as Łoś’ theorem, is the following: if
f_{0},\dots,f_{n-1}\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
is a first-order formula, then

\{i\in I\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\phi(f_{0}(i),\dots,f_{n-1}(i))\}\in\mathcal{U}\iff\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\models\phi(f_{0}/\mathcal{U},\dots,f_{n-1}/\mathcal{U}).

It was proved by
[
21
]
that first-order definable properties of teams are preserved in ultraproducts, which together with
Lemma

2.7
gives the following theorem.

Theorem 2.11

(Łoś’ Theorem of
\mathsf{FOT}
)
.

If
X_{i}
is a team of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with domain
D
for all
i\in I
and
\phi
is a formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
with
\Fv(\phi)\subseteq D
, then

\{i\in I\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X_{i}}\phi\}\in\mathcal{U}\iff\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}}\phi.

A version for
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
was proved by
[
24
]
:

Theorem 2.12

(Łoś’ Theorem of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
)
.

If
X_{i}
is a team of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with domain
D
for all
i\in I
and
\phi
is a formula of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
with
\Fv(\phi)\subseteq D
, then

\{i\in I\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X_{i}}\phi\}\in\mathcal{U}\implies\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}}\phi.

Note that in independence logic one does not obtain equivalence between a formula being satisfied
\mathcal{U}
-often and it being satisfied in the ultraproduct; this reflects the positive nature of
\mathsf{ESO}
. In fact, a universal second-order sentence that is preserved under ultraproducts is actually definable in first-order logic
[
14
]
. Furthermore, a logic that satisfies the stronger form of Łoś’ theorem is equiexpressive to first-order
[
26
]
. Fortunately, the missing direction of the equivalence is unnecessary for proving the compactness theorem, which we obtain as a corollary. We say that a set
\Gamma
of formulas of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
is satisfiable if there is a structure
\mathfrak{A}
, and a
nonempty
team
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
whose domain contains all the free variables of
\Gamma
, such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Gamma
.

Corollary 2.13
.

If
\Gamma
is a set of formulas of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and each finite
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
is satisfiable, then
\Gamma
is satisfiable.

In the sequel, the following classic result, usually dubbed the Keisler–Shelah theorem, will be of much use.

Theorem 2.14

(
[
14
]
,
[
29
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
\tau
-structures. If
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
, then there is a cardinal
\kappa
and an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\cong\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
.

We conclude this section by reviewing the notion of a limit ultrapower, which was introduced by
[
15
]
as a kind of generalization of ultrapowers.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
I
be a set,
\mathcal{F}
an ultrafilter on
I
and
\mathcal{G}
a filter on
I^{2}
.

(i)

For
f\in\mathfrak{A}^{I}
, we denote by
\eq(f)
the set of all pairs
(i,j)\in I^{2}
such that
f(i)=f(j)
.

(ii)

Let
A
be a set and denote
B=A^{I}/\mathcal{F}
. By
B|\mathcal{G}
we denote the set

\{f/\mathcal{F}\in B\mid\text{$\eq(g)\in\mathcal{G}$ for some $g\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}f$}\}.

(iii)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and let
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{A}^{I}/\mathcal{F}
. Then we denote by
\mathfrak{B}|\mathcal{G}
the substructure of
\mathfrak{B}
generated by the set
\dom(\mathfrak{B})|\mathcal{G}
and call it a
limit ultrapower
of
\mathfrak{A}
.

The limit ultrapower
\mathfrak{B}|\mathcal{G}
is a substructure of the ultrapower
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{A}^{I}/\mathcal{F}
that consists of equivalence classes of those sequences
f\in\mathfrak{A}^{I}
that are “almost constant” according to the filter
\mathcal{G}
. In the definition we do not require
\mathcal{G}
to be proper; if
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{P}(I^{2})
, we have
\mathfrak{B}|\mathcal{G}=\mathfrak{B}
, so in this sense limit ultrapowers generalize ultrapowers. It is proved in
[
15
]
that
\mathfrak{B}|\mathcal{G}
is always an elementary substructure of
\mathfrak{B}
, and that the ultrapower embedding
a\mapsto(a)_{i\in I}/\mathcal{F}
is an elementary embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}|\mathcal{G}
.

Limit ultrapowers are connected to complete embeddings, whose definition we now recall.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
\tau
-structures. An embedding
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is
complete
if for every
\tau^{\prime}\supseteq\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\mathfrak{A}
there is a
\tau^{\prime}
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
f
is an elementary embedding
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}\to\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Clearly the ultrapower embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{I}/\mathcal{F}
is complete. It turns out that this is a special case of the more general fact that the ultrapower embedding to a limit ultrapower is complete. In fact, every complete embedding is essentially a limit ultrapower embedding.

Theorem 2.17

(
[
15
]
)
.

The following are equivalent for
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.

(i)

f
is a complete embedding.

(ii)

There are
I
,
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
and an isomorphism
\pi\colon(\mathfrak{A}^{I}/\mathcal{F})|\mathcal{G}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
f=\pi\circ\iota
, where
\iota
is the ultrapower embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to(\mathfrak{A}^{I}/\mathcal{F})|\mathcal{G}
.

3.
Team Maps

In this section we introduce the notions of partial isomorphism and elementary map for team semantics and prove some of their basic characterisations. For the rest of the paper (except
Section

6
), we identify a relation
X
with the corresponding team
\team(X)
with domain
\{v_{0},\dots,v_{\ar(X)-1}\}
. If
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
is a formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
or
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
for a structure
\mathfrak{A}
, then by
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
we mean
\mathfrak{A}\models_{\team(X)}\phi
. If
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
and
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, then by
X(a/n)
we denote the
n+1
-ary relation
X\times\{a\}
. Note that
\team(X(a/n))=\team(X)(a/v_{n})
. Also note that
\emptyset(a/0)=\emptyset
and
\{\emptyset\}(a/0)=\{a\}
. Given a structure
\mathfrak{A}
, we denote by
\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}
the diagonal set
\{(a,a)\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}\}
, i.e. the identity relation (
a=b
if and only if
(a,b)\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be
\tau
-structures.

(i)

Given a set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
, we denote by
\cl(\mathcal{X};\mathfrak{A})
the
\subseteq
-least set
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
such that

(C1)

\emptyset\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}
for all
n<\omega
,

(C2)

if
X,Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, then
X\cap Y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

(C3)

if
X,Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, then
X\times Y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

(C4)

if
X\in\mathcal{Y}
is
n
-ary and
\vec{\imath}\in n^{<\omega}
, then
\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X)\in\mathcal{Y}
, and

(C5)

\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}},R^{\mathfrak{A}},F^{\mathfrak{A}},\{c^{\mathfrak{A}}\}\in\mathcal{Y}
for all relation symbols
R\in\tau
, function symbols
F\in\tau
and constant symbols
c\in\tau
.

If the structure
\mathfrak{A}
is clear from the context (which it almost always is), we simply write
\cl(\mathcal{X})
for
\cl(\mathcal{X};\mathfrak{A})
.

(ii)

We say that
\mathcal{f}
is a
(partial) team map

\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
if it is a (partial) function
\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})\to\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{B})
such that

•

\mathcal{f}
preserves arities, i.e. if
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
, then
\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
, and

•

the domain and range of
\mathcal{f}
satisfy the closure properties
(i)(C1)
–
(i)(C5)
, i.e.
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\dom(\mathcal{f});\mathfrak{A})
and
\ran(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\ran(\mathcal{f});\mathfrak{B})
.

If
\mathcal{f}
maps all singletons from its domain to singletons and
\{\vec{a}\}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, we write
\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})
for the unique element inhabiting the singleton
\mathcal{f}(\{\vec{a}\})
. We write
\mathcal{f}{\upharpoonright}_{n}
for the function
\mathcal{f}{\upharpoonright}(\dom(\mathcal{f})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{n}))
.

(iii)

We say that a partial team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a
partial team isomorphism
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(PI1)

X=\emptyset
if and only if
\mathcal{f}(X)=\emptyset
, and
X=\mathfrak{A}^{n}
if and only if
\mathcal{f}(X)=\mathfrak{B}^{n}
for all
n<\omega
,

(PI2)

for all
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,
X
is a singleton if and only if
\mathcal{f}(X)
is,

(PI3)

for all
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,
\mathcal{f}(X\times Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(Y)
,

(PI4)

for all
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
\vec{\imath}\in n^{<\omega}
,
\mathcal{f}(\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X))=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathcal{f}(X))
,

(PI5)

for all
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,
X\subseteq Y
if and only if
\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(Y)
, and

(PI6)

\mathcal{f}(\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}})=\Delta_{\mathfrak{B}}
,
\mathcal{f}(R^{\mathfrak{A}})=R^{\mathfrak{B}}
for any relation or function symbol
R\in\tau
and
\mathcal{f}(c^{\mathfrak{A}})=c^{\mathfrak{B}}
for any constant symbol
c\in\tau
.

If
\mathcal{f}
happens to be total, then we call
\mathcal{f}
a
team embedding
. If
\mathcal{f}
is such that for any
a\in\mathfrak{A}
,
\{a\}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, then we call
\mathcal{f}

element-total
, and if for any
b\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\{b\}\in\ran(\mathcal{f})
, then we call
\mathcal{f}

element-surjective
.

(iv)

We say that
\mathcal{f}
is a
team isomorphism
if it is an element-surjective team embedding. If
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an ordinary isomorphism, we denote by
\hat{\pi}
the team isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
defined by

\hat{\pi}(X)=\{\pi(\vec{a})\mid\vec{a}\in X\}

for all relations
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
.

(v)

We say that a (partial) team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a (partial)
elementary team map
if it satisfies
(iii)(PI3)
, and for all
\tau
-formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
and
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, we have

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

If
\mathcal{f}
happens to be total, then we call
\mathcal{f}
an
elementary team embedding
.

(vi)

We say that a (partial) elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a (partial)
independence team map
if for all
\tau
-formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
, we have

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\implies\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

If
\mathcal{f}
happens to be total, then we call
\mathcal{f}
an
independence team embedding
.

Note that
\mathcal{f}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
vacuously holds for any team map
\mathcal{f}
: the empty set is the only relation of every arity, and
\mathcal{f}
preserves arity of relations. If
\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, then
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})=(\mathcal{f}(a_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}(a_{n-1})
.

Example 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure,
\kappa
an infinite cardinal and
\mathcal{U}
an ultrafilter on
\kappa
. Let
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Now
\iota\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
defined by
\iota(X)=X^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
is an elementary team embedding by
Theorem

2.11
and furthermore an independence team embedding by
Theorem

2.12
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{f}
be a partial team isomorphism. Then

(i)

\mathcal{f}
is injective,

(ii)

\mathcal{f}(X(a/n))=\mathcal{f}(X)(\mathcal{f}(a)/n)
for an
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
\{a\}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
.

(iii)

\mathcal{f}(X\cap Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)
for all
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
.

Proof.

We show the third claim.
Let
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
. If they are of different arity, the intersection is empty and hence mapped to the empty set, so we may assume
\ar(X)=\ar(Y)
. By
(iii)(PI5)
, as
X\cap Y\subseteq X,Y
, we have
\mathcal{f}(X\cap Y)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X),\mathcal{f}(Y)
and hence
\mathcal{f}(X\cap Y)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)
. For the converse, note that as
\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)\in\ran(\mathcal{f})
, there is some
Z\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
such that
\mathcal{f}(Z)=\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)
. Since
\mathcal{f}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)
, we have
\mathcal{f}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X),\mathcal{f}(Y)
, so by
(iii)(PI5)
we have
Z\subseteq X,Y
. But then
Z\subseteq X\cap Y
. Thus
\mathcal{f}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X\cap Y)
. But as
\mathcal{f}(Z)=\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)
, we have
\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathcal{f}(Y)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X\cap Y)
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathcal{f}^{-1}
is a partial team isomorphism
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
. If
\mathcal{f}
is a partial elementary team map
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathcal{f}^{-1}
is a partial elementary team map
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
are partial team isomorphisms with
\ran(\mathcal{f})=\dom(\mathcal{g})
, then
\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
. If
\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}
are elementary, then so is
\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f}
. If
\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}
are independence team maps, then so is
\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f}
.

Proof.

Clear. ∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a partial team map. Then for any
n,m<\omega
and
n
-ary and
m
-ary relations
X
and
Y
of
\mathfrak{A}
, respectively, the following holds:
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
if and only if for all
k\leq n,m
,
X\bowtie_{k}Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism, then
\mathcal{f}(X\bowtie_{k}Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\bowtie_{k}\mathcal{f}(Y)
for all
k
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an arbitrary structure. We first show that for any
n<\omega
, the
2n
-ary relation

\Delta^{n}_{\mathfrak{C}}\coloneqq\{\vec{a}\vec{b}\mid|\vec{a}|=|\vec{b}|=n,\vec{a}=\vec{b}\}

is expressible in terms of products, projections and
\Delta_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Note that
\Delta^{0}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\{\emptyset\}=\Pr_{\emptyset}(\Delta_{\mathfrak{C}})
and
\Delta^{1}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\Delta_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Suppose we have shown this for
\Delta^{n}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Then

\displaystyle\Delta^{n+1}_{\mathfrak{C}}

\displaystyle=\{\vec{a}a\vec{b}b\mid|\vec{a}|=|\vec{b}|=n,\vec{a}=\vec{b},a=b\}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\{\vec{a}\vec{b}ab\mid|\vec{a}|=|\vec{b}|=n,\vec{a}=\vec{b},a=b\})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\Delta^{n}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\Delta_{\mathfrak{C}}),

where
\vec{\imath}=(0,\dots,n-1,2n,n,\dots,2n-1,2n+1)
.

Now for any
n+k
-ary and
k+m
-ary relations
X
and
Y
of
\mathfrak{C}
, we have

\displaystyle X\bowtie_{k}Y

\displaystyle=\{\vec{a}\vec{b}\vec{c}\mid\vec{a}\vec{b}\in X,\vec{b}\vec{c}\in Y\}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}}((X\times Y)\cap(\mathfrak{C}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathfrak{C}^{m})),

where
\vec{\jmath}=(0,\dots,n+k-1,n+2k,\dots,n+2k+m-1)
.

Now it is clear that
\Delta^{k}_{\mathfrak{A}}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
. It follows that
X\bowtie_{k}Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
whenever
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
. On the other hand, if
X\bowtie_{0}Y=X\times Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, then
X
and
Y
are easily obtained as projections. If
\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism, then clearly
\mathcal{f}(\Delta^{k}_{\mathfrak{A}})=\Delta^{k}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, whence also
\mathcal{f}(X\bowtie_{k}Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\bowtie_{k}\mathcal{f}(Y)
.
∎

The next proposition highlights a connection between partial elementary team maps and complete atomic Boolean algebras.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{f}
be a partial elementary team map. Then

(i)

for all
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
of the same arity,

\mathcal{f}(X\cup Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\cup\mathcal{f}(Y),

whenever
X\cup Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, and

(ii)

for all
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,

\mathcal{f}(X^{c})=\mathcal{f}(X)^{c},

whenever
X^{c}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, then for any
n<\omega
,
\mathcal{f}{\upharpoonright}_{n}
is an (ordinary) elementary embedding between
(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{n}),\cup,\cap,{}^{c},\emptyset,\mathfrak{A}^{n})
and
(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{B}^{n}),\cup,\cap,{}^{c},\emptyset,\mathfrak{B}^{n})
in the language
\{\lor,\land,\neg,\bot,\top\}
of Boolean algebras.

Proof.

(i)

Denote
\vec{x}=(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
,
\vec{y}=(v_{n},\dots,v_{2n-1})
,
\vec{z}=(v_{2n},\dots,v_{3n-1})
and
\vec{w}=(v_{3n},\dots,v_{4n-1})
. Then it is straightforward to verify that for any structure
\mathfrak{C}
and nonempty
X,Y,Z\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n}
,
Z=X\cup Y
if and only if

\mathfrak{C}\models_{X\times Y\times Z}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{z}\land\vec{y}\subseteq\vec{z}\land\forall^{1}\vec{w}(\vec{w}\subseteq\vec{z}\rightarrowtriangle(\vec{w}\subseteq\vec{x}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\vec{w}\subseteq\vec{y})),

whence the claim follows.

(ii)

Since
\mathcal{f}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{A}^{n})=\mathfrak{B}^{n}
, the claim holds whenever either of the sets
X
and
Y
is empty, so suppose that is not the case. Denote
\vec{x}=(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
and
\vec{y}=(v_{n},\dots,v_{2n-1})
. Now let
\phi
be the formula

\forall^{1}z_{0}\dots\forall^{1}z_{n-1}(\vec{z}\subseteq\vec{x}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\vec{z}\subseteq\vec{y})\land\vec{x}\mid\vec{y}.

Then

\displaystyle X=Y^{c}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X\times Y}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X\times Y)}\phi

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(Y)}\phi\iff\mathcal{f}(X)=\mathcal{f}(Y)^{c}.

For the “furthermore” part, note that the structures in question are infinite atomic Boolean algebras. The atoms of
(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{n}),\cup,\cap,{}^{c},\emptyset,\mathfrak{A}^{n})
are singletons, and
\mathcal{f}{\upharpoonright}_{n}
maps singletons (and nothing else) to singletons, i.e. preserves the atoms of the Boolean algebra in both directions. Hence it is enough to show that whenever
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
are infinite atomic Boolean algebras and
g\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
is an embedding that preserves atoms both ways, then
g
is an elementary embedding.

Claim
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
are infinite atomic Boolean algebras. Then
g\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
is an elementary embedding if and only if it is an embedding that preserves atoms.

Proof of claim.

If
g
is elementary, then it preserves atoms, as being an atom is a first-order definable property.
For the converse, let
\tau=\{\lor,\land,\neg,\bot,\top\}\cup\{P_{n}\mid n<\omega\}
, i.e. the signature of Boolean algebras augmented with infinitely many fresh unary predicates. Let
T
be the
\tau
-theory consisting of the first-order theory of infinite atomic Boolean algebras and the additional sentences stating that
P_{n}(a)
holds exactly when
a
is above at least
n
atoms in the Boolean algebra order. By
[
23
, Theorem 6.20]
we have that
T
admits quantifier elimination and so
T
is model complete. Now, if
g
is an atom-preserving embedding
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, then it is easy to show that
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}\models P_{n}(a)
if and only if
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}\models P_{n}(f(a))
for all
a\in\mathfrak{C}
, where
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
are the unique
\tau
-expansions of
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
, respectively, that satisfy
T
. This means that
g
is a
\tau
-embedding
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}\to\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
, and as
T
is model complete,
g
is elementary. Hence
g
is also an elementary embedding between the reducts
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
.
∎

3.1.
Characterizations of Partial Isomorphisms and Elementary Maps

Although we have defined partial team isomorphisms simply by requiring some specific commutativity conditions, the next lemma and the following proposition show that these are exactly those team maps which preserve the quantifier-free fragment of
\mathsf{FOT}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a partial team isomorphism. Then
\mathcal{f}
preserves first-order atomic formulas, i.e. for any
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and atomic first-order formula
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

Proof.

One would guess this is relatively easy to see but it proves to be surprisingly tricky due to flatness of first-order logic.

We begin by showing that for any
\tau
-term
t(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
, we have
G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{A})\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{A}))=G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{B})
, where

G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{C})=\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n+1}\mid t^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})=c_{n}\}

is the graph of the function
\vec{c}\mapsto t^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c})
. We proceed by induction on
t
.

(i)

If
t=v_{i}
, then the function
\vec{c}\mapsto t^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c})
is the
i^{\text{th}}
projection. Let
\vec{\imath}=(0,\dots,n-1,i)
. Then

G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{C})=\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n+1}\mid c_{i}=c_{n}\}=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathfrak{C}^{n+1}).

Hence

\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{A}))=\mathcal{f}(\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathfrak{A}^{n+1}))=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{A}^{n+1}))=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathfrak{B}^{n+1})=G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{B}).

(ii)

If
t=c
for a constant symbol
c\in\tau
, then the function
\vec{c}\mapsto t^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c})
is the constant map
\vec{c}\mapsto c
. Then

G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{C})=\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n+1}\mid c_{n}=c^{\mathfrak{C}}\}=\mathfrak{C}^{n}\times\{c^{\mathfrak{C}}\}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{A}^{n}\times\{c^{\mathfrak{A}}\})=\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{A}^{n})\times\mathcal{f}(\{c^{\mathfrak{A}}\})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}^{n}\times\{c^{\mathfrak{B}}\}=G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{B}).

(iii)

Finally suppose that
t=F(t_{0}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}),\dots,t_{m-1}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}))
for
\tau
-terms
t_{i}
and an
m
-ary function symbol
F\in\tau
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t_{i};\mathfrak{A}))=G_{n}(t_{i};\mathfrak{B})
for all
i<m
. First consider the relation

X_{0}(\mathfrak{C})=\{(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}),c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\mid c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{C}\}.

Clearly
X_{0}(\mathfrak{C})=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{0}}(G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{C}))
for
\vec{\imath}_{0}=(n,0,\dots,n-1)
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{f}(X_{0}(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{f}(\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{0}}(G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{A})))=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{0}}(\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{A})))

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{0}}(G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{B}))=X_{0}(\mathfrak{B}).

Then consider the relation

X_{1}(\mathfrak{C})=\{(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),t_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\mid\vec{c}=(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\}.

Denoting
\vec{\imath}_{1}=(0,n+1,1,\dots,n)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{1}(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}(\{(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1},t_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}))\mid\vec{c}=(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}(X_{0}(\mathfrak{C})\bowtie_{n}G_{n}(t_{1};\mathfrak{C})).

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{f}(X_{1}(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{f}(\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}(X_{0}(\mathfrak{A})\bowtie_{n}G_{n}(t_{1};\mathfrak{A})))

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}(\mathcal{f}(X_{0}(\mathfrak{A}))\bowtie_{n}\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t_{1};\mathfrak{A})))

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}_{1}}(X_{0}(\mathfrak{B})\bowtie_{n}G_{n}(t_{1};\mathfrak{B}))

\displaystyle=X_{1}(\mathfrak{B}).

Continuing this way we obtain a description of the relation

\hskip 30.00005ptX_{m-1}(\mathfrak{C})=\{(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),\dots,t_{m-1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\mid\vec{c}=(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\}

as an element of the closure of
G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{C}),\dots,G_{n}(t_{m-1};\mathfrak{C})
under projections and join, whence
\mathcal{f}(X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{A}))=X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{B})
. Then, denoting
\vec{\jmath}_{0}=(0,\dots,n-1,n+m)
and
\vec{\jmath}_{1}=(m,\dots,m+n-1,0,\dots,m-1)
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005ptG_{n}(t;\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{0}}\left(\{(\vec{c},t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),\dots,t_{m-1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),F^{\mathfrak{C}}(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),\dots,t_{m-1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c})))\mid\vec{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{0}}\left(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}}\big(X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{C})\big)\bowtie_{m}F^{\mathfrak{C}}\right),

whence

\displaystyle\mathcal{f}(G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{f}\left(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{0}}\left(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}}\left(X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{A})\bowtie_{m}F^{\mathfrak{A}}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{0}}\left(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}}\left(\mathcal{f}(X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{A}))\bowtie_{m}\mathcal{f}(F^{\mathfrak{A}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{0}}\left(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}}\left(X_{m-1}(\mathfrak{B})\bowtie_{m}F^{\mathfrak{B}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=G_{n}(t;\mathfrak{B}).

Now we show that
\mathcal{f}
preserves atomic formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of first-order logic. Suppose
\phi=R(t_{0},\dots,t_{m-1})
for some
m
-ary relation symbol
R\in\tau
and
\tau
-terms
t_{i}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
,
i<m
. Now

\displaystyle\phi(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\mid\mathfrak{C}\models\phi(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\}

\displaystyle\,=\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\mid(t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}),\dots,t_{m-1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}))\in R^{\mathfrak{C}}\}

\displaystyle\,=\Pr_{(0,\dots,n-1)}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})\bowtie_{m}R^{\mathfrak{C}}),

where

\displaystyle Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1},t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),\dots,t_{m-1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}))\mid\vec{c}=(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\}.

If we can show that
Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
and
\mathcal{f}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))=Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{f}(\phi(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{f}\left(\Pr_{(0,\dots,n-1)}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))\bowtie_{m}R^{\mathfrak{A}}\right)=\Pr_{(0,\dots,n-1)}(\mathcal{f}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{A}))\bowtie_{m}\mathcal{f}(R^{\mathfrak{A}}))

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(0,\dots,n-1)}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{B})\bowtie_{m}R^{\mathfrak{B}})

\displaystyle=\phi(\mathfrak{B}).

Then for any
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi

\displaystyle\iff X\subseteq\phi(\mathfrak{A})\iff\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(\phi(\mathfrak{A}))

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\phi(\mathfrak{B})\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi,

as desired. So left is to show that
Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})
can be presented in terms of products, projections, intersections and the sets
G(t_{i};\mathfrak{C})
. We show this by induction on
m>0
. If
m=1
, then
Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})=G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{C})
, so we have already proved this. If we have already handled
Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})
, then

\displaystyle Y^{m+1}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1},c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1},t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c}),\dots,t_{m}^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{c})\mid\vec{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\})

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\Pr_{\vec{\jmath}}(Y^{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{C})\times G_{n}(t_{m};\mathfrak{C}))\cap\Delta^{n}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathfrak{C}^{m+1}),

where
\vec{\imath}=(0,\dots,n-1,2n,\dots,2n+m)
(skips the second batch of
c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}
) and
\vec{\jmath}=(0,\dots,n-1,n+m,\dots,2n+m-1,n,\dots,n+m-1,2n+m)
(permutes the second batch of
c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1}
to be after the first).

Then, finally, suppose that
\phi
is the equation
t_{0}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})=t_{1}(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
. Now

\displaystyle\phi(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle=\{(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{n}\mid t_{0}^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})=t_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}}(c_{0},\dots,c_{n-1})\}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(0,\dots,n-1)}(G_{n}(t_{0};\mathfrak{C})\cap G_{n}(t_{1};\mathfrak{C})).

Thus
\mathcal{f}(\phi(\mathfrak{A}))=\phi(\mathfrak{B})
. Then we obtain

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi,

which concludes the proof. ∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a team map. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{f}
is a partial team isomorphism.

(ii)

\mathcal{f}
satisfies
(iii)(PI3)
and for all quantifier-free formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
and
n
-ary
X\in\dom(f)
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

In particular, a partial elementary team map is a partial team isomorphism.

Proof.

We first show that if
\mathcal{f}
satisfies
(iii)(PI3)
and preserves quantifier-free formulas of
\mathsf{FOT}
, then it is a partial team isomorphism.
We show
(iii)(PI5)
, the rest are similar.
Let
X,Y\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
be
n
-ary. If
X=\emptyset
, then
X\subseteq Y
and
\mathcal{f}(X)=\emptyset\subseteq\mathcal{f}(Y)
. If
X\neq\emptyset=Y
, then
X\nsubseteq Y
and
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{f}(X)\nsubseteq\mathcal{f}(Y)=\emptyset
.
Otherwise denote
\vec{x}=(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
and
\vec{y}=(v_{n},\dots,v_{2n-1})
. Then

\displaystyle X\subseteq Y

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X\times Y}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X\times Y)}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\ref{Partial isomorphisms preserve products}}}}{{\iff}}\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(Y)}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{f}(Y).

Next we show that if
\mathcal{f}
is a partial isomorphism, then for all quantifier-free formulas
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
and
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

We proceed by induction on
\phi
.
The case for first-order atomic formulas follows from
Lemma

3.7
, and the connective cases are immediate consequences of the induction hypothesis. We show the case for dependence atoms.
Suppose that
\phi=\dep(\vec{x})
for some
\vec{x}\in\{v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}\}^{<\omega}
, and let
\vec{\imath}
be the corresponding index tuple. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi

\displaystyle\iff\text{$X$ is empty or $\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X)$ is a singleton}

\displaystyle\iff\text{$\mathcal{f}(X)$ is empty or $\mathcal{f}(\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X))$ is a singleton}

\displaystyle\iff\text{$\mathcal{f}(X)$ is empty or $\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(\mathcal{f}(X))$ is a singleton}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.\qed

We conclude this section by pointing out some equivalent characterisations of elementary team maps. The usual Tarski–Vaught test applies immediately also in this context.

Proposition 3.9

(The Tarski–Vaught Test for
\mathsf{FOT}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a team map. The following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding.

(ii)

\mathcal{f}
is a team embedding and satisfies the following condition:

For any formula
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
,

if
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\exists^{1}v_{n}\phi
, there is
a\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)(\mathcal{f}(a)/n)}\phi
.

Proof.

After the observation that
\mathcal{f}(X(a/n))=\mathcal{f}(X)(\mathcal{f}(a)/n)
for all
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, the proof is identical to its first-order counterpart.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a team map. The following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{f}
is a partial elementary team map.

(ii)

For any first-order sentence
\phi(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
with free second-order variables
R_{i}
, and for any nonempty
\ar(R_{i})
-ary
X_{i}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,
i<n
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(\mathcal{f}(X_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}(X_{n-1})).

(iii)

For any
n<\omega
and
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, let
\underline{X}
be a new
n
-ary relation symbol and denote
\tau^{\prime}=\tau\cup\{\underline{X}\mid X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})\}
. Then the (partial) function
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
,
f(a)=\mathcal{f}(a)
for all
\{a\}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
, is a partial elementary map between the
\tau^{\prime}
-structures
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
, where
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}{\upharpoonright}\tau=\mathfrak{A}
,
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}{\upharpoonright}\tau=\mathfrak{B}
,
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}}=X
and
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}=\mathcal{f}(X)
.

Proof.

The equivalence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
follows immediately by the following observation. For any first-order
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1},\vec{R})
and elements
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
such that
\{a_{0}\},\dots,\{a_{n-1}\}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1},\vec{X})\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\phi^{*}(\{a_{0}\},\dots,\{a_{n-1}\},\vec{X}),

where
\phi^{*}(S_{0},\dots,S_{n-1},\vec{R})
is the formula

\exists x_{0}\dots\exists x_{n-1}\left(\phi\land\bigwedge_{i<n}(S_{i}(x_{i})\land\forall y(S_{i}(y)\to y=x_{i}))\right)

and
S_{i}
are fresh unary second-order variables. We next show the equivalence of
(ii)
and
(i)
.

(ii)
\implies
(i)
: The fact that
\mathcal{f}
preserves formulas of
\mathsf{FOT}
follows directly from
Lemma

2.7
. We show that
\mathcal{f}
satisfies
(iii)(PI3)
. Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
be an
n
-ary and
m
-ary nonempty relation, respectively, and let
R
and
R^{\prime}
be an
n
-ary and an
m
-ary and
S
an
n+m
-ary second-order variable. We let
\phi(S,R,R^{\prime})
be the sentence

\forall x_{0}\dots\forall x_{n-1}\forall y_{0}\dots\forall y_{m-1}(S(\vec{x}\vec{y})\leftrightarrow(R(\vec{x})\land R^{\prime}(\vec{y})),

expressing that “
S
is the cartesian product of
R
and
R^{\prime}
”. Denote
Y=X\times X^{\prime}
. Then
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(Y,X,X^{\prime})
, whence
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(\mathcal{f}(Y),\mathcal{f}(X),\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime}))
. Now
\mathcal{f}(Y)=\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime})
as desired.

(i)
\implies
(ii)
: Let
\phi(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
be a first-order sentence with free second-order variables
R_{i}
of arity
n_{i}
. The first observation is that whenever
X_{i}
is empty, so is
\mathcal{f}(X_{i})
. Let
\phi^{*}(R_{0},\dots,R_{i-1},R_{i+1},\dots,R_{n-1})
be the formula obtained by replacing in
\phi
each occurrence of formulas of the form
X_{i}(\vec{t}\,)
by
\bot
. Then, whenever
X_{i}=\emptyset
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\phi^{*}(X_{0},\dots,X_{i-1},X_{i+1},\dots,X_{n-1}),

and the same holds for
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{f}(X_{j})
.
Hence we only need to consider nonempty
X_{i}
.

Now let
\psi(R)
be the sentence one obtains by replacing in
\phi
formulas of the form
R_{i}(t_{0},\dots,t_{n_{i}-1})
by

\exists\vec{x}\exists\vec{y}R(\vec{x},t_{0},\dots,t_{n_{i}-1},\vec{y}),

where
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
are variable tuples of length
\sum_{j<i}n_{j}
and
\sum_{i<j<n}n_{j}
, respectively, and
R
is a
\sum_{i<n}n_{i}
-ary second order variable. Now for any structure
\mathfrak{C}
and
X_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n_{i}}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})\iff\mathfrak{C}\models\psi\left(\prod_{i<n}X_{i}\right).

Denote
N=\sum_{i<n}n_{i}
. By
Lemma

2.7
the is a formula
\chi(v_{0},\dots,v_{N-1})
of
\mathsf{FOT}
such that for any structure
\mathfrak{C}
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{N}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models\exists\vec{x}R(\vec{x})\to\psi(X)\iff\mathfrak{C}\models_{X}\chi.

Now, let
X_{i}\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
be nonempty and denote
X=\prod_{i<n}X_{i}
. As
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, we have
\mathcal{f}(X)=\prod_{i<n}\mathcal{f}(X_{i})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\psi\left(X\right)\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\chi

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}\left(X\right)}\chi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\psi\left(\mathcal{f}\left(X\right)\right)

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\psi\left(\prod_{i<n}\mathcal{f}(X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(\mathcal{f}(X_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}(X_{n-1})).\qed

Corollary 3.11
.

If
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a partial elementary team map, then
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
.

3.2.
Elementary Team Embeddings and Isomorphisms

Earlier we noticed that the ultrapower embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
,
\iota(X)=X^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
, is not just an elementary team embedding but an independence team embedding as well. This is actually a general phenomenon: in fact, all elementary team embeddings preserve
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
to the direction of the map, as demonstrated by the next result. The true difference between elementary and independence team maps shows up at the level of partial maps.

Proposition 3.12
.

If
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an elementary team embedding, then it is an independence team embedding.

Proof.

Let
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
be a formula of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
, let
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
. If
X=\emptyset
, then also
\mathcal{f}(X)=\emptyset
(and vice versa) and hence trivially
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi
. So we may assume that
X\neq\emptyset\neq\mathcal{f}(X)
.

By the translation of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
to
\mathsf{ESO}
and
Lemma

2.1
, there exists a first-order
\tau
-sentence
\alpha(R,R^{\prime})
such that for any structure
\mathfrak{C}
and
Y\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models_{Y}\phi\iff\mathfrak{C}\models\exists R^{\prime}\alpha(Y,R^{\prime}),

where
R^{\prime}
is some
m
-ary relation symbol. Hence
\mathfrak{A}\models\exists R^{\prime}\alpha(X,R^{\prime})
. Now, there is
X^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{m}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\alpha(X,X^{\prime})
.
Now either
X^{\prime}=\emptyset
or
X^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. We look at the two cases separately.

(i)

Suppose that
X^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Let
\alpha^{*}(R)
be the formula one obtains from
\alpha
by replacing every occurence of
R^{\prime}
by
\bot
. Then for any
\mathfrak{C}
and
Y\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models\alpha(Y,\emptyset)\iff\mathfrak{C}\models\alpha^{*}(Y).

Now
\mathfrak{A}\models\alpha^{*}(X)
. By the translation of
\mathsf{FO}
to
\mathsf{FOT}
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\psi
of
\mathsf{FOT}
such that for any structure
\mathfrak{C}
and
Y\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models\exists\vec{x}Y(\vec{x})\to\alpha^{*}(Y)\iff\mathfrak{C}\models_{Y}\psi.

Since
\mathfrak{A}\models\exists\vec{x}X(\vec{x})\to\alpha^{*}(X)
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi
. Since
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, we obtain
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\psi
. Then
\mathfrak{B}\models\exists\vec{x}\mathcal{f}(X)(\vec{x})\to\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{f}(X))
, and as
\mathcal{f}(X)
is nonempty, we have
\mathfrak{B}\models\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{f}(X))
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\alpha(\mathcal{f}(X),\emptyset)
, and so
\mathfrak{B}\models\exists R^{\prime}\alpha(\mathcal{f}(X),R^{\prime})
. Thus
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi
.

(ii)

If
X^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then let
X^{\prime\prime}=X\times X^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha^{*}
be the formula one obtains from
\alpha
by replacing each occurence of
R(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})
by

\exists x_{0}\dots\exists x_{m-1}R^{\prime\prime}(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},x_{0},\dots,x_{m-1})

and each
R^{\prime}(t_{0},\dots,t_{m-1})
by

\exists x_{0}\dots\exists x_{n-1}R^{\prime\prime}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1},t_{0},\dots,t_{m-1}),

where
x_{i}
are fresh variables. By the translation of
\mathsf{FO}
to
\mathsf{FOT}
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\psi
of
\mathsf{FOT}
such that for all
\mathfrak{C}
and
Y\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{n+m}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models\exists\vec{x}R^{\prime\prime}(\vec{x})\to\alpha^{*}(Y)\iff\mathfrak{C}\models_{Y}\psi.

Now as
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X^{\prime\prime}}\psi
, and as
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, we have
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime\prime})}\psi
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\exists\vec{x}R^{\prime\prime}(\vec{x})\to\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime\prime}))
. As
X
and
X^{\prime}
are both nonempty, so is
X^{\prime\prime}
and thus so is
\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore
\mathfrak{B}\models\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime\prime}))
. As
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, we have
\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime})
. As
\mathfrak{B}\models\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{f}(X)\times\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime}))
, we have
\mathfrak{B}\models\alpha(\mathcal{f}(X),\mathcal{f}(X^{\prime}))
and thus
\mathfrak{B}\models\exists R^{\prime}\alpha(\mathcal{f}(X),R^{\prime})
. But then
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi
.

Thus
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we show that element-total and element-surjective partial team isomorphisms and all only those arising from usual isomorphism.

Proposition 3.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an element-total and element-surjective partial team isomorphism. Then there is an (ordinary) isomorphism
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathcal{f}\subseteq\hat{\pi}
.

Proof.

Define
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
by setting
\pi(a)=\mathcal{f}(a)
(i.e.
\pi(a)
is the unique element of
\mathcal{f}(\{a\})
) for all
a\in\mathfrak{A}
. By
Proposition

3.8
,
\pi
is an embedding, and as
\pi
is surjective by assumption, it is an isomorphism.

Let
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
be
n
-ary. Then for any
\vec{b}\in\mathcal{f}(X)
we have
\{\vec{b}\}\in\ran(\mathcal{f})
. By
(iii)(PI2)
, there is
\vec{a}\in\mathfrak{A}^{n}
such that
\pi(\vec{a})=\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})=\vec{b}
. Hence
\mathcal{f}(X)\subseteq\{\pi(\vec{a})\mid\vec{a}\in X\}
. Then note that given any
\vec{a}\in\mathfrak{A}^{n}
, by
(iii)(PI5)
we have

\displaystyle\vec{a}\in X

\displaystyle\implies\{\vec{a}\}\subseteq X\implies\mathcal{f}(\{\vec{a}\})\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X)\implies\{\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})\}\subseteq\mathcal{f}(X)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})\in\mathcal{f}(X)\implies\pi(\vec{a})\in\mathcal{f}(X).

Hence
\mathcal{f}(X)\supseteq\{\pi(\vec{a})\mid\vec{a}\in X\}
. Thus
\mathcal{f}(X)=\hat{\pi}(X)
. It follows that
\mathcal{f}\subseteq\hat{\pi}
.
∎

Corollary 3.14
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a team map. Then
\mathcal{f}
is a team isomorphism if and only if there is an (ordinary) isomorphism
