\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathcal{f}=\hat{\pi}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
be a team embedding, and let
\mathfrak{B}
be a structure such that
\dom(\mathfrak{B})=\{\mathcal{f}(a)\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}\}
, if
R\in\tau
is a relation or function symbol, then
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{f}(R^{\mathfrak{A}})\cap\mathfrak{B}^{\ar(R)}
, and
c^{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{f}(c^{\mathfrak{A}})
for constant symbols
c\in\tau
. Then

(i)

\mathfrak{B}
is a substructure of
\mathfrak{C}
,

(ii)

\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathcal{g}(X)=\mathcal{f}(X)\cap\mathfrak{B}^{n}
for
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
, is a team isomorphism, and

(iii)

if
\mathcal{f}
is elementary, then also
\mathcal{f}\circ\mathcal{g}^{-1}
is elementary and
\mathfrak{B}
is an elementary substructure of
\mathfrak{C}
.

Proof.

We show that
\mathfrak{B}
is a substructure of
\mathfrak{C}
. The other claims are proved in a similar fashion.

First of all, note that
\mathcal{g}(a)=\mathcal{f}(a)
for all elements
a\in\mathfrak{A}
. Additionally,

\mathcal{f}(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})=(\mathcal{f}(a_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}(a_{n-1}))=(\mathcal{g}(a_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{g}(a_{n-1}))=\mathcal{g}(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}),

whence
\mathcal{g}(\vec{a})=\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})
for all
\vec{a}\in\mathfrak{A}^{n}
.
If
R\in\tau
is an
n
-ary relation symbol, then
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{f}(R^{\mathfrak{A}})\cap\mathfrak{B}^{n}=R^{\mathfrak{C}}\cap\mathfrak{B}^{n}
. If
F\in\tau
is a function symbol, the same holds for
F
, i.e.
F^{\mathfrak{B}}=F^{\mathfrak{C}}\cap\mathfrak{B}^{n}
. We show that
\mathfrak{B}
is closed under
F^{\mathfrak{C}}
. Let
\vec{b}\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
. Then
\vec{b}=\mathcal{f}(\vec{a})
for some
\vec{a}\in\mathfrak{A}^{n}
. Let
a=F^{\mathfrak{A}}(\vec{a})
. Then
\vec{a}a\in F^{\mathfrak{A}}
, so
\mathcal{f}(\vec{a}a)\in\mathcal{f}(F^{\mathfrak{A}})=F^{\mathfrak{C}}
, so
\mathcal{f}(a)=F^{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{f}(\vec{a}))=F^{\mathfrak{C}}(\vec{b})
. But by the definition of
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathcal{f}(a)\in\mathfrak{B}
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}
is closed under
F^{\mathfrak{C}}
, and thus
F^{\mathfrak{B}}
is well defined. Finally,
\mathfrak{B}
contains
c^{\mathfrak{C}}
for all constant symbols
c\in\tau
, as
c^{\mathfrak{C}}=\mathcal{f}(c^{\mathfrak{A}})
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}
is a substructure of
\mathfrak{C}
.
∎

3.3.
Diagrams

In this section, we give a simple condition to check if there is an elementary team embedding from a structure
\mathfrak{A}
to a structure
\mathfrak{B}
. Such condition is the obvious team-semantic extension of the methods of elementary diagrams used to test for elementary embeddings in first-order logic. In turn, this provides us with a connection between elementary team embeddings and complete (first-order) embeddings.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure,
A\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
.

(i)

By
\tau(A)
we denote the expansion of
\tau
by names for all elements of
A
, i.e. the signature
\tau\cup\{\underline{a}\mid a\in A\}
, where each
\underline{a}
is a fresh constant symbol. By
\tau(\mathcal{X})
we mean the expansion of
\tau
by names for all possible
nonempty
elements of
\mathcal{X}
, i.e. the signature
\tau\cup\bigcup_{n<\omega}\{\underline{X}\mid\emptyset\neq X\in\mathcal{X}\}
, where
\underline{X}
is a fresh
\ar(X)
-ary relation symbol for any
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

By
\mathfrak{A}_{A}
we mean the
\tau(A)
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\underline{a}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}}=a
for all
a\in A
. By
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
we mean the
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\mathfrak{A}
with
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}}=X
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

(iii)

The
(elementary) team diagram of
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, denoted by
\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, is the complete
\mathsf{FOT}
-theory of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then every
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
with
\mathfrak{B}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
has a unique
\tau(\cl(\mathcal{X}))
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
such that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\cl(\mathcal{X}))
.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathfrak{A}^{n}
,
\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
s^{\mathfrak{A}}
for all symbols
s\in\tau
are each definable by a first-order
\tau
-sentence. Likewise, given relations
X,Y\in\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
, the relations
X\cap Y
,
X\times Y
and
\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X)
for any
\vec{\imath}\in\ar(X)^{<\omega}
are each definable by a first-order sentence with only second-order parameters
X
and
Y
. Hence the relations
\underline{\mathfrak{A}^{n}}
,
\underline{\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}}
,
\underline{s^{\mathfrak{A}}}
for
s\in\tau
,
\underline{X\cap Y}
,
\underline{X\times Y}
and
\underline{\Pr_{\vec{\imath}}(X)}
are each definable by a
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-sentence of first-order logic. Hence each of them is also definable by a
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-sentence of
\mathsf{FOT}
. The claim clearly follows.
∎

Lemma 3.18
.

For
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

There is a
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
with
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
.

(ii)

There is a partial elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a partial elementary team map with
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\mathcal{X})
. Then by
Proposition

3.10
, the map
a\mapsto\mathcal{f}(a)
is a partial elementary map
\mathfrak{A}_{\cl(\mathcal{X})}\to\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
, where
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
is the
\tau(\cl(\mathcal{X}))
-expansion of
\mathfrak{B}
satisfying
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}=\mathcal{f}(X)
. Then
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\cl(\mathcal{X}))
. Then
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}{\upharpoonright}\tau(\mathcal{X})
is the desired expansion of
\mathfrak{B}
.

For the converse, suppose there is some
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A})
. By
Lemma

3.17
, there is a unique
\tau(\cl(\mathcal{X}))
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}
of
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
such that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\cl(\mathcal{X}))
. Let
\Gamma
be the set of
\mathsf{FO}
-translations of sentences of
\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\cl(\mathcal{X}))
. Now
\Gamma
is the complete first-order
\tau(\cl(\mathcal{X}))
-theory of
\mathfrak{A}_{\cl(\mathcal{X})}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\models\Gamma
. Define a team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
by setting
\mathcal{f}(X)=\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}
for all
X\in\cl(\mathcal{X})
. Let
\phi(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
be a first-order
\tau
-sentence, let
X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1}\in\cl(\mathcal{X})
with
\ar(X_{i})=\ar(R_{i})
and suppose that
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})
. Then
\mathfrak{A}_{\cl(\mathcal{X})}\models\phi(\underline{X}_{0},\dots,\underline{X}_{n-1})
, whence
\phi(\underline{X}_{0},\dots,\underline{X}_{n-1})\in\Gamma
and so
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\models\phi(\underline{X}_{0},\dots,\underline{X}_{n-1})
. Then, since
\underline{X}_{i}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}=\mathcal{f}(X_{i})
, we have
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(\mathcal{f}(X_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}(X_{n-1})
. By
Proposition

3.10
it follows that
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding.
∎

As a corollary of
Proposition

3.10
, we obtain a connection between complete embeddings in first-order logic and elementary team embeddings in our setting: every elementary team embedding is a complete embedding on the level of elements, and conversely every complete embedding gives rise to an elementary team embedding.

Corollary 3.19
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be an elementary team embedding. Then the function
a\mapsto\mathcal{f}(a)
is a(n ordinary) complete embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.

(ii)

Let
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a(n ordinary) complete embedding. Then there is an elementary team embedding
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathcal{f}(a)=f(a)
for all
a\in\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

(i)

Follows immediately from
Proposition

3.10
.

(ii)

As
f
is complete, there is a
\tau(\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A}))
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{B}
such that
f
is elementary between
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
. For all
X\in\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
, define
\mathcal{f}(X)=\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}
. Then by
Proposition

3.10
,
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding. Now, for every
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, we have
a\in\{a\}=\underline{\{a\}}^{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})}}
, whence, as
f
is elementary, we obtain
f(a)\in\underline{\{a\}}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}=\mathcal{f}(\{a\})
, yielding
f(a)=\mathcal{f}(a)
. ∎

As every complete embedding is essentially a limit ultrapower embedding, the same holds for elementary team embeddings.

Proposition 3.20
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an elementary team embedding. Then there is a cardinal
\kappa
, an ultrafilter
\mathcal{F}
on
\kappa
and a filter
\mathcal{G}
on
\kappa^{2}
such that, denoting
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{F})|\mathcal{G}
, there is an isomorphism
\pi\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
with

(\hat{\pi}\circ\mathcal{f})(X)=\mathcal{g}(X)\cap\mathfrak{C}^{n}

for all
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
, where
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{F}
is the ultrapower team embedding
{X\mapsto X^{\kappa}/\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Straightforwardly follows from
Theorem

2.17
and
Corollary

3.19
.
∎

4.
Abstract Elementary Classes for Team Semantics

Abstract elementary classes (AECs) were introduced by Shelah in
[
27
]
and provide an important tool to adapt several techniques of elementary model theory beyond the scope of first-order logic. It is, thus, very natural to consider whether we can introduce a suitable AEC to study (complete) theories in independence logic, or, equivalently, existential second-order logic. We start by recalling the definition of abstract elementary class.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of
\tau
-structures and
\preceq
a partial order on
\mathcal{K}
extending the substructure relation. We say that
(\mathcal{K},\preceq)
is an abstract elementary class if the following hold.

(i)

Closure under isomorphism
: If
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an isomorphism, then
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
, and if
\mathfrak{C}\preceq\mathfrak{A}
, then
\pi(\mathfrak{C})\preceq\mathfrak{B}
.

(ii)

Coherence
: If
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\preceq\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathfrak{A}\preceq\mathfrak{B}
.

(iii)

Unions of chains
: If
\alpha
is an ordinal and
(\mathfrak{A}_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is a
\preceq
-increasing continuous sequence of elements of
\mathcal{K}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\preceq\mathfrak{A}_{j}
for
i<j
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}=\bigcup_{i<\beta}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
for
\beta<\alpha
a limit, then

(a)

\bigcup_{i<\alpha}\mathfrak{A}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
,

(b)

for all
j<\alpha
,
\mathfrak{A}_{j}\preceq\bigcup_{i<\alpha}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, and

(c)

Smoothness
: if
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
is such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\preceq\mathfrak{B}
for all
i<\alpha
, then
\bigcup_{i<\alpha}\mathfrak{A}_{i}\preceq\mathfrak{B}
.

(iv)

Löwenheim–Skolem property
: There is a cardinal
\kappa\geq|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
such that such that for all
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
with
|X|\leq\kappa
, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
such that
X\subseteq\mathfrak{B}\preceq\mathfrak{A}
and
|\mathfrak{B}|\leq\kappa
.

We call the least cardinal
\kappa
that satisfies the Löwenheim–Skolem property for
\mathcal{K}
the Löwenheim–Skolem number of
\mathcal{K}
and denote it by
\LS(\mathcal{K})
.

The relation
\preceq
in the above definition is often called “strong embedding”. So far, we have seen two possible candidates for a notion of strong embedding in the context of team semantics:

(i)

a team embedding, and

(ii)

an elementary team embedding (which is the same thing as an independence team embedding).

In this section we shall study all the properties of abstract elementary classes in the context of team semantics. In particular, we shall see that one has modify the definition of an AEC to fit the context of team maps. In fact, in this context it is more natural to consider just embeddings and strong embeddings, rather than to bother with substructures and strong substructures.

We shall see that all key properties of abstract elementary classes essentially hold in our setting, though a major issue is that we have to drop the requirement that strong maps “extend” the substructure relation. We will consider this issue at length in the following section. We begin by studying the properties of coherence, closure under direct limits and Löwenheim–Skolem. Then in
Section

4.4
we conclude our discussion describing a “quasi-AEC” for team semantics, namely an accessible category of models of elementary team embeddings.

4.1.
Coherence

The following example shows why the traditional substructure relation is not a good basis for our study of structures in
\mathsf{ESO}
and
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
.

Example 4.2
.

Let
\tau=\{P\}
, where
P
is a unary predicate, and let
\preceq
be the relation

\mathfrak{A}\preceq\mathfrak{B}
if and only if
\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
and there is an elementary team embedding
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\mathcal{f}(a)=a
for all
a\in\mathfrak{A}
.

We show that coherence fails for this relation. For this, let
\mathfrak{B}
be a
\tau
-structure with
|\mathfrak{B}|=|P^{\mathfrak{B}}|=\aleph_{1}
and
|\mathfrak{B}\setminus P^{\mathfrak{B}}|=\aleph_{0}
. Now
\mathfrak{B}
has a countable elementary substructure
\mathfrak{A}
. As
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
, by the Keisler–Shelah theorem there is
\kappa
and an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\cong B^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Let
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
be an isomorphism, let
\iota_{\mathfrak{A}}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
and
\iota_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
be the ultrapower embeddings, and denote
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Now
\mathcal{f}=\iota_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\mathcal{g}=\pi\circ\iota_{\mathfrak{B}}
are elementary team embeddings
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
, respectively. Let
\phi
be a sentence of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
expressing that
P
and the complement of
P
have the same cardinality. Now, suppose that there is an elementary team embedding
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
(such that
\mathcal{h}(a)=a
for all
a\in\mathfrak{A}
). Now, as
\mathfrak{A}
is countable, we have
|P^{\mathfrak{A}}|=|\mathfrak{A}\setminus P^{\mathfrak{A}}|=\aleph_{0}
, whence
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
. By
Proposition

3.12
,
\mathcal{h}
is an independence team embedding and hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
. But this is impossible, since
|P^{\mathfrak{B}}|>|\mathfrak{B}\setminus P^{\mathfrak{B}}|
. Hence no such
\mathcal{h}
can exist.

The fault in the above example seems to lie in the fact that if
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an independence team embedding,
\mathfrak{B}
may satisfy more sentences of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
than
\mathfrak{A}
, due to the positive nature of
\mathsf{ESO}
. But even in the case that there exist elementary team embeddings
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
and a team embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
that do not move elements, there is no guarantee that these map commute in a nice way. We take this as an argument in favour of a more category-theoretic approach. In particular, the following form of coherence works.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
are elementary team embeddings and
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a team embedding such that
\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}
, then
\mathcal{h}
is elementary.

Proof.

Let
\phi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
be a formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
. Then

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{C}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi\iff\mathfrak{C}\models_{\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{h}(X))}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{h}(X)}\phi.

Hence
\mathcal{h}
is elementary. ∎

4.2.
Direct Limits

Since we are considering (elementary) team embeddings and not so interested in substructures, we want to also consider the natural generalization of unions of chains, that is, direct limits.

If
I
is a set and
\leq
is a binary relation on
I
, we say that
(I,\leq)
is a
\kappa
-directed set
if
\leq
is a preorder (i.e. reflexive and transitive) and every
J\subseteq I
of power
<\kappa
has a
\leq
-upperbound.
(I,\leq)
is a
directed set
if it is an
\aleph_{0}
-directed set. A set
J\subseteq I
is an
upset
if it is upwards closed, i.e. if
i\in I
,
j\in J
and
i\geq j
, then
i\in J
. For an element
i\in I
, the
upward closure

\upset i
of
i
is the upset
\{j\in I\mid j\geq i\}
. For a subset
J\subseteq I
, the upward closure
\upset J
is the upset
\bigcup_{j\in J}\upset j
. A subset
J\subseteq I
is
cofinal
if for every
i\in I
there is
j\in J
with
i\leq j
, i.e. it meets all upsets.

Definition 4.4
.

(i)

Let
(I,\leq)
be a directed set. A directed system of
\tau
-structures and team maps based on
(I,\leq)
is a sequence
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
such that

(a)

each
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is
\tau
-structure and
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}_{j}
a team map,

(b)

for all
i,j\in I
with
j\geq i
, we have
\dom(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})=\dom(\mathcal{f}_{i,i})
and
\mathcal{f}_{i,i}=\id{\upharpoonright}\dom(\mathcal{f}_{i,i})
, and

(c)

for all
i,j,k\in I
with
i\leq j\leq k
, we have
\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{f}_{j,k})
and
\mathcal{f}_{i,k}=\mathcal{f}_{j,k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
.

We say that a directed system is
\kappa
-directed if
(I,\leq)
is a
\kappa
-directed set.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
be a directed system of
\tau
-structures and partial team isomorphisms. We define a structure
\mathfrak{B}\coloneqq\lim\mathcal{A}
, called the
direct limit
of the system
\mathcal{A}
, as follows. The domain of
\mathfrak{B}
is the set of functions
\eta
such that

(a)

\dom(\eta)
is a nonempty upset of
(I,\leq)
,

(b)

\eta(i)\in\mathfrak{A}_{i}
for all
i\in\dom(\eta)
,

(c)

for all
i,j\in\dom(\eta)
, if
i\leq j
, then
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(\eta(i))=\eta(j)
, and

(d)

if
i\in I
,
j\in\dom(\eta)
,
i\leq j
and there is
a\in\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(a)=\eta(j)
, then
i\in\dom(\eta)
.

For all
i\in I
, we define team maps
\mathcal{g}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{B}
as follows: given
X\in\dom(f_{i,i})
, we let
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
be the set of all
(\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{n-1})\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
such that there is some
j\in\bigcap_{k<n}\dom(\eta_{k})
with
j\geq i
such that
(\eta_{0}(j),\dots,\eta_{n-1}(j))\in\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)
. Then the interpretations of relation and function symbols
R\in L
are defined by setting
R^{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{g}_{i}(R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}})
, and for constant symbols
c\in\tau
,
c^{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{g}_{i}(c^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}})
, where
i\in I
is arbitrary.

When there is no risk of confusion, we write simply
\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
instead of
\lim\mathcal{A}
. We call the maps
\mathcal{g}_{i}
the direct limit maps of the system
\mathcal{A}
.

We immediately make the following observations. In particular, this shows that the direct limit is well defined.

Lemma 4.5
.

(i)

If
\eta,\xi\in\lim_{i}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\eta(i)=\xi(i)
for some
i\in\dom(\eta)\cap\dom(\xi)
, then
\eta=\xi
. Hence for any
i\in I
and
a\in\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, there is a unique
\eta\in\lim_{i}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with
\eta(i)=a
.

(ii)

For all
i\leq j
,
\mathcal{g}_{i}=\mathcal{g}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
.

(iii)

The interpretations of symbols
R\in\tau
in
\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
are well defined.

(iv)

Each
\mathcal{g}_{i}
is a well-defined team map.

(v)

For any
i\in I
,
n
-ary
X\in\dom(\mathcal{g}_{i})
and
\vec{a}\in X
, there is unique
\vec{\eta}\in\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
with
\eta_{k}(i)=a_{i}
for all
k<n
. Furthermore,
\vec{\eta}=\mathcal{g}_{i}(\vec{a})
.

(vi)

If each
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is (element-)total, then so is each
\mathcal{g}_{i}
.

(vii)

If
J\subseteq I
is a cofinal subset such that
(J,\leq)
is also directed, then there is an isomorphism
\pi\colon\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{j}\to\lim_{j\in J}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
such that if
\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}
and
\mathcal{g}^{I}_{i}
are the direct limit maps in the systems based on
J
and
I
, respectively, then for all
j\in J
,
\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}=\hat{\pi}\circ\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}
.

Proof.

We prove the last claim and leave the rest to the reader.

Define
\pi
by setting
\pi(\eta)=\eta{\upharpoonright}(\dom(\eta)\cap J)
for all
\eta\in\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
. We show that this is an isomorphism.
For injectivity, let
\eta,\xi\in\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and suppose that
\pi(\eta)=\pi(\xi)
. Let
j\in\dom(\pi(\eta))=\dom(\pi(\xi))
. Now
\eta(j)=\pi(\eta)(j)=\pi(\xi)(j)=\xi(j)
, whence by item (i) we have that
\eta=\xi
.

For surjectivity, let
\eta\in\lim_{j\in J}\mathfrak{A}_{j}
. Pick some
j\in\dom(\eta)
. Now there is a unique
\xi\in\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with
\xi(j)=\eta(j)
. Let
i\in\dom(\eta)
. Letting
k\in J
be an upper bound of
\{i,j\}
, we have
\xi(k)=\mathcal{f}_{j,k}(\xi(j))=\mathcal{f}_{j,k}(\eta(j))=\eta(k)=\mathcal{f}_{i,k}(\eta(i))
, whence
i\in\dom(\xi)
. Hence
\dom(\eta)\subseteq\dom(\xi)
, and by uniqueness of all the values, this means that
\eta\subseteq\xi
. Then let
i\in\dom(\xi)\cap J
. Let again
k\in J
be an upper bound of
\{i,j\}
. Now
\mathcal{f}_{i,k}(\xi(i))=\xi(k)=\eta(k)
, so we have
i\in\dom(\eta)
. Hence
\dom(\eta)=\dom(\xi)\cap J
and for all
i\in\dom(\eta)
,
\xi(i)=\eta(i)
. Thus
\eta=\xi{\upharpoonright}(\dom(\xi)\cap J)=\pi(\xi)
.

Since the interpretations of symbols of
\tau
are defined via
\mathcal{g}^{I}_{i}
and
\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}
, the isomorphism condition of
\pi
will follow from
\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}=\hat{\pi}\circ\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}
. So we show this final fact. Let
X\in\dom(\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j})=\dom(\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j})
, and let
\vec{\eta}\in\hat{\pi}(\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X))=\pi[\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X)]
. Then
\vec{\eta}=\pi(\vec{\xi})
for some
\vec{\xi}\in\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X)
. Let
i\in\upset j\cap J\cap\bigcap_{k<n}\dom(\xi_{k})
be such that
(\xi_{0}(i),\dots,\xi_{n-1}(i))\in\mathcal{f}_{j,i}(X)
. Now
\eta_{k}(i)=\xi_{k}(i)
for all
k<n
, whence
\vec{\xi}\in\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}(X)
. Hence
\hat{\pi}(\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X))\subseteq\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}(X)
.

Then let
\vec{\eta}\in\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}(X)
and denote
\vec{\xi}=\pi(\vec{\eta})
. Now there is
i\in\upset j\cap J\cap\bigcap_{k<n}\dom(\eta_{k})
such that
(\eta_{0}(i),\dots,\eta_{n-1}(i))\in\mathcal{f}_{j,i}(X)
. As
i\in\dom(\xi_{k})
and
\eta_{k}(i)=\xi_{k}(i)
for all
k<n
, we have
\vec{\xi}\in\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X)
and so
\vec{\eta}\in\pi[\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X)]=\hat{\pi}(\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X))
. Hence
\mathcal{g}^{J}_{j}(X)\subseteq\hat{\pi}(\mathcal{g}^{I}_{j}(X))
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Each
\mathcal{g}_{i}
is a partial team isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
.

Proof.

Denote
\mathfrak{B}=\lim_{i}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
.
We prove
(iii)(PI2)
, the rest are similar although tedious.

Let
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{n}
. We show that
X
is a singleton if and only if
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
is. We consider the case
n=1
, the rest follows from
(iii)(PI3)
. Suppose
X=\{a\}
. Let
\eta,\xi\in\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
. Now there are
j\in\dom(\eta)
and
k\in\dom(\xi)
such that
j,k\geq i
,
\eta(j)\in\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)
and
\xi(k)\in\mathcal{f}_{i,k}(X)
. Let
l
be an upper bound of
\{j,k\}
. Now
\eta(l)=\mathcal{f}_{j,l}(\eta(j))\in\mathcal{f}_{j,l}(\mathcal{f}_{i,l}(X))=\mathcal{f}_{i,l}(X)
, and similarly
\xi(l)\in\mathcal{f}_{i,l}(X)
. As
\mathcal{f}_{i,l}
is a team embedding,
\mathcal{f}_{i,l}(X)
is a singleton, whence
\eta(l)=\xi(k)
. But then
\eta=\xi
. Hence
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
is a singleton.

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)=\{\eta\}
, let
j\in\dom(\eta)
be such that
j\geq i
and
\eta(j)\in\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)
. Now, if
a\in\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)
is such that
a\neq\eta(j)
, then the unique
\xi\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
\xi(j)=a
is different from
\eta
, but this is not possible, since then
\xi
would be an element of
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)
. Hence
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)
is a singleton, and as
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is a partial team isomorphism, so is
X
.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

If each
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is elementary, then so is each
\mathcal{g}_{i}
.

Proof.

Denote
\mathfrak{B}=\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, and let
\phi
be a formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
. We show by induction on
\phi
that for any
i\in I
and
X\in\dom(\mathcal{g}_{i})
of arity
|\Fv(\phi)|
,

\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)}\phi.

If
\phi
is atomic, this follows from the fact that
\mathcal{g}_{i}
is a partial team isomorphism, and the connective cases follow directly from the induction hypothesis. So suppose that
\phi=\exists^{1}v_{n}\psi(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
. If
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X}\phi
, then there is
a\in\mathfrak{A}_{i}
with
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X(a/n)}\psi
, whence the induction hypothesis gives
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{g}_{i}(X(a/n))}\psi
. Since
\mathcal{g}_{i}
is a team embedding, we have
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X(a/n))=\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)(\mathcal{g}_{i}(a)/n)
, whence
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)}\phi
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)}\phi
. Then there is some
\eta\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)(\eta/n)}\psi
. Let
j\in\dom(\eta)
be such that
j\geq i
. Then as
\mathcal{g}_{j}
is a team embedding, we have
\mathcal{g}_{i}(X)(\eta/n)=\mathcal{g}_{j}(\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X))(\mathcal{g}_{j}(\eta(j))/n)=\mathcal{g}_{j}(\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)(\eta(j)/n))
. Now by the induction hypothesis,
\mathfrak{A}_{j}\models_{\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)(\eta(j)/n)}\psi
, whence
\mathfrak{A}_{j}\models_{\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)}\phi
. Since
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is elementary, this means that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X}\phi
.

The case
\phi=\forall^{1}v_{n}\psi
is dual to the existential quantifier case.
∎

The previous results show that direct limits are well defined in the setting of team semantics. We conclude this section by pointing out how a directed limit comes naturally equipped with a specific set of relations, which we shall call admissible.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
be a directed system of
\tau
-structures and team embeddings, and let
\mathfrak{B}=\lim\mathcal{A}
.
We say that a relation
R\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
is
admissible
(in the system
\mathcal{A}
) if there is
i\in I
and
S\subseteq\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{n}
such that
R=\mathcal{g}_{i}(S)
.

Now, if
\mathfrak{B}=\lim\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{C}
is another structure with (elementary) team embeddings
\mathcal{h}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{C}
,
i\in I
, such that

\mathcal{h}_{i}=\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j},

then we may define a partial map
\mathcal{k}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that for any admissible
X\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
,
\mathcal{k}(X)=\mathcal{h}_{i}(X)
for any
i\in I
such that
X\in\ran(\mathcal{h}_{i})
, and this will satisfy

\mathcal{k}(\mathcal{g}_{i}(X))=\mathcal{h}_{i}(X)

for all admissible
X
. However, there is no obvious way to extend the map to non-admissible sets. Hence it is unclear whether it is possible for our direct limits to satisfy the smoothness property of the unions of chains axiom of AECs (which corresponds to the universal mapping property of direct limit in category theory). However, we shall solve this problem in
Section

4.4
by transitioning to work with something reminiscent of general models of second-order logic.

4.3.
The Löwenheim–Skolem Property

In the definition of an AEC, the Löwenheim–Skolem number
\LS(\mathcal{K})
of an AEC
\mathcal{K}
is the least
\kappa\geq|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
such that for any
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
, whenever
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
has power
\leq\kappa
, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
with power
\leq\kappa
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{B}\preceq\mathfrak{A}
. This is equivalent to the seemingly stronger claim that for every
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
, whenever
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
, then there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
with
X\subseteq\mathfrak{B}\preceq\mathfrak{A}
and
|\mathfrak{B}|\leq|X|+\kappa
. However, this seems to be too much to ask in our context, as even the weaker property where the set
X
of objects is required to be strictly smaller than
\kappa
has enormous consistency strength: we observe that the existence of this weaker notion of a Löwenheim–Skolem number, even in the model class of the empty theory, requires the existence of a supercompact cardinal. We show this using a theorem of
[
22
]
.

Definition 4.9
.

For a class
\mathcal{K}
of
\tau
-structures, denote by
\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K})
the least infinite cardinal
\kappa>|\tau|
such that the following holds and call it the weak Löwenheim–Skolem number of
\mathcal{K}
.
For any
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
with
|\mathcal{X}|<\kappa
, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
and an elementary team embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
|\mathfrak{B}|<\kappa
and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\ran(\iota)
.

We fix some notation. We denote by
\mathbf{V}
the universe of sets. We denote other models of set theory by boldface letters such as
\mathbf{M}
. If
j\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{M}
is an elementary embedding in the language of set theory, the critical point of
j
is the least ordinal
\alpha
such that
j(\alpha)>\alpha
.

Definition 4.10
.

A cardinal
\kappa
is supercompact if for every
\lambda\geq\kappa
there is a transitive model
\mathbf{M}
of set theory with
\mathbf{M}^{\lambda}\subseteq\mathbf{M}
, and an elementary embedding
j\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{M}
with critical point
\kappa
, such that
j(\kappa)>\lambda
.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
22
]
)
.

The first supercompact cardinal is the least
\kappa
such that the following holds: for any finite
\tau
, a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
with
|\mathfrak{A}|\geq\kappa
and a
\tau
-sentence
\phi
of universal second-order logic with
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
, there exists
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
with
|\mathfrak{B}|<|\mathfrak{A}|
and
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Suppose that for any finite signature
\tau
, if
\mathcal{K}_{\tau}
is the class of all
\tau
-structures, then
\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau})
exists. Then there exists a supercompact cardinal.

Proof.

Denote
\kappa=\sup_{|\tau|<\aleph_{0}}\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau})
. Here note that if
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are finite signatures and
f\colon\tau\to\tau^{\prime}
is a bijection such that
\ar(R)=\ar(f(R))
for all relation or function symbols
R\in\tau
, then
\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau})=\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau^{\prime}})
, so
\kappa
is well defined (the class of finite signatures is set-sized modulo renaming of symbols). By the definition of the weak LS-number, if
\tau
is finite and
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}_{\tau}
has power
\geq\kappa
, then, as
\mathfrak{A}
has power
\geq\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau})
, there exists
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}_{\tau}
of power
<\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K}_{\tau})\leq\kappa
and an elementary team embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
. Let
\mathfrak{C}=\iota(\mathfrak{B})
. Now
\mathfrak{C}
is a substructure of
\mathfrak{A}
that is isomorphic with
\mathfrak{B}
, and by
Proposition

3.12
, for all formulas
\phi
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
and relations
X
of
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathfrak{C}\models_{\iota(X)\cap\mathfrak{C}^{\ar(X)}}\phi\implies\mathfrak{B}\models_{X}\phi\implies\mathfrak{A}\models_{\iota(X)}\phi.

In particular, for every sentence
\phi
of
\mathsf{ESO}
, we have

\mathfrak{C}\models\phi\implies\mathfrak{A}\models\phi.

Taking the contrapositive, this means that for every sentence
\phi
of
\mathsf{ESO}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\neg\phi\implies\mathfrak{C}\models\neg\phi,

i.e. for every sentence
\phi
of universal second-order logic,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\implies\mathfrak{C}\models\phi.

But then
\kappa
is such that for every finite
\tau
, a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
with
|\mathfrak{A}|\geq\kappa
and a universal second-order
\tau
-sentence
\phi
with
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
, there exists
\mathfrak{C}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
with
|\mathfrak{C}|<|\mathfrak{A}|
and
\mathfrak{C}\models\phi
. Hence the class of cardinals with this property is nonempty. By Theorem
4.11
, the first cardinal of this class is supercompact.
∎

Certainly enough, a supercompact cardinal also suffices as our weak Löwenheim–Skolem number. The proof for said fact, which uses standard techniques involving supercompacts, also would suggest that the stronger form of the LS-property with non-strict inequalities would be too much to ask.

Theorem 4.13
.

Suppose
\kappa>|\tau|
is supercompact and
\mathcal{K}=\Mod(T)
for some first-order complete
\tau
-theory in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
. Then
\LS_{\mathrm{w}}(\mathcal{K})\leq\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{n})
,
|\mathcal{X}|<\kappa
. If
|\mathfrak{A}|\leq\kappa
, we are done, so assume that
|\mathfrak{A}|>\kappa
. We augment
\tau
into a signature
\tau^{\prime}
by adding fresh relation symbols for elements of
\mathcal{X}
. Then denote
\mu=|\mathfrak{A}|
and let
\lambda=2^{\mu}
. Note that
|\tau^{\prime}|\leq\kappa<\mu
. Let
j
be an elementary embedding of
\mathbf{V}
into a transitive model
\mathbf{M}
with critical point
\kappa
such that
\mathbf{M}^{\lambda}\subseteq\mathbf{M}
and
j(\kappa)>\lambda
. Note that as
\tau^{\prime}
has power
<\kappa
, we may assume that
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbf{V}_{\kappa}
and hence
j(\tau^{\prime})=\tau^{\prime}
. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be the
\tau^{\prime}
-structure with domain
j[\dom(\mathfrak{A})]\coloneqq\{j(a)\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}\}
and with the interpretation of symbols of
\tau^{\prime}
induced by
j
.
We make the following claims.

Claim
.

(i)

\mathfrak{B}\in\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}\models|\mathfrak{B}|\leq\lambda
.

(ii)

For any
R\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
, we have
R\in\mathbf{M}
and
R=j[S]\coloneqq\{j(\vec{a})\mid\vec{a}\in S\}
for some
S\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
.

(iii)

The map
X\mapsto j(j^{-1}[R])
,
R\in\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{B})
, is in
\mathbf{M}
.

Proof.

(i)

First notice that in
\mathbf{V}
we have
|\mathfrak{B}|=\mu\leq\lambda
, so there exists a surjection
f\colon\lambda\to\dom(\mathfrak{B})
. As
\dom(\mathfrak{B})\subseteq\mathbf{M}
and since
\mathbf{M}
is closed under
\lambda
-sequences, we have that
f\in\mathbf{M}
. Thus also
\dom(\mathfrak{B})\in\mathbf{M}
, and a similar argument works for
R^{\mathfrak{B}}
for any
R\in\tau^{\prime}
. As
|\tau^{\prime}|\leq\lambda
we obtain, again by closure under
\lambda
-sequences, that
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathbf{M}
. As being a surjection is absolute, we have
\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$f$ is a surjection $\lambda\to\dom(\mathfrak{B})$''}
, so
\mathbf{M}\models|\mathfrak{B}|\leq\lambda
.

(ii)

Since
\mathfrak{B}^{n}=j[\mathfrak{A}^{n}]
, we have for any
R\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
that
R=j[S]
for some
S\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
. Now
R\in\mathbf{M}
for the same reason that
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathbf{M}

(iii)

For the third claim, first notice that

|\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{B})|=\left|\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{B}^{n})\right|=\aleph_{0}\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{B}|}=2^{\mu}\leq\lambda.

Hence the map
R\mapsto j(j^{-1}[R])
also has cardinality
\leq\lambda
, and as it is a set of elements of
\mathbf{M}
, it is in itself in
\mathbf{M}
. ∎ Claim

Now, suppose for a moment that for any
\tau
-formula
\phi
of
\mathsf{FOT}
and any
S\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
for
n<\omega
, we have managed to prove that

(1)

\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[S]}\phi$''}\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{S}\phi.

By claim (ii) above, relations of the form
j[S]
exhaust all relations of
\mathfrak{B}
. Now for any
R\subseteq\mathfrak{B}^{n}
, by elementarity of
j
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{R}\phi$''}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{j^{-1}[R]}\phi

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{V}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{A}\models_{j^{-1}[R]}\phi$''}

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$j(\mathfrak{A})\models_{j(j^{-1}[R])}\phi$''},

By claim (iii) above, the map
R\mapsto j(j^{-1}[R])
is an element of
\mathbf{M}
.
Hence,

\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$R\mapsto j(j^{-1}[R])$ is an elementary team map $\mathfrak{B}\to j(\mathfrak{A})$''}.

Now, note that by claim (i) above, in
\mathbf{M}
we have
|\mathfrak{B}|\leq\lambda<j(\kappa)
. Thus,
\mathbf{M}
satisfies the statement “there is
\mathfrak{B}
and an elementary team embedding
\mathfrak{B}\to j(\mathfrak{A})
such that
|\mathfrak{B}|<j(\kappa)
”. By elementarity of
j
, we have (in
\mathbf{V}
) that there exists
\mathfrak{B}
and an elementary team embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
|\mathfrak{B}|<\kappa
. Additionally, each
X\in\mathcal{X}
is in the range of
\iota
, as
\iota
is, in particular, a partial team isomorphism in the signature
\tau^{\prime}
and
X
is the interpretation of
\underline{X}\in\tau^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{A}
. This is enough to prove the theorem.

Therefore, left to show is that (
1
) holds. We prove it by induction on the complexity of
\phi\in\mathsf{FOT}
.

(i)

Suppose first that
\phi=R(\vec{t})
for
R\in\tau^{\prime}\cup\{=\}
and
\vec{t}
a tuple of
\tau^{\prime}
-terms. Fix
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
with the free variables of
\phi
contained in
\{v_{i}\mid i\leq n\}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X]}\phi$''}

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\forall s\in j[X],\ s(\vec{t})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}$''}

\displaystyle\iff\forall s\in j[X],\ s(\vec{t})\in j[R^{\mathfrak{A}}]\text{ (absoluteness)}

\displaystyle\iff\forall s\in X,\ s(\vec{t})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}\text{ (elementarity)}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi.

Nothing changes if one considers
\phi=\neg R(\vec{t})
instead.

(ii)

Next suppose that
\phi=\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}
and again fix
X
. Now

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X]}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}$\,''}

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\forall s\in j[X]\ \exists s^{\prime}\in j[X]\ s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})$''}

\displaystyle\iff\forall s\in j[X]\ \exists s^{\prime}\in j[X]\ s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})\text{ (absoluteness)}

\displaystyle\iff\forall s\in X\ \exists s^{\prime}\in X\ s(\vec{x})=s^{\prime}(\vec{y})\text{ (elementarity)}

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\vec{x}\subseteq\vec{y}.

(iii)

The case of the constancy atom
\dep(\vec{x})
follows analogously to the previous one. The cases
\phi=\psi\land\theta
,
\phi=\psi\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\geqslant$}}\theta
and
\phi=\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\psi
follow immediately from the induction hypothesis.

(iv)

Next suppose that
\phi=\exists^{1}v_{n}\psi
and fix
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X]}\phi$''}

\displaystyle\iff\mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X](b/n)}\psi$ for some $b\in\mathfrak{B}$''}

\displaystyle\iff\exists a\in\mathfrak{A},\ \mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X](j(a)/n)}\psi$''}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\iff}}\exists a\in\mathfrak{A},\ \mathbf{M}\models\text{``$\mathfrak{B}\models_{j[X(a/n)]}\psi$''}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.h.}}}{{\iff}}\exists a\in\mathfrak{A},\ \mathfrak{A}\models_{X(a/n)}\psi

\displaystyle\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi.

The step
(*)
follows by the fact that

j[X(a/n)]=j[X](j(a)/n).

To see this, let
(b_{0},\dots,b_{n})\in j[X(a/n)]
. Then
b_{i}=j(a_{i})
for some
a_{i}\in X
,
i<n
,
b_{n}=j(a)
and
(b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1})=j(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in j[X]
, so

(b_{0},\dots,b_{n})=j(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})^{\frown}j(a)\in j[X](j(a)/x).

Conversely, we have that every
(b_{0},\dots,b_{n})\in j[X](j(a)/x)
is of the form
j(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1},a)
for
(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in X
.

(v)

The remaining case
\phi=\forall^{1}x\psi
is similar.

This completes the proof of (
1
) and thus of our theorem.
∎

Even while having a traditional Löwenheim–Skolem property in general seems hopeless, in the framework of
Section

4.4
, the following very weak formulation of the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem, following essentially from the LS theorem of first-order logic, seems to suffice.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then there is a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
of power
\leq|\mathcal{X}|+|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
and an (element-total) partial elementary team map
\iota\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{B}\models\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\ran(\iota)
.

Proof.

By the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem of
\mathsf{ESO}
, there is an elementary substructure
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
with
|\hat{\mathfrak{B}}|\leq|\tau(\mathcal{X})|+\aleph_{0}=|\mathcal{X}|+|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
. Now let
\mathfrak{B}=\hat{\mathfrak{B}}{\upharpoonright}\tau
. Then, by
Lemma

3.18
, the conditions
\mathcal{f}(\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}})=X
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{f}(b)=b
for all
b\in\mathfrak{B}
uniquely determine a partial elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
with
\ran(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\mathcal{X}\cup\{\{b\}\mid b\in\mathfrak{B}\})
.
∎

4.4.
A Category-theoretic Framework

We solve all of our AEC problems introduced in the previous sections by moving to work inside a framework where structures come with a predetermined set of relations of interest, much resembling general models, or Henkin models, of second-order logic. General models in the setting of team semantics were investigated in
[
8
]
, but our definition will be slightly different. We define our framework as a category and show that it is an example of an
accessible category

[
1
]
, which are known to generalize abstract elementary classes (see e.g.
[
3
]
).

Definition 4.15
.

(i)

Given a signature
\tau
, a
general
\tau
-structure
is a pair
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, where
\mathfrak{A}
is a
\tau
-structure and
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
is a set of relations such that
\cl(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{X}
and for every
a\in\mathfrak{A}
,
\{a\}\in\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{A}=((\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}),\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
is a directed system of general models and partial team isomorphisms
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}_{j}
such that
\dom(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})=\mathcal{X}_{i}
and
\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{j}
for all
i\leq j
, then the direct limit
\lim\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{A}
is the general model
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, where
\mathfrak{A}=\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{X}
is the collection of all admissible sets of
\mathfrak{A}
, i.e. all relations
R\in\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A})
such that there is
i\in I
with
R\in\ran(\mathcal{g}_{i})
, where
\mathcal{g}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}
,
i\in I
, are the direct limit maps. Like before, when the maps
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
are clear from the context, we simply write
\lim_{i\in I}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})
for the direct limit.

(iii)

An
abstract elementary team category
(AETC) is a category
\mathcal{C}

•

whose objects are general
\tau
-structures
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
for some fixed
\tau
,

•

morphisms
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
are partial team isomorphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{X}
and
\ran(\mathcal{f})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
, and

•

composition of morphisms is simply function composition,

satisfying the following conditions.

(a)

Closure under isomorphisms
: If
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{C}
and
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a
\tau
-isomorphism, then
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\in\mathcal{C}
for
\mathcal{Y}=\hat{\pi}[\mathcal{X}]\coloneqq\{\hat{\pi}(R)\mid R\in\mathcal{X}\}
and
\hat{\pi}{\upharpoonright}\mathcal{X}
is a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
.

(b)

Closure under inverses
: If
\mathcal{f}\colon(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
is a morphism such that
\ran(\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{Y}
, then
\mathcal{f}^{-1}
is a morphism
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\to(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
(and hence
\mathcal{f}
is an isormorphism in the category
\mathcal{C}
).

(c)

Coherence
: If
\mathcal{f}\colon(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{Y})
and
\mathcal{g}\colon(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\to(\mathfrak{C},\mathcal{Z})
are morphisms and
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a partial team isomorphism with
\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}
, then
\mathcal{h}
is a morphism
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
.

(d)

Direct limits of directed systems
:
\mathcal{C}
is closed under direct limits, i.e. if
((\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}),\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
is a directed system such that each
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and each
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is a morphism of
\mathcal{C}
, then
\lim_{i\in I}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{C}
, and the direct limit maps
\mathcal{g}_{i}
are morphisms
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})\to\lim_{j\in I}(\mathfrak{A}_{j},\mathcal{X}_{j})
. Furthermore, this direct limit is a colimit of the category
\mathcal{C}
and hence satisfies the universal property of colimits (
smoothess
).

(e)

Löwenheim–Skolem property
: There is a cardinal
\kappa
such that for any
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, there is
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\in\mathcal{C}
with
|\mathfrak{B}|\leq|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|+\kappa
and
|\mathcal{Y}|\leq|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|+\aleph_{0}
, and a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\to(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
such that
\cl(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{f})
. The least such
\kappa
will be denoted by
\LS(\mathcal{C})
.

The identity morphism
\id
of an object
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
is such that
\dom(\id)=\ran(\id)=\mathcal{X}
. Therefore the requirement that
\cl(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{X}
is necessary.

Observe that in an AETC, an automorphism of an object
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
is a partial team isomorphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\ran(\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{X}
. We denote by
\Aut(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
the set of all automorphisms of
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
. If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, we denote by
\Aut((\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})/\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
the set of all
\mathcal{f}\in\Aut(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
such that
\mathcal{f}(X)=X
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.

If
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
are general structures, we say that
\mathcal{f}
is a partial team map
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
if
\dom(\mathcal{f})\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
\ran(\mathcal{f})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
.

Next we recall some preliminary notions from category theory.

Definition 4.16
.

(i)

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal. An object
X
in some category
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-presentable
if the functor
\mathrm{Hom}(X,-)
preserves all
\kappa
-directed colimits, i.e. for every
\kappa
-directed system
(Y_{i},f_{i,j})_{i\in I,i\leq j}
of objects and morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
, for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
and morphism
h\colon X\to\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}Y_{i}
there is
i\in I
and a morphism
h_{i}\colon X\to Y_{i}
such that
h=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
, where
g_{i}\colon Y_{i}\to\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}Y_{i}
are the colimit morphisms.

(ii)

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal. A category
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-accessible
if

(a)

\mathcal{C}
has
\kappa
-directed colimits, and

(b)

there is a set
\mathcal{A}
of
\kappa
-presentable objects such that every object in
\mathcal{C}
is (isomorphic to) a
\kappa
-directed colimits of objects from
\mathcal{A}
.

(iii)

A category is
accessible
if it is
\kappa
accessible for some regular cardinal
\kappa
.

There are many examples of accessible categories which are relevant to model theory. Among the most natural ones are the following
[
13
, Def. 2.2]
.

Example 4.17
.

(i)

Let
T
be a complete first-order theory. The category
\Mod(T)
of all models of
T
with elementary embeddings is accessible.

(ii)

Given a complete first-order theory
T
, let
\Sub(T)
be the category of pairs
(\mathfrak{A},X)
, where
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}\models T
, and morphisms
(\mathfrak{A},X)\to(\mathfrak{B},Y)
are partial elementary maps
f\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\dom(f)=X
and
\ran(f)\subseteq Y
. Then
\Sub(T)
is also an accessible category.

Theorem 4.18
.

Abstract elementary team categories are accessible.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an abstract elementary team category and
\kappa=\LS(\mathcal{C})^{+}
. We show that
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-accessible. Since
\mathcal{C}
has colimits, in particular it has
\kappa
-directed colimits.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
whose objects are all
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{C}
with
|\mathfrak{A}|,|\mathcal{X}|<\kappa
. We claim that objects of
\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
are
\kappa
-presentable. Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
and consider the colimit
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
of a
\kappa
-directed system
((\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i}),\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
, where we denote by
\mathcal{g}_{i}\colon(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
the colimit morphisms. We claim that we can factor any morphism
\mathcal{h}\colon(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
through some
(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})
. First, notice that since
|\mathcal{X}|<\kappa
and
\kappa
is regular, the fact that
I
is
\kappa
-directed entails that there is some
i\in I
such that
\mathcal{h}(X)\in\ran(\mathcal{g}_{i})
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
. Define a partial team isomorphism
\mathcal{h}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}_{i}
by letting

\mathcal{h}_{i}(X)=\mathcal{g}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{h}(X))

for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
. Notice that
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})
and
\ran(h_{i})\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{g}_{i})=\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i,i})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{i}
. Thus
\mathcal{h}=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h}_{i}
, so by coherence,
\mathcal{h}_{i}
is a morphism
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})
. Clearly, such factorization is unique. Now by e.g. taking in only pairs
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, where the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
is a cardinal, we can make
\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
a set.

Left is to show that every object in
\mathcal{C}
can be obtained as a
\kappa
-directed colimit of objects of
\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
. Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
(\mathcal{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
list all
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
of power
<\kappa
such that
\cl(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{Y}
. By letting
i\leq j
whenever
\mathcal{X}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{j}
, we make
(I,\leq)
a directed set. Since
\kappa
is regular,
(I,\leq)
is also
\kappa
-directed.

Now, by the Löwenheim–Skolem property, for each
i\in I
, we obtain a general model
(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})
with
|\mathfrak{B}_{i}|\leq|\mathcal{X}_{i}|+\LS(\mathcal{C})<\kappa
and a morphism
\iota_{i}\colon(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})\to(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
such that
\ran(\iota_{i})=\mathcal{X}_{i}
. As both
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
have power
<\kappa
, we have
(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{<\kappa}
.

Now, given
i,j\in I
with
i\leq j
, we define a partial team isomorphism
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{i}\to\mathfrak{B}_{j}
by letting, for every
X\in\mathcal{Y}_{i}
,

\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X)=\iota_{j}^{-1}(\iota_{i}(X)),

which is well defined since for
i\leq j
we have
\mathcal{X}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{j}
. Additionally, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\dom(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})\quad\text{ and}\quad\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i,j})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{j}.

Now clearly
\iota_{i}(X)=\iota_{j}(\mathcal{f}_{i,j}(X))
for all
X\in\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, so by coherence
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
is a morphism
(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})\to(\mathfrak{B}_{j},\mathcal{Y}_{j})
. It follows that
((\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}),\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
forms a
\kappa
-directed system, so we can consider its colimit
(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
. Let
\mathcal{g}_{i}\colon(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
be the colimit maps and notice that, by construction, we have

\iota_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j}=\iota_{i}

for every
i\leq j
. Therefore, by the universal property of colimit, there is a unique morphism
\mathcal{u}\colon(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})\to(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
such that, for all
i\in I
,

\mathcal{u}\circ\mathcal{g}_{i}=\iota_{i}.

Now, for every
X\in\mathcal{X}
, there is by construction some
i\in I
such that
X\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
. Then
X=\iota_{i}(Y)
for some
Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, so
\mathcal{u}(\mathcal{g}_{i}(Y))=X
. This shows that
\mathcal{u}
is surjective with respect to
\mathcal{X}
and so it is an isomorphism in the category
\mathcal{C}
. Finally, this shows
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
can be obtained as a colimit of
\kappa
-presentable objects.
∎

Definition 4.19
.

Let
T
be a first-order complete theory in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
. We denote by
\GMod(T)
the category whose objects are general models of
T
and morphisms
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\to(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{Y})
are elementary team maps
\mathcal{f}
with
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{X}
and
\ran(\mathcal{f})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
.

Corollary 4.20
.

\GMod(T)
is an abstract elementary team category.

Proof.

Closure under isomorphisms and inverses are clear. Coherence follows from
Proposition

4.3
. By
Proposition

4.7
,
\GMod(T)
has direct limits and since the limit object
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
of a directed system is such that
\mathcal{X}
contains only admissible sets, we have the universal property of colimits (note that all singletons are admissible). Finally, by
Proposition

4.14
,
\LS(\GMod(T)),\LS(\GSubMod(T))\leq|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
.
∎

As we remarked before, the setting of accessible categories is known to generalize the notion of abstract elementary class. The above results thus show that our setting of general models and (elementary) team maps fits this categorical framework to study the model theory of independence logic and existential second-order logic. Additionally, we remark a further connection with another categorification of AECs, namely
abstract elementary categories (AECat)
, which were introduced by Kirby and Kamsma
[
16
,
13
]
. These are pairs of accessible categories with some further structure, the protypical example of which are
(\Sub(T),\Mod(T))
and
(\Mod(T),\Mod(T))
.

Definition 4.21

(
[
13
]
)
.

An abstract elementary category (AECat) is a pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
of accessible categories such that

(i)

\mathcal{M}
is a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
,

(ii)

all morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
are mono, and

(iii)

\mathcal{M}
has directed colimits, which are preserved by the inclusion functor
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{C}
.

Corollary 4.22
.

The pair
(\GMod(T),\GMod(T))
is an AECat.

We also remark that one could introduce in a category
\GSubMod(T)
analogously to
\Sub(T)
, but we shall not pursue this in the present paper, as we required general models of an AETC to contain all singleton relations.

5.
Galois Types and the Monster Model

In this section we continue the study of the model-theoretic properties of AETCs and, in particular, of the model categories of first-order complete theories in
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
(or
\mathsf{ESO}
). For notational convenience, when given a general structure
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
, we often omit the set
\mathcal{X}
from the notation and denote it by
\rel(\mathfrak{A})
, i.e. if we say that
\mathfrak{A}
is a general structure, we mean the structure
(\mathfrak{A},\rel(\mathfrak{A}))
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC.

(i)

We say that
\mathcal{K}
has
arbitrarily large models
(ALM) if for every cardinal
\kappa
there is
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\mathfrak{A}|\geq\kappa
.

(ii)

We say that
\mathcal{K}
has the
joint embedding property
(JEP) if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
, there is
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
.

(iii)

We say that
\mathcal{K}
has the
amalgamation property
(AP) if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
, there is
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{h}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
such that
\mathcal{h}_{0}\circ\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{h}_{1}\circ\mathcal{g}
.

Remark
.

Notice that if
\mathcal{K}
has AP and contains a prime model
\mathfrak{A}
, i.e. for all
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
there is a morphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathcal{K}
also has JEP.

We start by showing some sufficient conditions for an AETC to satisfy the above versions of ALM, JEP and AP.

Definition 5.2
.

(i)

Given general structures
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})
,
i\in I
, and an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
I
, the ultraproduct
\prod_{i\in I}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i})/\mathcal{U}
of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is the general structure
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
such that
\mathfrak{A}=\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{X}=\{\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}\mid\text{$X_{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}$ for all $i\in I$}\}
.

(ii)

We say that an AETC
\mathcal{K}
is
closed under ultrapowers
if for all
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
, for all infinite cardinals
\kappa
and for all ultrafilters
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
,
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{K}
and the ultrapower embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
is a morphism of
\mathcal{K}
. We say that
\mathcal{K}
is
closed under ultraproducts
if for all infinite
\kappa
, for all
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
,
i<\kappa
, and for all ultrafilters
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
,
\prod_{i<\kappa}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{K}
.

(iii)

We say that an AETC
\mathcal{K}
is first-order complete if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
we have
\mathfrak{A}\equiv\mathfrak{B}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC.

(i)

If
\mathcal{K}
has infinite models and is closed under ultrapowers, then
\mathcal{K}
has ALM.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{K}
is first-order complete and closed under ultrapowers, then
\mathcal{K}
has JEP.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{K}
is closed under ultrapowers and morphisms of
\mathcal{K}
are elementary team maps, then
\mathcal{K}
has AP.

Proof.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\kappa
be infinite. By a theorem of
[
7
]
, there is an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
such that
|\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}|\geq 2^{\kappa}>\kappa
. By closure under ultrapowers,
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{K}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
. As
\mathcal{K}
is first-order complete,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are elementarily equivalent. By the Keisler–Shelah theorem, there is a cardinal
\kappa
and an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\cong\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Let
\pi
be an isomorphism
\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
, and denote
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Now
\pi
lifts to a team isomorphism
\hat{\pi}\colon\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathfrak{C}
in the obvious way. Since
\mathcal{K}
is closed under ultrapowers, we have
\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U},\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{K}
and the ultrapower embeddings
\iota_{\mathfrak{A}}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
and
\iota_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
are morphisms. Hence
\hat{\pi}\circ\iota_{B}
is also a morphism. Thus may take
\mathcal{f}=\iota_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\mathcal{g}=\hat{\pi}\circ\iota_{\mathfrak{B}}
as our desired joint embeddings, which finishes the proof.

(iii)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
,
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}
be as in the definition of AP. Let
\mathcal{X}=\rel(\mathfrak{A})
and let
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}
be the
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-expansions of
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}
, respectively, by interpreting
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}=\mathcal{f}(X)
and
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{C}}}=\mathcal{g}(X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
. We first show that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\equiv\hat{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Suppose that this is not the case. Then there is a first-order
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-sentence
\phi
such that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\phi
and
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}\models\neg\phi
. Let
R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1}
enumerate all
R\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\underline{R}
occurs in
\phi
. Without loss of generality, each
R_{i}
is nonempty (otherwise replace the symbol
\underline{R}_{i}
in
\phi
by
\bot
whenever
R_{i}=\emptyset
). Then, coding the relation symbols
\underline{R}_{0},\dots\underline{R}_{n-1}
into one
\sum_{i<n}\ar(R_{i})
-ary relation symbol
S
as in the proof of
Proposition

3.10
, we obtain a
\tau\cup\{S\}
-sentence
\psi
such that
(\mathfrak{B},\prod_{i<n}R_{i}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}})\models\psi
and
(\mathfrak{C},\prod_{i<n}R_{i}^{\hat{\mathfrak{C}}})\models\neg\psi
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is closed under Cartesian products, we have
\prod_{i<n}R_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
, and by
(iii)(PI3)
we additionally obtain

\mathcal{f}\left(\prod_{i<n}R_{i}\right)=\prod_{i<n}\mathcal{f}(R_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{g}\left(\prod_{i<n}\mathcal{R}_{i}\right)=\prod_{i<n}\mathcal{g}(R_{i}).

Then, let
\chi
be the
\mathsf{FOT}
-translation of
\psi
. Now
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(\prod_{i<n}R_{i})}\chi
and
\mathfrak{C}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{\mathcal{g}(\prod_{i<n}R_{i})}\chi
. As
\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}
are elementary team maps, it follows that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{\prod_{i<n}R_{i}}\chi
but
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{\prod_{i<n}R_{i}}\chi
, which is a contradiction.

Next, since
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\equiv\hat{\mathfrak{C}}
, it follows by Keisler–Shelah that the structures have isomorphic ultrapowers
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{C}}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
. Let
\pi
be an isomorphism
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\to\hat{\mathfrak{C}}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
, denote
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}=\hat{\mathfrak{C}}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
and let
\mathfrak{D}=\hat{\mathfrak{D}}{\upharpoonright}\tau
. As
\mathcal{K}
is closed under ultrapowers,
\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U},\mathfrak{C}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{K}
and the ultrapower embeddings
\iota_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}
and
\iota_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
are morphisms. Let
\mathcal{h}_{0}=\hat{\pi}\circ\iota_{B}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}=\iota_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Since
\mathcal{h}_{0}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}
are elementary team maps even in the signature
\tau(\mathcal{X})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{h}_{0}(\mathcal{f}(R))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{h}_{0}(\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}})=\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}=\mathcal{h}_{1}(\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{C}}})=\mathcal{h}_{1}(\mathcal{g}(R))

for all
R\in\mathcal{X}
. Hence
\mathfrak{D}
with
\mathcal{h}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
suffices as an amalgam of
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{C}
over
\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

Note that the amalgamation property holds in a tiny bit stronger form in the category
\GMod(T)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{K}=\GMod(T)
for some first-order complete theory
T
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
. Then for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and partial elementary team maps
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
, there is
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{h}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
such that
\mathcal{h}_{0}(\mathcal{f}(X))=\mathcal{h}_{1}(\mathcal{g}(X))
for all
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})\cap\dom(\mathcal{g})
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially same as that of
Proposition

5.3
.
∎

Amalgamation is a fundamental property in the context of abstract elementary classes as it grants a good behaviour to so-called Galois types. Here we define Galois types of relations and show that also in our context they induce a natural equivalence relation.

Definition 5.5

(Galois Types)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC.

(i)

We define a relation
\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}
on the class of all pairs
(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})
, where
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\vec{X}=(X_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})^{\alpha}
for some ordinal
\alpha
, as follows. If
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\vec{X}=(X_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})^{\alpha}
and
\vec{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i<\beta}\in\rel(\mathfrak{B})^{\beta}
, then

(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\mathfrak{B},\vec{Y})

if
\alpha=\beta
,
\ar(X_{i})=\ar(Y_{i})
for all
i<\alpha
and there are
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{h}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{h}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that
\mathcal{h}_{0}(X_{i})=\mathcal{h}_{1}(Y_{i})
for all
i<\alpha
.

(ii)

Given
(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})
, we denote the class of all
(\mathfrak{B},\vec{Y})
such that
(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})\equiv_{g}(\mathfrak{B},\vec{Y})
by
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})
and call it the
Galois type
of
\vec{X}
(over
\emptyset
, in
\mathfrak{A}
).

The following lemma shows that the notion of Galois type is well defined and, in the presence of AP, having the same type is an equivalence relation.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}
has AP. Then

(i)

\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}
is an equivalence relation, and

(ii)

for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\vec{X}\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})^{\alpha}
and morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, we have

\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\gtype(\mathcal{f}(\vec{X})/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B}).

Proof.

(i)

Reflexivity and symmetry are clear, so we verify that transitivity holds. Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\vec{X}=(X_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})^{\alpha}
,
\vec{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in\rel(\mathfrak{B})^{\alpha}
and
\vec{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in\rel(\mathfrak{C})^{\alpha}
and suppose that

(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\mathfrak{B},\vec{Y})\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathfrak{B},\vec{Y})\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\mathfrak{C},\vec{Z}).

Then there are
\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{E}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{h}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{D}
,
\mathcal{h}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{D}
,
\mathcal{h}_{2}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{E}
and
\mathcal{h}_{3}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{E}
such that
\mathcal{h}_{0}(X_{i})=\mathcal{h}_{1}(Y_{i})
and
\mathcal{h}_{2}(Y_{i})=\mathcal{h}_{3}(Z_{i})
for all
i<\alpha
. Apply AP to the morphisms
h_{1}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{h}_{2}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{E}
to obtain
\mathfrak{F}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{k}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathcal{k}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{E}\to\mathfrak{F}
such that
\mathcal{k}_{0}\circ\mathcal{h}_{1}=\mathcal{k}_{1}\circ\mathcal{h}_{2}
. It follows that for all
i<\alpha
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{k}_{0}\circ\mathcal{h}_{0})(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}_{0}(\mathcal{h}_{0}(X_{i}))=\mathcal{k}_{0}(\mathcal{h}_{1}(Y_{i}))=(\mathcal{k}_{1}\circ\mathcal{h}_{2})(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}_{1}(\mathcal{h}_{2}(Y_{i}))=\mathcal{k}_{1}(\mathcal{h}_{3}(Z_{i}))=(\mathcal{k}_{1}\circ\mathcal{h}_{3})(Z_{i}),

which shows that
(\mathfrak{A},\vec{X})\equiv_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathcal{K}}(\mathfrak{C},\vec{Z})
.

(ii)

Trivial.
∎

We have defined Galois types for arbitrary ordinal-length sequences. The next proposition shows that, when we restrict to finite tuples, we can show that
\mathsf{FOT}
actually provides an alternative, syntactic way to define types in
\GMod(T)
.

Definition 5.7

(Syntactic Types)
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a general
\tau
-structure,
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\rel(\mathfrak{A})
with
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{X}
and
n>0
a natural number.

(i)

An
\mathsf{FOT}
-type over
\mathcal{X}
in variables
\vec{x}^{0},\dots,\vec{x}^{n-1}
, where each
\vec{x}^{i}
is a finite tuple of variables and
\vec{x}^{i}
and
\vec{x}^{j}
do not share variables for
i\neq j
, is a nonempty set of
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-formulas of
\mathsf{FOT}
with free variables among
\vec{x}^{0},\dots,\vec{x}^{n-1}
.

(ii)

A type
p(\vec{x}^{0},\dots,\vec{x}^{n-1})
over
\mathcal{X}
is consistent if there is a general
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
and a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models_{\prod_{i<n}Y_{i}}\phi
for all
\phi\in p
for some
Y_{0},\dots,Y_{n-1}\in\rel(\mathfrak{B})\setminus\{\emptyset\}
, where
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
is the
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-extension of
\mathfrak{B}
with
\underline{X}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}=\mathcal{f}(X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
. We say that the tuple
\vec{Y}=(Y_{0},\dots,Y_{n-1})
realizes
p
.

(iii)

A type
p
is complete if for all
\tau
-formulas
\phi
with the proper free variables, we have either
\phi\in p
or
\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\in p
.

(iv)

We denote by
\fottype(\vec{Y}/\mathcal{X};\mathfrak{A})
the unique complete type realized by
\vec{Y}\in(\rel(\mathfrak{A})\setminus\{\emptyset\})^{n}
. In particular,

\fottype((X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1})/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\{\phi\in\mathsf{FOT}\mid\mathfrak{A}\models_{\prod_{i<n}X_{i}}\phi\}.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}
is closed under ultrapowers and morphisms are elementary. Let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\vec{X}=(X_{i})_{i<n}\in(\rel(\mathfrak{A})\setminus\{\emptyset\})^{n}
and
\vec{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i<n}\in(\rel(\mathfrak{B})\setminus\{\emptyset\})^{n}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
.

(ii)

\fottype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\fottype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
.

Proof.

First suppose that
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
. Then there is
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that
\mathcal{f}(X_{i})=\mathcal{g}(Y_{i})
for all
i<n
. Then
\mathcal{f}(\prod_{i<n}X_{i})=\mathcal{g}(\prod_{i<n}Y_{i})
, which entails
\fottype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\fottype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
by elementarity of the morphisms.

Then suppose that
\fottype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\fottype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
. Then in particular the structures
(\mathfrak{A},\prod_{i<n}X_{i})
and
(\mathfrak{B},\prod_{i<n}Y_{i})
are elementary equivalent by
Lemma

2.8
. By applying Keisler–Shelah exactly as in
Proposition

5.3
, we find
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that
\mathcal{f}(X_{i})=\mathcal{g}(Y_{i})
for all
i<n
. Hence
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{A})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
.
∎

Remark
.

For the
aficionados
of topology, we allow ourselves a short excursion in this otherwise very concise article. In fact, we would like to remark that, in the case where
\mathcal{K}=\GMod(T)
, we can provide a natural Stone space topology to the the set of types. In fact, since they have a syntactical representation as
\mathsf{FOT}
-types by
Proposition

5.8
, we can use formulas to define a clopen basis. Write
G(\mathfrak{A})
for the set of all Galois types consistent with
\mathfrak{A}
, i.e. all
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
, where
\vec{X}\in(\rel(\mathfrak{B})\setminus\{\emptyset\})^{<\omega}
for some
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
such that there is a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
.
We provide a topology to
G(\mathfrak{A})
by taking as basic opens the sets induced by formulas of
\mathsf{FOT}
:

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket:=\{\gtype((X_{i})_{i<n}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})\mid\mathfrak{B}\models_{\prod_{i<n}X_{i}}\phi\text{ and there is }\mathcal{f}:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}\}.

We verify that the resulting topological space is a Stone space, namely that it is totally disconnected, Hausdorff and compact.

(i)

G(\mathfrak{A})
is totally disconnected.
In fact if a set
Z\subseteq G(\mathfrak{A})
contains two distinct types
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
and
\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{C})
, then by
Proposition

5.8
there is a formula
\phi\in\mathsf{FOT}
such that
Z\cap\llbracket\phi\rrbracket
and
Z\cap\llbracket\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\rrbracket
split
Z
into two nonempty opens.

(ii)

G(\mathfrak{A})
is Hausdorff.
If
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})\neq\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{C})
then again by
Proposition

5.8
we can find a
\mathsf{FOT}
-formula
\phi
such that
\mathfrak{B}\models_{\prod_{i<n}X_{i}}\phi
but
\mathfrak{C}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{\prod_{i<n}Y_{i}}\phi
.

(iii)

G(\mathfrak{A})
is compact.
Without loss of generality an open cover is of the form
\{\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket\mid i\in I\}
, thus
\bigcap_{i\in I}\llbracket\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi_{i}\rrbracket=\emptyset
and in particular
\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi_{i}\mid i\in I\}\models\bot
. Since
\mathsf{FOT}
is compact, we obtain that
\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi_{i}\mid i\in I_{0}\}\models\bot
for a finite set
I_{0}\subseteq I
. This is equivalent to say that
\{\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket\mid i\in I_{0}\}
is an open cover, showing that the topology is compact.

Now let
S(\mathfrak{A})
be the subset of
G(\mathfrak{A})
whose types are determined by tuples, i.e. they are of the form
\gtype(\{\vec{a}\}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
. Then
S(\mathfrak{A})
with the induced subspace topology is a Stone subspace of
G(\mathfrak{A})
and, additionally, it is exactly (up to homeomorphism) the standard Stone space of first-order types. Interestingly, the above inclusion induces via Stone’s duality a homomorphism from the algebra of
\mathsf{FOT}
-formulas to the algebra of
\mathsf{FO}
-formulas. This homomorphism is given by the quotient map that sends every
\mathsf{FOT}
-formula to the
\mathsf{FO}
-formulas it is equivalent to when evaluated in singleton teams. Finally, we also remark that, using amalgamation, it is easy to verify that the Stone space
G(\mathfrak{A})
does not depend on
\mathfrak{A}
but only on its first-order theory. Thus we can meaningfully speak of the Stone space of Galois types (or
\mathsf{FOT}
-types) of relations of any theory which is first-order complete

We now work towards building a version of the monster model in our setting. We notice this follows the general idea of the usual construction of the monster model in AECs. However, we remark that one can construct monster-like objects in any accessible category which is closed under colimits and has the amalgamation property – we refer the reader to
[
25
, Thm.1]
and to
[
18
]
for an extensive discussion of this result.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC and
\kappa
an infinite cardinal.

(i)

We say that
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
is
\kappa
-universal
if for every
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\rel(\mathfrak{B})|<\kappa
, there is a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
.

(ii)

We say that
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
is
\kappa
-model-homogeneous
if for all
\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\rel(\mathfrak{B})|=|\rel(\mathfrak{C})|<\kappa
, for all morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{A}
and all
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphisms
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
, there is
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{A})
with
\pi\circ\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}
, i.e. the below diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Then suppose further that
\mathcal{K}=\GMod(T)
for a first-order complete theory
T
of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
.

(iii)

We say that
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
is
strongly
\kappa
-homogeneous
if for any partial elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}
with
|\dom(\mathcal{f})|<\kappa
, there is
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{A})
with
\pi\supseteq\mathcal{f}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}
has JEP. Then for every cardinal
\kappa
there exists a
\kappa
-universal structure
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
,
i<\mu
, list all models of
\mathcal{K}
of power
<\kappa
up to isomorphism. We define a chain
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i<j\leq\mu}
of models
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}_{j}
recursively as follows.

(i)

For
i=0
, we let
\mathfrak{A}_{i}=\mathfrak{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{f}_{0,0}=\id
.

(ii)

If
i=j+1
, then we let
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
be a structure into which
\mathfrak{A}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
joint embed. We let
\mathcal{f}_{j,i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{j}\to\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{g}_{j}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{j}\to\mathfrak{A}_{i}
be the respective joint embeddings. For
l<j
, we let
\mathcal{f}_{l,i}=\mathcal{f}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{f}_{l,j}
.

(iii)

If
i
is limit, then we let
\mathfrak{A}_{i}=\lim_{j<i}\mathfrak{A}_{j}
and let
\mathcal{f}_{j,i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{j}\to\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, for
j<i
be the direct limit morphisms.

Now consider any model
\mathfrak{B}
of size
\leq\kappa
. Then
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{i}
for some
i<\mu
. By construction,
\mathcal{f}_{i+1,\mu}\circ\mathcal{g}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}_{\mu}
is a morphism, which shows that
\mathfrak{A}\coloneqq\mathfrak{A}_{\mu}
is
\kappa
-universal.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC with AP and
\kappa
an infinite cardinal. Then for any
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
and a morphism
\iota\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that for any
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{K}
of power
<\kappa
, morphisms
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{A}
, and a
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphism
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, there is a morphism
\mathcal{k}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that
