\iota\circ\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}
, i.e. the below diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}=\GMod(T)
for first-order complete
T
. Then for any
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
and a morphism
\iota\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
such that for any partial elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{A}
there is a morphism
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\iota(\mathcal{f}(X))=\mathcal{g}(X)
for all
X\in\dom(\mathcal{f})
.

Proof.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
,
i<\mu_{0}
, list all
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
of power
<\kappa
such that there is a morphism
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{A}
, up to
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphism. For each
i<\mu_{0}
, let
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
,
j<\mu_{1}
, list all morphisms
\mathfrak{C}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}
. Then let
\mu=\mu_{0}\cdot\mu_{1}
and fix a bijection
\theta\colon\mu\to\mu_{0}\times\mu_{1}\times\mu_{1}
. Denote by
\theta_{i}
for
i\leq 2
the functions such that
\theta(\alpha)=(\theta_{0}(\alpha),\theta_{1}(\alpha),\theta_{2}(\alpha))
for all
\alpha<\mu
.

We define by recursion a directed system
(\mathfrak{B}_{i},\mathcal{i}_{i,j})_{i\leq j\leq\mu}
of objects and morphisms of
\mathcal{K}
. We begin by letting
\mathfrak{B}_{0}=\mathfrak{A}
. At a limit
i
, we let
\mathfrak{B}_{i}=\lim_{j<i}\mathfrak{B}_{j}
and let
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}
be the direct limit morphisms. When
i=j+1
, we amalgamate the morphisms
\mathcal{i}_{0,j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(j),\theta_{1}(j)}\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\theta_{0}(j)}\to\mathfrak{B}_{j}
and
\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(j),\theta_{2}(j)}\colon\mathfrak{C}_{\theta_{0}(j)}\to\mathfrak{A}
to obtain
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{k}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{j}\to\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{k}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{D}
with

\mathcal{k}_{0}\circ\mathcal{i}_{0,j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(j),\theta_{1}(j)}=\mathcal{k}_{1}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(j),\theta_{2}(j)}.

We let
\mathfrak{B}_{i}=\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}=\mathcal{k}_{0}
and define the other
\mathcal{i}_{k,i}
in the obvious way via composition.

Finally, we let
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{\mu}
and
\iota=\mathcal{i}_{0,\mu}
. Now, let
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{K}
have power
<\kappa
,
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
be a
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphism, and
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{A}
be morphisms. Then there is
i<\mu_{0}
and an isomorphism
\pi\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}\circ\pi
and
\mathcal{f}\circ\pi
are morphisms
\mathfrak{C}_{i}\to\mathfrak{A}
, there are
j,k<\mu_{1}
such that
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}=\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}\circ\pi
and
\mathcal{f}_{i,k}=\mathcal{f}\circ\pi
. Let
\alpha<\mu
be such that
\theta(\alpha)=(i,j,k)
. By construction, there is
\mathcal{k}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}_{\alpha+1}
such that

\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,\alpha+1}\circ\mathcal{i}_{0,\alpha}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(\alpha),\theta_{1}(\alpha)}=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(\alpha),\theta_{2}(\alpha)}.

Then

\displaystyle\iota\circ\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{0,\mu}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\circ\pi^{-1}=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha+1,\mu}\circ\mathcal{i}_{0,\alpha+1}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,j}\circ\pi^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha+1,\mu}\circ(\mathcal{i}_{0,\alpha}\circ\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,\alpha+1}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(\alpha),\theta_{1}(\alpha)})\circ\pi^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\theta_{0}(\alpha),\theta_{2}(\alpha)}=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i,k}\circ\pi^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f},

as desired.

(ii)

Similar. ∎

Theorem 5.12
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC with JEP and AP. Then for every cardinal
\kappa>\LS(\mathcal{K})
there is a
\kappa
-universal,
\kappa
-model-homogeneous
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{K}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}=\GMod(T)
for first-order complete
T
. Then there is a
\kappa
-universal, strongly
\kappa
-homogeneous
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem in the case that
\mathcal{K}
is an AETC with JEP and AP. The other case is similar. We define a directed system
(\mathfrak{M}_{i},\mathcal{i}_{i,j})_{i\leq j\leq\kappa^{+}}
as follows.

(i)

\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is a
\kappa
-universal structure whose existence is guaranteed by
Proposition

5.10
.

(ii)

We let
\mathfrak{M}_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{i}_{i,i+1}
be the structure
\mathfrak{B}
and the morphism
\iota
given by
Lemma

5.11
for
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{M}_{i}
. All the other maps
\mathcal{i}_{k,i+1}
are defined via composition.

(iii)

For
i
limit, we let
\mathfrak{M}_{i}=\lim_{j<i}\mathfrak{M}_{j}
and
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}
be the direct limit embeddings.

We show that
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{M}_{\kappa^{+}}
is as wanted.

For
\kappa
-universality, let
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
have cardinality
<\kappa
. As
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is
\kappa
-universal, we can find a morphism
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{M}_{0}
. Now,
\mathcal{i}_{0,\kappa^{+}}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\to\mathfrak{M}
. Hence
\iota\coloneqq\mathcal{i}_{0,\kappa^{+}}\circ\mathcal{h}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{M}
.

For
\kappa
-model-homogeneity, let
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
be such that
|\mathfrak{A}|,|\mathfrak{B}|<\kappa
and
|\rel(\mathfrak{A})|=|\rel(\mathfrak{B})|<\kappa
, let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{M}
be morphisms, and let
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be a
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphism. Now by the definition of the direct limit,
\rel(\mathfrak{M})=\bigcup_{i<\kappa^{+}}\ran(\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}})
. Since
|\rel(\mathfrak{A})|=|\rel(\mathfrak{B})|<\kappa
and
\kappa^{+}
is regular, there is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
such that
\mathcal{f}[\rel(\mathfrak{A})]\cup\mathcal{g}[\rel(\mathfrak{B})]\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,\kappa^{+}})
. For all
i\geq\alpha
, let
\mathcal{f}_{i}=\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}_{i}=\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{g}
. By coherence,
\mathcal{f}_{i}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{g}_{i}
a morphism
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
. Note that
\mathcal{f}_{\kappa^{+}}=\mathcal{f}
and
\mathcal{g}_{\kappa^{+}}=\mathcal{g}
. We now define, by recursion on
i\geq\alpha
, partial team isomorphisms
\mathcal{h}_{i}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{i}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
such that whenever
j\leq i
, we have
\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i})\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})
and
\mathcal{h}_{i}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h}
.

(i)

Suppose
i=\alpha
. We let
\mathcal{h}_{i}=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}^{-1}
. Then

\mathcal{h}_{i}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}=(\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}^{-1})\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h},

as desired.

(ii)

Suppose
i=j+1
and
i
is odd. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}=\mathcal{g}_{j}\circ\mathcal{h}
, whence
\mathcal{g}_{j}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}
is a
\mathcal{K}
-isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
. By the choice of
\mathfrak{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}
, there is a morphism
\mathcal{k}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{j}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
such that

\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{g}_{j}\circ(\mathcal{g}_{j}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j})=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j},

i.e.
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}
.
We let
\mathcal{h}_{i}=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1}
. Now notice that

\displaystyle\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i})

\displaystyle=\ran(\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f})=\ran(\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{i}_{j,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f})

\displaystyle\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{i}_{j,i})=\dom(\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1})=\dom(\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i}).

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{h}_{i}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1})\circ(\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{k}\circ(\mathcal{i}_{j,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f})=\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{h}_{j}\circ\mathcal{f}_{j}=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{g}_{j}\circ\mathcal{h}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ(\mathcal{i}_{j,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{g})\circ\mathcal{h}=(\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{g})\circ\mathcal{h}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h},

as desired.

(iii)

If
i=j+1
is even, then similarly to above, we find a morphism
\mathcal{k}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{j}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
such that
\mathcal{k}\circ\mathcal{g}_{j}=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{h}_{j}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{g}_{j}
and let
\mathcal{h}_{i}=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}\circ\mathcal{k}^{-1}
.

(iv)

If
i
is a limit, we let
\mathcal{h}_{i}
be the partial team isomorphism
\mathfrak{M}_{i}\to\mathfrak{M}_{i}
with
\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})=\bigcup_{\alpha\leq j<i}\{X\in\rel(\mathfrak{M}_{i})\mid\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1}(X)\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{j})\}
such that

\mathcal{h}_{i}(X)=\mathcal{i}_{j,i}(\mathcal{h}_{j}(\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1}(X)))

for the least
j
such that
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1}(X)\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{j})
. It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{h}_{i}
is a partial isomorphism. Now let
X\in\ran(\mathcal{f}_{i})
. Then
X=\mathcal{f}_{i}(Y)=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}(\mathcal{f}_{\alpha}(Y))
for some
Y\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})
. By the induction hypothesis
\ran(\mathcal{f}_{\alpha})\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{h}_{\alpha})
, so
\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}^{-1}(X)=\mathcal{f}_{\alpha}(Y)\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{\alpha})
. By the definition of
\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})
, we have
X\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})
.

Let
X\in\rel(\mathfrak{A})
. By the above,
\alpha
is the least
j<i
such that
\mathcal{i}_{j,i}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{j}(X))\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{j})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{h}_{i}(\mathcal{f}_{i}(X))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}(\mathcal{h}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{i}(X))))=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}(\mathcal{h}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{f}_{\alpha}(X)))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{i}_{\alpha,i}(\mathcal{g}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{h}(X)))=\mathcal{g}_{i}(\mathcal{h}(X)).

Thus
\mathcal{h}_{i}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}=\mathcal{g}_{i}\circ\mathcal{h}
.

Finally, we let
\pi=\mathcal{h}_{\kappa^{+}}
. We show that
\dom(\pi)=\ran(\pi)=\rel(\mathfrak{M})
.
By definition,

\dom(\pi)=\bigcup_{\alpha\leq i<\kappa^{+}}\{X\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})\mid i_{i,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X)\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{i})\}.

Let
X\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})
. Let
\gamma
be the least even
i\in[\alpha,\kappa^{+})
such that
X\in\ran(\mathcal{i}_{i,\kappa^{+}})
. Let
\mathcal{k}
be the morphism
\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma}\to\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma+1}
from the construction of
\mathcal{h}_{\gamma+1}
. As
\dom(\mathcal{k})=\rel(\mathfrak{M}_{\gamma})
, we have
\mathcal{i}_{\gamma,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X)\in\dom(\mathcal{k})
. Then

\mathcal{k}(\mathcal{i}_{\gamma,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X))=\mathcal{k}(\mathcal{i}_{\gamma,\gamma+1}^{-1}(\mathcal{i}_{\gamma+1,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X)))=\mathcal{h}_{\gamma+1}(\mathcal{i}_{\gamma+1,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X)),

whence
\mathcal{i}_{\gamma+1,\kappa^{+}}^{-1}(X)\in\dom(\mathcal{h}_{\gamma+1})
. But this means that
X\in\dom(\pi)
. Using a symmetric argument, and odd
\gamma
, one can show that
\pi
is surjective. Moreover, by construction,

\pi\circ\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{h}_{\kappa^{+}}\circ\mathcal{f}_{\kappa^{+}}=\mathcal{g}_{\kappa^{+}}\circ\mathcal{h}=\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{h}.

Finally, since
\mathfrak{M}
is a general model and contains all singleton relations, we have that
\pi
is element-total and element-surjective. Therefore, by
Proposition

3.13
there is a
\tau
-automorphism
\pi^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}\supseteq\pi
. Hence
\pi
(
=\hat{\pi}^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}\rel(\mathfrak{M})
) is an automorphism of the category
\mathcal{K}
by closure under isomorphisms.
∎

The above
\kappa
-monster model
\mathfrak{M}
is
\kappa
-saturated in the following sense.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an AETC with JEP and AP, and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\kappa
-universal and
\kappa
-homogeneous model of
\mathcal{K}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{B}
is a morphism. Then for any
\alpha,\beta<\kappa
,
\vec{Y}\in\rel(\mathfrak{B})^{\alpha}
and
\vec{D}\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})^{\beta}
, there is
\vec{X}\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})^{\alpha}
such that

\gtype(\vec{X}\vec{D}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}\mathcal{g}(\vec{D})/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B}).

Proof.

Let
\mu=|\alpha|+|\beta|<\kappa
, and denote
\vec{B}=\mathcal{g}(\vec{D})
. By the Löwenheim–Skolem property, there is
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\mathfrak{A}|\leq\mu+\LS(\mathcal{K})<\kappa
and
|\rel(\mathfrak{A})|\leq\mu+\aleph_{0}<\kappa
and a morphism
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\vec{D}\in\ran(\mathcal{f})^{\beta}
. Now, let
\vec{A}=\mathcal{f}^{-1}(\vec{D})
.

Again, by the Löwenheim–Skolem property, there is
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\mathfrak{C}|\leq\mu+\LS(\mathcal{K})<\kappa
and
|\rel(\mathfrak{C})|\leq\mu+\aleph_{0}<\kappa
, and a morphism
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{B}
, such that

\cl(\ran(\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f})\cup\{Y_{i}\mid i<\alpha\})\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{h}).

Let
\vec{Z}=\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\vec{Y})
and
\vec{C}=\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\vec{B})
, and denote
\mathcal{k}=\mathcal{h}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f}
. Now
\mathcal{g}\circ\mathcal{f}=\mathcal{h}\circ\mathcal{k}
, so by coherence
\mathcal{k}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
. By
\kappa
-universality of
\mathfrak{M}
, there is a morphism
\mathcal{i}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{M}
. By
\kappa
-model-homogeneity, there is
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
such that
\pi\circ\mathcal{f}=(\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k})\circ\id_{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}
. Now, let
\vec{X}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{i}(\vec{Z}))
. Notice that
\mathcal{k}(\vec{A})=\vec{C}
, so

\displaystyle\vec{D}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}(\pi(\mathcal{f}(\vec{A})))=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{k}(\vec{A})))=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{i}(\vec{C})).

Now, as
\pi^{-1}\circ\mathcal{i}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{M}
and
(\pi^{-1}\circ\mathcal{i})(\vec{Z}\vec{C})=\vec{X}\vec{D}
, it follows that
\gtype(\vec{X}\vec{D}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Z}\vec{C}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{C})
. On the other hand, as
\mathcal{h}
is a morphism
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathcal{h}(\vec{Z}\vec{C})=\vec{Y}\vec{B}
, we have
\gtype(\vec{Z}\vec{C}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{C})=\gtype(\vec{Y}\vec{B}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
. By transitivity of Galois types, we then have
\gtype(\vec{X}\vec{D}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}\vec{B}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{B})
, as desired.
∎

Like classically, the monster model makes it easier to deal with Galois types: instead of amalgamating into a third model, having the same type can be now witnessed by an automorphism of the monster.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
\alpha<\kappa
and
\vec{X},\vec{Y}\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})^{\alpha}
. Then
\gtype(\vec{X}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})
if and only if there is
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
such that
\pi(\vec{X})=\vec{Y}
.

Proof.

If
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
are conjugates by
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
, then the morphisms
\id_{\mathfrak{M}}
and
\pi
witness that
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
have the same type.

Suppose that
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
have the same type. Then there is
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{K}
and morphisms
\mathcal{f}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{f}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathcal{f}_{0}(\vec{X})=\mathcal{f}_{1}(\vec{Y})
. By the Löwenheim–Skolem property, there is
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\mathfrak{B}|,|\rel(\mathfrak{B})|<\kappa
and a morphism
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\vec{X},\vec{Y}\in\ran(\mathcal{g})^{\alpha}
. There is also
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathcal{K}
with
|\mathfrak{C}|,|\rel(\mathfrak{C})|<\kappa
and a morphism
\mathcal{h}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{A}
such that

\cl(\ran(\mathcal{f}_{0}\circ\mathcal{g})\cup\ran(\mathcal{f}_{1}\circ\mathcal{g}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{h}).

Now by coherence, the maps
\mathcal{k}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{h}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{f}_{i}\circ\mathcal{g}
,
i<2
, are morphisms
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
. By
\kappa
-universality of
\mathfrak{M}
, there is a morphism
\mathcal{i}\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{M}
. Now
\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{0}
,
\mathcal{g}
and
\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{1}
are morphisms
\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{M}
, so by
\kappa
-model-homogeneity, there are
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
such that
\pi_{0}\circ\mathcal{g}=(\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{0})\circ\id_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{0}
and
\pi_{1}\circ(\mathcal{i}\circ\mathcal{k}_{1})=\mathcal{g}\circ\id_{\mathfrak{B}}=\mathcal{g}
. Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}
. Then
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
and

\displaystyle\pi(\vec{X})

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(\pi_{0}(\vec{X}))=\pi_{1}(\pi_{0}(\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{X}))))=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{k}_{0}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{X}))))

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{0}(\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{X}))))))=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{0}(\vec{X}))))

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{1}(\vec{Y}))))=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{h}^{-1}(\mathcal{f}_{1}(\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{Y}))))))

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{i}(\mathcal{k}_{1}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{Y}))))=\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{g}^{-1}(\vec{Y}))

\displaystyle=\vec{Y}.\qed

In particular, the previous lemma allows one to extend the notion of Galois type and take parameters into account. For a small set
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\rel(\mathfrak{M})
and given two sequences
\vec{X},\vec{Y}\in\rel(\mathfrak{M})^{\alpha}
, we can define

\gtype(\vec{X}/\mathcal{Z};\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\mathcal{Z};\mathfrak{M})

if there is
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/\mathcal{Z})
such that
\pi(\vec{X})=\vec{Y}
. One can then verify that
\gtype(\vec{X}/\mathcal{Z};\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}/\mathcal{Z};\mathfrak{M})
if and only if
\gtype(\vec{X}\vec{Z}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})=\gtype(\vec{Y}\vec{Z}/\emptyset;\mathfrak{M})
, where
\vec{Z}
enumerates
\mathcal{Z}
.

Remark
.

To recap, in this section we introduced a notion of Galois type of a tuple of relations in an abstract elementary team category and built a monster model which is sufficiently saturated with respect to these types. However, we shall now see why this notion of type is not, in fact, a good basis for the definition of stability. Instead, it would seem that in the concrete example
\GMod(T)
for a complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory
T
(cf.
Section

7.2
), the stability properties of the first-order reduct of
T
are a more useful measure of complexity.

More precisely, to see that Galois types in team semantics are ill-behaved from the stability-theoretic point of view, we consider the formula
\phi\coloneqq x\subseteq y
in the context of an arbitrary
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory
T
with infinite models. We show that this formula has both a version of the
indipendence property
(IP) and the
strict order property
(SOP) in our context (see
[
30
, p.134]
for the standard definition of IP and SOP in first-order logic).

First, consider any model
\mathfrak{A}
of
T
and let
A=\{a_{i}\mid i<\omega\}
be an enumeration of some countable subset thereof. Then for every
I\subseteq\omega
consider the set of formulas

\displaystyle\Gamma_{I}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\subseteq x\mid i\in I\}\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}(y_{i}\subseteq x)\mid i\notin I\}.

Then consider a team
X_{I}
with domain
\dom(X_{I})=\{x\}\cup\{y_{i}\mid i<\omega\}
and such that
X_{I}[x]=\{a_{i}\mid i\in I\}
and
X_{I}[y_{i}]=\{a_{i}\}
for all
i<\omega
. Then we obtain
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X_{I}}\Gamma_{I}
, showing that any theory
T
with infinite models satisfies this version of IP.

Similarly, we can also show that any theory
T
with infinite model has the strict order property. Let again
\mathfrak{A}
be an arbitrary infinite model and
A=\{a_{i}\mid i<\omega\}
a countable subset of it. For every
i<\omega
, we let
A_{i}=\{a_{j}\mid i\leq j\}
. Consider the formula
\phi\coloneqq x\subseteq y
and let
X_{i}
be the team with domain
\{x_{i}\}
such that
X_{i}[x_{i}]=A_{i}
. Then it is easy to verify that, for all
i<j<\omega

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X_{i}\times X_{j}}\forall y\;(y\subseteq x_{i}\rightarrowtriangle y\subseteq x_{j})\iff i\leq j.

Which shows that any theory
T
with infinite models has this version of SOP for team semantics.

The two observations above hint at the fact that a robust classification theory for (complete) theories in
\mathsf{ESO}
(or
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
) cannot rely exclusively on the notion of Galois types introduced in this section. In particular, we obtain as a consequence that for any set of size
\kappa
of parameters the number of resulting Galois types is
2^{\kappa}
, showing that the cardinality of the space of types does not allow to track any information about the underlying theory.

For these reasons, we take later in
Section

7.2
a different approach. In particular, given a complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory
T
, we shall consider the (standard) stability properties of its first-order reduct
T^{*}
. We shall apply this approach especially to the problem of transferring categoricity among different cardinals, and prove two results in this direction. We take these preliminary findings as a hint that the standard notions of stability and independence also have a key role in the present higher-order context, and may be the correct ones to study the model theory of complete theories in
\mathsf{ESO}
or
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
.

6.
Application I – Lindström’s Theorem for
\mathsf{FOT}

A celebrated result by
[
20
]
characterises first-order logic as the maximal abstract logic satisfying both the compactness and the downwards Löwenheim-Skolem theorems. Moreover, he also proved in
[
19
]
that first-order logic is maximal among the abstract logics which are both compact and have the so-called Tarski union property. A similar result by
[
26
]
shows that first-order logic is also the maximal abstract logic to satisfy the Łoś’ theorem.

Motivated by these results, we provide a first application of the abstract machinery of the previous sections and prove two maximality results for
\mathsf{FOT}
in terms of its model-theoretic properties. We believe our results could also be obtained from the classical Lindström’s theorem via
Lemma

2.7
with a clever translation, but here we merely demonstrate the use of our newly acquired tools. We start by defining a suitable notion of abstract logic in the setting of team semantics, which we call
abstract team logic
. Given a signature
\tau
, we denote by
\Str(\tau)
the class of all
\tau
-structures.

In this section, we make a distinction between teams and their underlying relations, to allow teams with infinite domains. However, results about team maps are only applied in the case where the argument of the map is a finitary relation.

Definition 6.1
.

An
abstract team logic
is a pair
L=(\mathrm{Fml}_{L},\mathrm{Sat}_{L})
, where

•

\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
is a class of pairs
\phi=(i,\Fv(\phi))
such that
i
is an identifier and
\Fv(\phi)
a set of variables, and

•

\mathrm{Sat}_{L}
is a class of triples
(\mathfrak{A},X,\phi)
, where
\phi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
,
\mathfrak{A}
is a structure (of some signature) and
X
is a team of
\mathfrak{A}
with
\dom(X)\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
,

satisfying the following.

(i)

L
is
closed under isomorphisms
, i.e. if
\pi\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
is an isomorphism between
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, then for all
\phi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
,
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
,

(\mathfrak{A},X,\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L}\iff(\mathfrak{B},\pi^{*}(X),\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L},

where
\pi^{*}(X)=\{\pi\circ s\mid s\in X\}
.

(ii)

L
satisfies the
occurence condition
, i.e. for any
\phi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
there is a signature
\sigma
such that for any
\tau
and
\mathfrak{A}\in\Str(\tau)
,
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
implies
\tau\supseteq\sigma
, and for any
\tau\supseteq\sigma
,
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
,
\mathfrak{A}\in\Str(\tau)
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
,

(\mathfrak{A},X,\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L}\iff(\mathfrak{A}{\upharpoonright}\sigma,X,\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L}.

(iii)

L
is
closed under renaming
, i.e. if
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are signatures and
\pi\colon\tau\to\tau^{\prime}
is a bijection that preserves the type and the arity of symbols, then for any
\phi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
there is
\psi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
with
\Fv(\psi)=\Fv(\phi)
such that for all
D
,
\mathfrak{A}\in\Str(\tau)
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
, we have

(\mathfrak{A},X,\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L}\iff(\pi(\mathfrak{A}),X,\psi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L},

where
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
is the
\tau^{\prime}
-structure with
\dom(\pi(\mathfrak{A}))=\dom(\mathfrak{A})
and
\pi(R)^{\pi(\mathfrak{A})}=R^{\mathfrak{A}}
for any symbol
R\in\tau
.

If
\phi\in\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
, we simply write
\phi\in L
. We say that
\phi
is a
\sigma
-formula if
\sigma
is as in the occurrence condition. If
(\mathfrak{A},X,\phi)\in\mathrm{Sat}_{L}
, we write
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
. If
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
, we write
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma
if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
for all
\phi\in\Sigma
. We write
\Mod_{L}(\phi)
for the class of all
(\mathfrak{A},X)
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
and
X\neq\emptyset
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is a
\tau
-structure, we also denote by
\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)
the set of all
\tau
-formulas
\phi\in L
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
. We write
\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A})
for
\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},\{\emptyset\})
. Note that if
\phi\in\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)
, then
\Fv(\phi)\subseteq\dom(X)
. We write
(\mathfrak{A},X)\equiv_{L}(\mathfrak{B},Y)
if
\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)=\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{B},Y)
. We say that a set
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
is
consistent
if
\bigcap_{\phi\in\Sigma}\Mod_{L}(\phi)\neq\emptyset
. We say that
\phi
is consistent if
\{\phi\}
is, and
\phi
is consistent with
\Sigma
if
\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}
is consistent. We define the following properties of abstract team logics.

Definition 6.2
.

We say that an abstract team logic
L
is
regular
if the following hold.

(i)

L
is
closed under conjunction
, i.e. for all
\phi,\psi\in L
there is
\chi\in L
such that for all
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi),\Fv(\psi),\Fv(\chi)
, and for all
\mathfrak{A}
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\chi\iff\text{$\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi$ and $\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi$}.

Furthermore, if
\phi
and
\psi
are
\sigma
-formulas, also
\chi
can be chosen to be a
\sigma
-formula. We denote this
\chi
by
\phi\land\psi
.

(ii)

L
is
closed under (weak classical) negation
, i.e. for all
\phi\in L
there is
\psi\in L
such that for all
D
,
\mathfrak{A}
and
nonempty

X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\psi\iff\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\phi.

Furthermore, if
\phi
is a
\sigma
-formula, also
\psi
can be chosen to be a
\sigma
-formula. We denote this
\psi
by
\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
.

We say that
L
is
positive
if it is closed under conjunction but not necessarily under weak classical negation.

We say that
L
is
team-finitary
if

(iii)

for any
\phi\in L
there is a finite set
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
such that if there are
\mathfrak{A}
and nonempty
X
with
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
, then there is a team
Y
such that
\dom(Y)=D
, and for all teams
Z
such that
Z{\upharpoonright}D=Y
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{Z}\phi
. In particular,
\Fv(\phi)
is finite.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
L
be an abstract team logic.

(i)

We say that
L
is
compact
if for any
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
,
\Sigma
is consistent if and only if every finite subset of
\Sigma
is.

(ii)

We say that a team embedding
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
preserves
\phi\in L
if for all
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

We say that
L
has
direct limits
if the following holds. Let
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathcal{f}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,i\leq j}
be a directed system of
\tau
-structures, let
\mathfrak{B}=\lim_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and denote for all
i\in I
by
\mathcal{g}_{i}
the direct limit embedding
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\mathfrak{B}
. Then for all
\phi\in L
, if
\mathcal{f}_{i,j}
preserves
\phi
for all
i,j\in I
,
i\leq j
, then
\mathcal{g}_{i}
preserves
\phi
for all
i\in I
. This is the team logic counterpart of the Tarski union property.

(iii)

We say that
L
has a
Łoś’ theorem
if for any set
I
,
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, teams
X_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{D}
and ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
I
, for any formula
\phi\in L
:

\{i\in I\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X_{i}}\phi\}\in\mathcal{U}\implies\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}}\phi.

We say that
L
has a
strong Łoś’ theorem
if the above holds in both directions:

\{i\in I\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models_{X_{i}}\phi\}\in\mathcal{U}\iff\prod_{i\in I}\mathfrak{A}_{i}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}/\mathcal{U}}\phi.

We define a partial order among abstract team logics as follows.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
L,L^{\prime}
be two abstract team logics. We write
L\leq L^{\prime}
if for any
\tau
-formula
\phi\in L
there is a
\tau
-formula
\phi^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}
such that
\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime}
, i.e. for all
\mathfrak{A}\in\Str(\tau)
and all teams
X
of
\mathfrak{A}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi^{\prime}.

We write
L\equiv L^{\prime}
if
L\leq L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime}\leq L
.

If
\phi,\psi\in L
and
\Mod_{L}(\phi)\subseteq\Mod_{L}(\psi)
, then we write
\phi\models\psi
. We write
\phi\models\psi
even if
\phi\in L
,
\psi\in L^{\prime}
and
\Mod_{L}(\phi)\subseteq\Mod_{L^{\prime}}(\psi)
. We write
\phi\equiv\psi
if
\phi\models\psi
and
\psi\models\phi
. Note that if
\phi
and
\psi
are consistent and
\phi\equiv\psi
, we must have
\Sig(\phi)=\Sig(\psi)
. If
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Fml}_{L}
and
\phi\in L^{\prime}
, we write
\Sigma\models\phi
if
\bigcap_{\psi\in\Sigma}\Mod_{L}(\psi)\subseteq\Mod_{L^{\prime}}(\phi)
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
L
be a regular abstract team logic.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a
\tau
-structure and
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
nonempty. Then
\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)
is complete with respect to
\tau
-formulas of
L
whose free variables are contained in
D
, i.e. if
\phi\in L
is a
\tau
-formula and
\Fv(\phi)\subseteq D
, then either
\phi\in\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)
or
\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\in\Th_{L}(\mathfrak{A},X)
.

(ii)

For any
\phi\in L
,
\phi\equiv\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
.

(iii)

Let
\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}\subseteq L
. Then
\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}
is inconsistent if and only if
\Sigma\models\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
.

Proof.

(i)

Since
X
is nonempty, for any
\phi\in L
we have

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\iff\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\phi.

Clearly exactly one of the options
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\phi
and
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
is true.

(ii)

Trivial.

(iii)

Suppose that
\Sigma\models\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
. Then for any
\mathfrak{A}
and
X
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
. This means that for such
\mathfrak{A}
and
X
, whenever
X
is nonempty,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\phi
, which means that there are no
\mathfrak{A}
and nonempty
X
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}
. Hence
\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}
is inconsistent.

On the other hand, if
\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}
is inconsistent, then
\bigcap_{\psi\in\Sigma}\Mod_{L}(\psi)\cap\Mod_{L}(\phi)=\bigcap_{\psi\in\Sigma\cup\{\phi\}}\Mod_{L}(\psi)=\emptyset
. Hence for any
\mathfrak{A}
and nonempty
X
, if
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\phi
. But this means that
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
. Hence
\Sigma\models\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
. ∎

We can now prove two characterisations of
\mathsf{FOT}
in abstract terms. We notice that the fist one characterises
\mathsf{FOT}
in the context of abstract team logics and adapts
[
19
]
in our context. The second result applies to all positive abstract team logic and adapts
[
26
]
.

Theorem 6.6
.

(i)

The logic
\mathsf{FOT}
is maximal among all regular abstract team logics that are team-finitary, compact and closed under direct limits.

(ii)

The logic
\mathsf{FOT}
is maximal among all positive abstract team logics that are team-finitary and have a strong Łoś’ theorem.

Proof.

(i)

Suppose this is not the case. Then there is a regular abstract team logic
L
such that
\mathsf{FOT}\leq L\nleq\mathsf{FOT}
and
L
is team-finitary, compact and closed under direct limits. Let
\phi\in L
be such that
\phi\not\equiv\psi
for all
\psi\in\mathsf{FOT}
. By the occurrence condition, there is
\tau
such that
\phi
is a
\tau
-formula, and by team-finitarity there is a finite
D\supseteq\Fv(\phi)
such that whenever
\phi
is satisfied in a team, it is satisfied in a team with domain
D
. Let
\Sigma
be the set of all
\tau
-formulas
\psi\in\mathsf{FOT}
such that
\phi\models\psi
and
\Fv(\psi)\subseteq D
. We show that
\Sigma\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\}
is consistent. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
be finite. Now if
\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\}
were inconsistent, then we would have
\Sigma^{\prime}\models\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
, whence
\bigwedge\Sigma^{\prime}\models\phi
. As
\Sigma
is clearly closed under conjunction, we would also have
\bigwedge\Sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, whence
\phi\models\bigwedge\Sigma^{\prime}
. But then
\phi\equiv\bigwedge\Sigma^{\prime}
, contradicting the choice of
\phi
. Hence we conclude that
\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\}
is consistent. Thus by compactness of
L
,
\Sigma\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\}
is consistent. Thus there is a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and nonempty
X\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{D}
with
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma\cup\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{\{a\}\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}\}\cup\{X[\vec{x}]\}
, where
\vec{x}
lists
D
. Now we consider the set
\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\cup\{\phi,\theta(\underline{X[\vec{x}]},\vec{x})\}
, where
\theta
is as in
Lemma

2.9
, and show that it is consistent. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})
be finite. Let
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
enumerate such
a\in\mathfrak{A}
that
\underline{\{a\}}
occurs in a formula of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Now there is a first-order
\tau
-formula
\psi(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1},R)
, where
R
is a second-order variable with arity
|D|
, such that for any
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
and its
\tau\cup\{\underline{\{a_{0}\}},\dots,\underline{\{a_{n-1}\}},\underline{X}\}
-expansion
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
, we have

\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\bigwedge\Sigma^{\prime}\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\psi(b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1},\underline{X[\vec{x}]}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}),

where
b_{i}
is the unique element inhabiting
\underline{\{a_{i}\}}^{\hat{\mathfrak{B}}}
.
Let
\psi^{*}(\vec{x})
be an
\mathsf{FOT}
-translation of
\exists y_{0}\dots\exists y_{n-1}\psi(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1},R)
, i.e. such a
\tau
-formula of
\mathsf{FOT}
that for any
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{B}^{D}
,

\mathfrak{B}\models\exists y_{0}\dots\exists y_{n-1}\psi(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1},Y[\vec{x}])\iff\mathfrak{B}\models_{Y}\psi^{*}.

Now suppose for a contradiction that
\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\phi,\theta(\underline{X[\vec{x}]},\vec{x})\}
is inconsistent. Then so is
\{\psi^{*},\phi\}
. This means that
\phi\models\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\psi^{*}
, whence
\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\psi^{*}\in\Sigma
. As
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\Sigma
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\psi^{*}
. Since
X\neq\emptyset
, we obtain
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X}\psi^{*}
. Thus
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\exists y_{0}\dots\exists y_{n-1}\psi(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1},X[\vec{x}])
, which is a contradiction since
\mathfrak{A}\models\psi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1},X[\vec{x}])
. Therefore
\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\phi,\theta(\underline{X[\vec{x}]},\vec{x})\}
is consistent and, by compactness of
L
, so is
\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\cup\{\phi,\theta(\underline{X[\vec{x}]},\vec{x})\}
. Thus there is a
\tau(\mathcal{X})
-structure
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
such that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{X})\cup\{\phi,\theta(\underline{X[\vec{x}]},\vec{x})\}
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}=\hat{\mathfrak{B}}{\upharpoonright}\tau
. Then by
Lemma

3.18
, there is partial elementary team map
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
with
\dom(\mathcal{f})=\cl(\mathcal{X})
. Now notice that
\mathfrak{A}
,
\mathfrak{B}
,
X
and
\mathcal{f}
are such that

\mathfrak{A}\models_{X}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{B}\models_{\mathcal{f}(X)}\phi.

Now we define, by recursion on
n<\omega
,
\tau
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, sets
\mathcal{X}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{A}_{n})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
, teams
X_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
Y_{n}\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}
, and partial elementary team maps
\mathcal{i}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{n}\to\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{j}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{n}\to\mathfrak{B}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{f}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{n}\to\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{g}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{n}\to\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}
, satisfying the following.

(a)

\mathcal{X}_{n}=\{X_{n}\}\cup\{\{a\}\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}\}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{n}=\{Y_{n}\}\cup\{\{b\}\mid b\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}
.

(b)

\mathfrak{A}_{n}\models_{X_{n}}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}_{n}\models_{Y_{n}}\phi
.

(c)

\dom(\mathcal{i}_{n})=\dom(\mathcal{f}_{n})=\cl(\mathcal{X}_{n})
and
\dom(\mathcal{j}_{n})=\dom(\mathcal{g}_{n})=\cl(\mathcal{Y}_{n})
.

(d)

\ran(\mathcal{i}_{n})\cup\ran(\mathcal{g}_{n})\subseteq\cl(\mathcal{X}_{n+1})
and
\ran(\mathcal{j}_{n})\cup\ran(\mathcal{f}_{n})\subseteq\cl(\mathcal{Y}_{n+1})
.

(e)

Y_{n}=\mathcal{f}_{n}(X_{n})
,
Y_{n+1}=\mathcal{j}_{n}(Y_{n})
and
X_{n+1}=\mathcal{i}_{n}(X_{n})=\mathcal{g}_{n}(Y_{n})
.

(f)

The resulting diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{A}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{i}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{i}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{i}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{j}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{j}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{f}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}_{2}}

We start by letting
\mathfrak{A}_{0}\coloneqq\mathfrak{A}
,
X_{0}\coloneqq X
,
\mathfrak{B}_{0}\coloneqq\mathfrak{B}
and
Y_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{f}(X)
. Then we let
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\mathcal{X}=\{X_{0}\}\cup\{\{a\}\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}_{0}\}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{0}=\{Y_{0}\}\cup\{\{b\}\mid b\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}\})
, and
\mathcal{f}_{0}=\mathcal{f}
. These are all clearly as desired.

Suppose we have constructed
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
,
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
and
\mathcal{f}_{i}
for
i\leq n
and
\mathcal{i}_{i}
,
\mathcal{j}_{i}
and
\mathcal{g}_{i}
for
i<n
. We now construct
\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{f}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{i}_{n}
,
\mathcal{j}_{n}
and
\mathcal{g}_{n}
. Let
\Gamma
be the set

\hskip 30.00005pt\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi,\theta(\underline{X_{n}},\vec{x})\}\cup\Diag(\mathfrak{A}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{n})\cup\Diag(\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})\cup\{\text{``$\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(R)}=\underline{R}$''}\mid R\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\},

where “
S=S^{\prime}
” is a shorthand for
\forall^{1}\vec{w}(S(\vec{w})\leftrightarrowtriangle S^{\prime}(\vec{w}))
.
We use compactness of
L
to show that
\Gamma
is consistent. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathfrak{A}_{n}\models_{X_{n}}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
, and clearly
\mathfrak{A}_{n}\models_{X_{n}}\{\theta(\underline{X_{n}},\vec{x})\}\cup\Diag(\mathfrak{A}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{n})
. Now, look at a finite
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Diag(\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})
. Then
\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}
is equivalent to a first-order
\tau(\mathcal{Y}_{n})
-sentence of the form
\psi(\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(\{a_{0}\})},\dots,\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(\{a_{k-1}\})},\underline{\{b_{0}\}},\dots,\underline{\{b_{m-1}\}},\underline{Y_{n}})
for some
a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}
and
b_{0},\dots,b_{m-1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
such that
\{b_{i}\}\notin\ran(\mathcal{f}_{n})
for all
i<m
. Now

\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{B}_{n}\models\exists y_{0}\dots\exists y_{k-1}\psi(\mathcal{f}_{n}(a_{0}),\dots,\mathcal{f}_{n}(a_{k-1}),y_{0},\dots,y_{m-1},\mathcal{f}_{n}(X_{n})),

so as
\mathcal{f}_{n}
is elementary, we have

\mathfrak{A}_{n}\models\exists y_{0}\dots\exists y_{k-1}\psi(a_{0},\dots,a_{k-1},y_{0},\dots,y_{m-1},X_{n}),

so we can find
a_{k},\dots,a_{m+k-1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}
such that
\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}
is satisfied in an expansion of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
that interprets
\underline{\{b_{i}\}}
as
\{a_{k+i}\}
,
\underline{\mathcal{f}(\{a_{i}\})}
as
\{a_{i}\}
and
\underline{Y_{n}}
as
X_{n}
. Such an expansion clearly also satisfies the sentences “
\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(R)}=\underline{R}
”. Then by compactness,
\Gamma
has a model, so we let
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}
be one such. As
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{A}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{n})
, we can find a partial elementary team map
\mathcal{i}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{n}\to\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}
with
\dom(\mathcal{i}_{n})=\cl(\mathcal{X}_{n})
and
\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}}=\mathcal{i}_{n}(R)
for all
R\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
. As
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}\models\Diag(\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})
, we can find a partial elementary team map
\mathcal{g}_{n}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{n}\to\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}
with
\dom(\mathcal{g}_{n})=\cl(\mathcal{Y}_{n})
and
\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}}=\mathcal{g}_{n}(R)
for all
R\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}
. As
\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}\models\text{``$\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(R)}=\underline{R}$''}
for all
R\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
, we have

\mathcal{g}_{n}(\mathcal{f}_{n}(R))=\underline{\mathcal{f}_{n}(R)}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}}=\underline{R}^{\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}}=\mathcal{i}_{n}(R)

for all
R\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
, and hence
\mathcal{i}_{n}=\mathcal{g}_{n}\circ\mathcal{f}_{n}
. Then we can let
\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}=\hat{\mathfrak{A}}_{n+1}{\upharpoonright}\tau
and
\mathcal{X}_{n+1}=\{X_{n}\}\cup\{\{a\}\mid a\in\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}\}
as desired.

The model
\mathfrak{B}_{n+1}
, the set
\mathcal{Y}_{n+1}
and the maps
\mathcal{j}_{n}
and
\mathcal{f}_{n+1}
are constructed in a similar manner, using
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{A}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{X}_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{g}_{n}
.

In the end, we let
\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}=\lim_{i<\omega}\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
X_{\omega}=\mathcal{i}_{\omega}(X_{0})
, where
\mathcal{i}_{\omega}
is the direct limit map
\mathfrak{A}_{0}\to\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}
of the system
(\mathfrak{A}_{i})_{i<\omega}
, and
\mathfrak{B}_{\omega}=\lim_{i<\omega}\mathfrak{B}_{i}
and
Y_{\omega}=\mathcal{j}_{\omega}(Y_{0})
, where
\mathcal{j}_{\omega}
is the direct limit map
\mathfrak{B}_{0}\to\mathfrak{B}_{\omega}
of the system
(\mathfrak{B}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. Now, since
L
has direct limits, we have
\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}\models_{X_{\omega}}\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\omega}\models_{Y_{\omega}}\phi
. But as both
(\mathfrak{A}_{i})_{i<\omega}
and
(\mathfrak{B}_{i})_{i<\omega}
are cofinal in the directed system, we must have
\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}\cong\mathfrak{B}_{\omega}
and furthermore, the isomorphism maps
X_{\omega}
to
Y_{\omega}
, which is impossible.

(ii)

Suppose this is not the case. Then there is an abstract positive team-finitary team logic
L
which has a strong Łoś’ theorem with the following property: there exists a formula
\phi\in L
such that for all
\psi\in\mathsf{FOT}
either

(a)

there is a model
\mathfrak{A}_{\psi}
and a team
X_{\psi}
such that
\mathfrak{A}_{\psi}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X_{\psi}}\phi
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\psi}\models_{X_{\psi}}\psi
, or

(b)

there is a model
\mathfrak{B}_{\psi}
and a team
Y_{\psi}
such that
\mathfrak{B}_{\psi}\models_{Y_{\psi}}\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\psi}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{Y_{\psi}}\psi
.

We also remark that since
L
has a strong Łoś’ theorem then it is also compact, as the usual ultraproduct proof works in this setting (see
[
24
, Theorem 3.14]
).

Let
D
be a set of variables satisfying the team-finitarity condition for
\phi
and
\tau
a signature satisfying the occurrence condition for
\phi
. Consider the set
\Gamma
of all
\tau
-formulas
\psi\in\mathsf{FOT}
such that
\phi\models\psi
and
\Fv(\psi)\subseteq D
. Also, we let
\mathcal{S}
be the family of finite subsets of
\Gamma
. Since
\phi\models\Gamma
, it follows by the choice of
\phi
that for every
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}
there are a model
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}
and a team
X_{\Sigma}
on
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}
such that
\dom(X_{\Sigma})=D
,
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}\models_{X_{\Sigma}}\Sigma
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X_{\Sigma}}\phi
. For every
\psi\in\Gamma
, we let
[\psi]=\{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}\mid\psi\in\Sigma\}
and
F=\{[\psi]\mid\psi\in\Gamma\}
. Then, given
\psi_{0},\dots,\psi_{n-1}\in\Gamma
we have that
\{\psi_{i}\mid i<n\}\in\bigcap_{i<n}[\psi_{i}]
, showing that
F
has the finite intersection property. It follows that we can extend
F
to an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Consider now the ultraproduct
\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}
. For every formula
\psi\in\Gamma
we have that
[\psi]\in\mathcal{U}
and for every
\Sigma^{\prime}\supseteq\{\psi\}
we have that
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\models_{X_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}\psi
. It follows by the Los’ Theorem of
\mathsf{FOT}
that
\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}}\psi
, and so that
\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}}\Gamma
. Moreover, for any
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}
we have that
\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{X_{\Sigma}}\phi
, thus by the strong Łoś’ theorem of
L
we obtain that
\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}}\phi
.

Now consider the theory
T=\Th_{\mathsf{FOT}}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U},\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})
, we claim this is consistent with
\phi
. If not, there is a finite sets of formulas
\Delta\subseteq\Th_{\mathsf{FOT}}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U},\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})
such that
\Delta\cup\{\phi\}
is not consistent, whence
\phi\models\bigvee\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\delta\mid\delta\in\Delta\}
. It follows that
\bigvee\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\delta\mid\delta\in\Delta\}\in\Gamma
and so

\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}\models_{\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}}\bigvee\{\mathord{\dot{\sim}}\delta\mid\delta\in\Delta\}

which contradicts
\Delta\subseteq T
.

We conclude that there are a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}\equiv_{\mathsf{FOT}}\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}
and a team
Y
on
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\Th_{\mathsf{FOT}}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U},\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})=\Th_{\mathsf{FOT}}(\mathfrak{B},Y)
and
\mathfrak{B}\models_{Y}\phi
. Notice that, by the choice of
D
and the fact that
D=\dom(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})
, we have that both these teams are finitary. Then, by the translation from
\mathsf{FOT}
to
\mathsf{FO}
it follows that

(\mathfrak{B},Y)\equiv_{\mathsf{FO}}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U},\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})

and so, by Keisler–Shelah, there are two elementary maps
\pi_{0}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\pi_{1}\colon\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{A}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathfrak{C}
into some common ultrapower
\mathfrak{C}
(up to isomorphism). By assumption,
L
has a strong Łoś’ theorem, and so does
\mathsf{FOT}
. Thus it follows that
\mathfrak{C}\models_{\pi_{0}(Y)}\phi
and
\mathfrak{C}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}_{\pi_{1}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})}\phi
, which contradicts
\pi_{0}(Y)=\pi_{1}(\prod_{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}}X_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{U})
. ∎

7.
Application II – Complete Theories in
\mathsf{ESO}

In this last section, we apply the techniques and the methodology developed so far to the context of complete theories in existential second-order logic (or equivalently of
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
). In
Section

7.1
, we study some basic properties of complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theories and relate them to so-called resplendent models. In
Section

7.2
, we use stability theory to prove some versions of categoricity transfer for complete theories in
\mathsf{ESO}
.

7.1.
Complete Theories & Resplendent Models

In this section, we study complete theories in
\mathsf{ESO}
and their models. As we are always dealing with sentences, in all results one may replace first-order logic by
\mathsf{FOT}
and
\mathsf{ESO}
by
\mathsf{FO}(\perp_{c})
.

Recall that a theory
T
of
\mathsf{ESO}
is complete if for all
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\models T
and sentences
\phi
of
\mathsf{ESO}
,

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
T
be a complete first-order
\tau
-theory. Then there is a unique
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory
\bar{T}
such that

(i)

\bar{T}\supseteq T
,

(ii)

\bar{T}
is closed under logical consequence and

(iii)

\bar{T}
is complete.

Proof.

Let
\kappa=|\tau|+\aleph_{0}
, and let
\phi_{i}
,
i<\kappa
, enumerate all
\tau
-sentences of
\mathsf{ESO}
. We define
\bar{T}
by recursion. Let
T_{0}=T
and
T_{i}=\bigcup_{j<i}T_{j}
for
i
limit. For
i=j+1
, we let
T_{i}=T_{j}\cup\{\phi_{j}\}
if it is consistent and
T_{i}=T_{j}
otherwise. Then we let
\bar{T}=\bigcup_{i<\kappa}T_{i}
. By compactness of
\mathsf{ESO}
,
\bar{T}
is consistent, and as such it is clearly a maximal consistent extension of
T
and hence closed under logical consequence. Now, let
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
be models of
\bar{T}
and
\phi
a sentence of
\mathsf{ESO}
. Now if
\phi\in\bar{T}
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
, as both are models of
\bar{T}
. On the other hand, if
\phi\notin\bar{T}
, then
\bar{T}\cup\{\phi\}
is inconsistent, whence
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\phi
. Hence
\bar{T}
is complete.

For uniqueness, let
T^{\prime}
be another complete theory in
\mathsf{ESO}
extending
T
. If
T^{\prime}\cup\bar{T}
is consistent, then
T^{\prime}\subseteq\bar{T}
, as
\bar{T}
is a maximal consistent theory, whence the closure of
T^{\prime}
under logical consequence is exactly
\bar{T}
. Thus it suffices to show that
T^{\prime}\cup\bar{T}
is consistent. Let
\mathfrak{A}\models T^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{B}\models\bar{T}
. As
T
is first-order complete, it has the joint embedding property. Hence there is
\mathfrak{C}
and elementary team embeddings
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
. Since elementary team embeddings are independence team embeddings,
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
implies
\mathfrak{C}\models\phi
and
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
implies
\mathfrak{C}\models\phi
for all
\phi\in\mathsf{ESO}
. Hence
\mathfrak{C}\models T^{\prime}\cup\bar{T}
.
∎

Definition 7.2
.

We say that a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
is
complete
if for any other
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{B}
such that there is an elementary team embedding
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, satisfies

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi

for all sentences
\phi\in\mathsf{ESO}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For a first-order complete theory
T
of
\mathsf{ESO}
and a structure
\mathfrak{A}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathfrak{A}\models\bar{T}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{A}
is a complete model of
T
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mathfrak{A}\models\bar{T}
. Let
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
be an elementary team embedding. Then, in particular, for all sentences
\phi\in\mathsf{ESO}
we have that
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
entails
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
. It follows that
\mathfrak{B}\models\bar{T}
. But then, as
\bar{T}
is complete, we have

\mathfrak{A}\models\phi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\phi

for all sentences
\phi\in\mathsf{ESO}
. Hence
\mathfrak{A}
is complete.

Then, suppose that
\mathfrak{A}
is a complete model of
T
. Let
\mathfrak{B}\models\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
. By joint embedding, there is
\mathfrak{C}
and elementary team embeddings
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{C}
. Let
\phi
be a sentence of
\mathsf{ESO}
. Now, if
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi
, then
\phi\in\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
, whence
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
. On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\phi
, since
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, by completeness of
\mathfrak{A}
we have
\mathfrak{C}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\phi
. If it were the case that
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
, since
\mathcal{g}
is an independence team embedding, we would have
\mathfrak{C}\models\phi
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathfrak{B}\mathbin{\cancel{\models}}\phi
. Therefore
\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
is complete, whence
\bar{T}=\Th_{\mathsf{ESO}}(\mathfrak{A})
.
∎

We conclude this section by highlighting a connection between complete structures and so-called resplendent models. The following can be found in e.g.
[
23
, Ch. 9.3]
.

Definition 7.4
.

A
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
is
resplendent
if
for any elementary extension
\mathfrak{B}
of
\mathfrak{A}
and a
\tau
-formula
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})
of
\mathsf{ESO}
, we have

\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})

for all
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{A}
.

For the proof of the following fact, we refer the reader again to
[
23
, Ch. 9.3]
.

Fact
.

Let
T
be a first-order theory. Then every model of
T
with cardinality
\geq|T|
has a resplendent elementary extension of the same cardinality.

Proposition 7.5
.

The following are equivalent for a structure
\mathfrak{A}
.

(i)

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is complete.

(ii)

\mathfrak{A}
is resplendent.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mathfrak{A}
is resplendent. To show that
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is complete, let
\mathfrak{B}
be a
\tau(\mathfrak{A})
-structure and
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{B}
an elementary team embedding, and let
\phi
be a
\tau(\mathfrak{A})
-sentence of
\mathsf{ESO}
such that
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
. By
Proposition

3.15
, we may assume that
\mathfrak{B}
is an elementary extension of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\mathcal{f}
is the identity on singletons. Now
\phi=\psi(\underline{a}_{0},\dots,\underline{a}_{n-1})
for some
\tau
-formula
\psi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\in\mathsf{ESO}
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{A}
, so as
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi
, we have
\mathfrak{B}{\upharpoonright}\tau\models\psi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
. Now
\mathfrak{B}{\upharpoonright}\tau
is an elementary extension of
\mathfrak{A}
, so by the resplendence of
\mathfrak{A}
, we obtain
\mathfrak{A}\models\psi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
, whence
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}\models\phi
. Hence
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is complete.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is complete. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be an elementary extension of
\mathfrak{A}
and let
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1})\in\mathsf{ESO}
be such that
\mathfrak{B}\models\phi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
for some
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{A}
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is an elementary extension of
\mathfrak{A}
, we have
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}\equiv\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, so by the joint embedding property there is a
\tau(\mathfrak{A})
-structure
\mathfrak{C}
and elementary team embeddings
\mathcal{f}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathcal{g}\colon\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathfrak{C}
. Now
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{A}}\models\phi(\underline{a}_{0},\dots,\underline{a}_{n-1})
, so as
\mathcal{g}
is an independence team embedding, we have
\mathfrak{C}\models\phi(\underline{a}_{0},\dots,\underline{a}_{n-1})
. As
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is complete and
\mathcal{f}
is an elementary team embedding, we have
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{A}}\models\phi(\underline{a}_{0},\dots,\underline{a}_{n-1})
. But then
\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})
. Thus
\mathfrak{A}
is resplendent.
∎

7.2.
Categoricity Transfer

In this section we prove two results concerning the transfer of categoricity between countable and uncountable cardinals for complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theories. We assume the reader is familiar with the standard notions from classification theory and refer to
[
30
,
2
,
28
]
for the basic definitions.

We also remark that the following results provide a first study of the spectrum function for existential second order theories. In connection to this topic, we remark the following fact.

Fact
.

Let
T
be a complete theory in
\mathsf{ESO}
and
T^{*}
its first-order reduct. If
T^{*}
is unstable, then
T
has
2^{\kappa}
models in any cardinal
\kappa>|T|
.

If
T
is a complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory, the class of its models is clearly pseudoelementary, as it is the class of models of the theory
T^{1}
obtained by adding to the signature witnesses for every existential second-order statement in
T
. It follows that the fact above is an immediate corollary of Shelah’s general result on the number of models of pseudoelementary classes
[
28
, Ch. VIII, Thm.2.1]
.

7.2.1.
Downwards Categoricity

We show in this section that if the first-order reduct of an
\mathsf{ESO}
theory
T
in a finite signature is uncountably categorical, then the theory
T
is also
\aleph_{0}
-categorical. It follows that
T
is quasi-categorical, i.e. it has a unique model in all cardinals for which it has a model to begin with.

The proof of this fact is essentially a corollary of Baldwin and Lachlan’s study of uncountably categorical theories (see e.g.
[
30
, Section 6.3]
). In particular, recall that if
T
is an uncountably categorical first-order theory, then

(i)

T
is totally transcendental and thus has a (countable) prime model
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
,

(ii)

there are a formula
\phi(x,\vec{y})
and
\vec{a}\in\mathfrak{A}_{0}
such that
\phi(x,\vec{b})
is strongly minimal for every
\vec{b}\models\foltype(\vec{a}/\emptyset)
, and

(iii)

since
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
is atomic, the type
\foltype(\vec{a}/\emptyset)
is isolated by some
\psi(\vec{y})
.

Then
\phi(x,\vec{a})
determines a pregeometry whose dimension does not depend on the choice of
\vec{a}
as long as it realizes
\psi
. For
\mathfrak{A}\models T
, we shall write
\dim_{\phi,\psi}(\mathfrak{A})
for the dimension of the pregeometry
(\phi(\mathfrak{A},a),\acl)
. We denote the dimension of
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
by
m_{0}
. The following well-known theorem due to Baldwin and Lachlan
[
30
, Theorem 6.3.7]
classifies all countable models of uncountably categorical theories.

Theorem 7.6

(Baldwin–Lachlan)
.

Let
T
be an uncountably categorical first-order theory in a countable signature. Then for each cardinal
\kappa\geq m_{0}
there is a unique model
\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa}
with
\dim_{\phi,\psi}(\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa})=\kappa
.

In particular, since
m_{0}\leq\aleph_{0}
, this means that either
T
is countably categorical, or it has
\aleph_{0}
-many countable models up to isomorphism. We shall see now how its
\mathsf{ESO}
-completions is satisfied only by the countable model of dimension
\aleph_{0}
.

Theorem 7.7
.

If
T
is a complete theory in
\mathsf{ESO}
in a finite signature and its first-order reduct
T^{*}
is uncountably categorical, then
T
is categorical in all infinite cardinalities.

Proof.

First, notice that since the signature
\tau
of
T
is finite,
\mathsf{ESO}
can talk about automorphisms of a
\tau
-structure. In particular, for any second-order function variable
f
, there is a
\tau
-formula
\aut(f)
such that

\mathfrak{A}\models\aut(\pi)\iff\text{$\pi$ is a $\tau$-automorphism of $\mathfrak{A}$}.

Also,
\mathsf{ESO}
can express when a set is infinite, namely there is a formula
\inf(X)
in
\mathsf{ESO}
such that:

\mathfrak{A}\models\inf(A)\iff|A|\geq\aleph_{0}.

Let
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
be a prime model of
T^{*}
, and let
\phi(x,a^{*})
,
a^{*}\in\mathfrak{A}_{0}
, be strongly minimal. Let
\theta(v)
isolate
\foltype(a^{*}/\emptyset)
. Now, for all
n<\omega
, let
\Phi_{n}
be the following sentence of
\mathsf{SO}
:

\displaystyle\forall v\big(\theta(v)\to\forall x_{0}\dots\forall x_{n-1}\exists X\big(\inf(X)\land\forall y(X(y)\to\phi(y,v))\land\psi\big)\big),

where
\psi(v,x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1},X)
is the formula

\forall y\forall z\bigg(\big(X(y)\land X(z)\big)\to\exists f\bigg(\aut(f)\land f(y)=z\land f(v)=v\land\bigwedge_{i<n}f(x_{i})=x_{i}\bigg)\bigg).

In other words,
\Phi_{n}
expresses that whenever
a
is such that
\varphi(x,a)
is strongly minimal, then for any
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
there are infinitely many elements of the strongly minimal set such that any two of them are conjugates over
a,a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
. One can easily check that
\Phi_{n}\in\mathsf{ESO}
.

First of all, we show that
T\models\Phi_{n}
for all
n<\omega
. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be an uncountable elementary extension of
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
. As
T
has a model in every infinite cardinality but
T^{*}
is uncountably categorical, we must have
\mathfrak{B}\models T
. Now it is enough to show that
\mathfrak{B}\models\Phi_{n}
for all
n
. For this, fix
n
, let
b^{*}\in\mathfrak{B}
be such that
\mathfrak{B}\models\theta(b^{*})
and let
b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{B}
be arbitrary. Now
\varphi(x,b^{*})
is strongly minimal, so it has a unique non-forking extension
p\in S(\{b^{*},b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1}\})
. In particular,
p
is not algebraic. Hence, as
\mathfrak{B}
is
\aleph_{0}
-saturated,
p
is realized in
\mathfrak{B}
, and as
p
is not algebraic, there are actually infinitely many
b\in\mathfrak{B}
with
b\models p
. Let
B
be the set of them. Now
B\subseteq\phi(\mathfrak{B},b^{*})
and any two elements of
B
are conjugates over
\{b^{*},b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1}\}
. Hence
\mathfrak{B}\models\Phi_{n}
.

In order to show that
T
is
\aleph_{0}
-categorical, it is enough to show that any countable model
\mathfrak{A}
of
T
has dimension
\aleph_{0}
. Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a countable model of
T
. We now construct by recursion an independent sequence
a_{i}
,
i<\omega
, in the pregeometry
(\phi(\mathfrak{A},a^{*}),\acl)
. Suppose that we have already found independent
a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
. As
T\models\Phi_{n}
, we have
\mathfrak{A}\models\Phi_{n}
. Hence there is an infinite set
A\subseteq\phi(\mathfrak{A},a^{*})
such that any two elements of
A
are conjugates over
\{a^{*},a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\}
. Now all elements of
A
have the same type over
\{a^{*},a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\}
, and since
A
is infinite, it must be that
A\subseteq\phi(\mathfrak{A},a^{*})\setminus\acl(\{a^{*},a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\})
. Now any element of
A
is algebraically independent of
a^{*},a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}
and hence suffices as
a_{n}
.
∎

Example 7.8
.

The motivating example for the result above is given by the theories in algebraically closed fields. In particular, let
T_{\mathrm{ACF}_{0}}
be the theory of algebraically closed fields of characteristic 0 and let
T^{1}_{\mathrm{ACF}_{0}}
be its (unique)
\mathsf{ESO}
-completion. Then the previous theorem shows that
T^{1}_{\mathrm{ACF}_{0}}
is
\aleph_{0}
-categorical and its only countable model is the algebraic closure of the field
\mathbb{Q}(t_{0},t_{1},\dots)
of polynomial fractions over the rational numbers.

7.2.2.
Upwards Categoricity

We prove in this section an upwards categoricity result for complete theories in existential second-order logic. In particular, we show that under the assumptions of
\omega
-stability and 1-basedness,
\aleph_{0}
-categoricity entails uncountable categoricity.

We follow in this section the standard notation and terminology from classification theory. In particular, we use the symbol
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
to refer to forking independence and we identify the strong type
\stp(\vec{a}/C)
of a tuple
\vec{a}
over parameters
C
with the set of all equivalence classes
E(\vec{x},\vec{a})
, where
E
is any finite equivalence relation definable over
C
. The set of finite equivalence relations over
C
we denote by
\FE(C)
. We refer the reader to
[
2
]
for these and the related notions from classification theory.

As the notion of
1
-based theory is less standard, we recall its definition and one of its key properties. We stress this is a folklore result and refer the reader to
[
5
, Fact 3.1]
for a proof. The converse of the lemma is also true, but we shall not need it.

In this subsection,
\mathfrak{M}
denotes the ordinary elementary monster model which is suitably universal, homogeneous and saturated.

Definition 7.9
.

A first-order theory
T
is 1-based if for all
A,B\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{eq}}
, we have
A\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt_{\acl^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\cap\acl^{\mathrm{eq}}(B)}B
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose
T
is 1-based and
C
is finite. If
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
is
C
-indiscernible, then for all
0<i<j
we have
a_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{Ca_{0}}(a_{j})_{i\neq j\in I}
.

Many steps in the proof of the following theorem are well-known results among stability theorists but we include them for completeness.

Theorem 7.11
.

Let
T
be a complete
\mathsf{ESO}
-theory and let
T^{*}
be its first-order reduct. If
T^{*}
is
\omega
-stable,
\aleph_{0}
-categorical and 1-based, then
T
is uncountably categorical.

Proof.

Let
\lambda
be an uncountable cardinal. We show that all models of
T
of cardinality
\lambda
are saturated, from which
\lambda
-categoricity follows. Let
\mathfrak{A}
be such a model, and let
p
be a type over some parameter set of cardinality
<\lambda
. The general strategy of the proof is to show that
p
is realised in
\mathfrak{A}
. Without loss of generality,
p\in S_{1}(\mathfrak{B})
for some elementary submodel
\mathfrak{B}\preceq\mathfrak{A}
of power
<\lambda
. We denote
\kappa=|\mathfrak{B}|
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\succeq\mathfrak{A}
be the (first-order) monster model of
T^{*}
. Since
T^{*}
is
\omega
-stable, we may have
\mathfrak{M}
be saturated. Note that a saturated structure is resplendent: as it has a resplendent elementary extension of the same cardinality and is itself saturated, it must be isomorphic to the extension. It thus follows from
Proposition

7.3
and
Proposition

7.5
that
\mathfrak{M}\models T
.

Let
a\in\mathfrak{M}
realize
p
, by superstability of
T^{*}
, there is a finite
B\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}\mathfrak{B}
. Since
T^{*}
is
\aleph_{0}
-categorical, by Ryll-Nardzewski, there are only finitely many non-equivalent formulas with free variables
x,y
and parameters from
B
. In particular, there is a finite list of formulas
\delta_{i}(x,y,\vec{d}_{i})
,
i<n
, defining all finite equivalence relations over
B
. We let
E_{i}\in\FE(B)
be the relation defined by
\delta_{i}
. Now, for each
i<n
, pick
c_{i}\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
(a,c_{i})\in E_{i}
. The type
\foltype(a/B\cup\{c_{i}\mid i<n\})
has a finite number of parameters, so by
\aleph_{0}
-categoricity it is isolated by some formula and thus realised in
\mathfrak{B}
by some element
b
. Then we clearly have, for all
i<n
,

\displaystyle(a,c_{i})\in E_{i}

\displaystyle\iff\delta_{i}(x,c_{i},\vec{d}_{i})\in\foltype(a/B\cup\{c_{i}\mid i<n\})

\displaystyle\iff\delta_{i}(x,c_{i},\vec{d}_{i})\in\foltype(b/B\cup\{c_{i}\mid i<n\})

\displaystyle\iff(b,c_{i})\in E_{i},

and so we obtain that
\stp(a/B)=\stp(b/B)
.

Let
C=B\cup\{b\}
. We claim that
\foltype(a/C)
(
=p{\upharpoonright}C
) is stationary, i.e. has a unique non-forking extension to
\mathfrak{B}
. To this end, suppose that
c,d\models\foltype(a/C)
,
c\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}\mathfrak{B}
and
d\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}\mathfrak{B}
. Since
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}\mathfrak{B}
, by monotonicity
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}C
. As
\foltype(c/C)=\foltype(a/C)
, we then obtain
c\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}C
. But then from
c\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}C
and
c\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}\mathfrak{B}
, transitivity gives
c\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}\mathfrak{B}
. Similarly
d\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{B}\mathfrak{B}
.
Now, for every
E\in\FE(B)
, if
\delta(x,y,\vec{d})
is the defining formula of
E
, then since
(a,b)\in E
, we have
\delta(x,b,\vec{d})\in\foltype(a/C)
and so
\delta(x,b,\vec{d})\in\foltype(c/C),\foltype(d/C)
. Hence
(c,b),(d,b)\in E
. It follows that
(c,d)\in E
. Hence
\stp(c/C)=\stp(d/C)
. Thus, as
c
and
d
have the same strong type over
C
and both of them are independent of
\mathfrak{B}
over
C
, by stationarity of strong types this means that
\foltype(c/\mathfrak{B})=\foltype(d/\mathfrak{B})
. Hence
\foltype(a/C)
is stationary.
In particular, we have so far found an element
a
and a finite
C\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}\mathfrak{B}
,
\foltype(a/C)
is stationary and
a\models p
.

Now, since
\mathfrak{M}
is saturated, we can find a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<|\mathfrak{M}|}
such that
b_{i}\models p{\upharpoonright}C
for all
i<|\mathfrak{M}|
and
b_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}b_{j}
for all
i,j<|\mathfrak{M}|
. We claim that the existence of such sequence can be expressed via a sentence of
\mathsf{ESO}
. First we define the the following formulas:

(i)

We write
\max(X)
for the formula saying that there is a bijection between the background structure and
X
. This is clearly expressible in
\mathsf{ESO}
.

(ii)

We let
\psi(x,\vec{y})
be a formula isolating
\foltype(aC/\emptyset)
and
\chi(\vec{y})
be a formula isolating
\foltype(C/\emptyset)
. The existence of such formulas follows from
\aleph_{0}
-categoricity.

(iii)

We let
\theta(x,y,\vec{z})
be a first-order formula such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\theta(d_{0},d_{1},\vec{e})
if and only if
d_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{\vec{e}}d_{1}
. The reason why such a formula exists is the following. First of all, if
d_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{\vec{e}}d_{1}
and
\pi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
, then
\pi(d_{0})\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{\pi(\vec{e})}\pi(d_{1})
. Hence the property of
d_{0}
being independent of
d_{1}
over
\vec{e}
only depends on the type
\foltype(d_{0}d_{1}\vec{e}/\emptyset)
. By Ryll-Nardzewski, there are only finitely many
|\vec{e}|+2
-types over
\emptyset
, so in particular there are only finitely many
q
